"Whole substance" shuts the Platonist exit, God as craftsman of uncreated matter, against which Theophilus of Antioch argued in the late second century (*To Autolycus* II.4): the stuff is made, not only its shape. "Out of" names no material: "it is not made from anything" (ST I q.45 a.1 ad 3). Augustine: God's works "were made from nothing by Thee, not of Thee, nor of any matter not Thine" (*Confessions* XIII.33.48, Pilkington). Scripture: the doctrine rests on the whole canon read in the Church (John 1:3; Rom 4:17; Heb 11:3); Gen 1:1-2 does not itself say "out of nothing", Wis 11:17 [DR 11:18] has the world made "of matter without form", and whether 2 Macc 7:28 means absolute nothing is disputed ([`old-testament` 6.2](../old-testament/lessons/06-02-tobit-judith-baruch-and-the-maccabees.md)). On Gerhard May's account (1978), the technical formula took shape among second-century Christian writers. The metaphysics (creation is not a change; conservation is the same act still given) is [`thomistic-synthesis` 5.1](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/05-01-creation-and-conservation.md)'s.

**Level:** **rung 1** (*Firmiter*; *Dei Filius* can. 5). Creation as a relation of dependence, not a change (ST I q.45 a.2 ad 2, a.3): theology, **no magisterial level**. In 1.4 it is the rung-1 presupposition of every act on evolution.

**Keep apart:** "out of nothing" is not "after nothing": eternalists could say the world was made "from nothing, not ... after nothing" (ST I q.46 a.2 ad 2), so the [beginning in time](#beginning-in-time) needs its own warrant. Pilkington's "of almost nothing" in *Confessions* XIII.33.48 mistranslates *de omnino nihilo*, "from altogether nothing".

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-creation-out-of-nothing.md) (owner), [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-angels.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-creation-and-evolution-in-church-teaching.md)

### Firmiter

Lateran IV's profession of faith (1215, const. 1): the course's most-cited act, for creation, the demons, the married, the resurrection and the final separation.

The first constitution of the Fourth Lateran Council (November 1215). Const. 3 excommunicates and anathematizes every heresy against the faith set out in const. 1, which is why the profession counts as a definition. It names no group: "against the Albigensians" is a heading Denzinger's editors added ([Cathars](#cathars)). Its clauses as the lessons read them (lesson's translations of the Latin in Denzinger):

| Clause | What it fixes | Lessons |
|---|---|---|
| *unum universorum principium*, "creator of all things visible and invisible, spiritual and corporeal" (visible first) | one source of everything; no second principle ([dualism](#dualism)) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-creation-out-of-nothing.md), [1.2](lessons/01-02-and-god-saw-that-it-was-good.md) |
| *simul ab initio temporis utramque de nihilo condidit creaturam, spiritualem et corporalem* | both orders out of nothing; the world not eternal ([beginning in time](#beginning-in-time)); *simul* left undefined | [1.1](lessons/01-01-creation-out-of-nothing.md), [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-angels.md) |
| *angelicam videlicet et mundanam: ac deinde humanam, quasi communem ex spiritu et corpore constitutam* | the angels are the spiritual creature; the human is "constituted of spirit and body" | [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-angels.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-body-and-soul-one-substance.md) |
| *Diabolus enim et alii daemones a Deo quidem natura creati sunt boni, sed ipsi per se facti sunt mali* | the demons good by nature, evil through themselves ([fall of the angels](#fall-of-the-angels)) | [1.2](lessons/01-02-and-god-saw-that-it-was-good.md), [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-angels.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md) |
| *aeternus, immensus et incommutabilis, incomprehensibilis, omnipotens et ineffabilis* | God's [incomprehensibility](#incomprehensibility) (and the attributes [`trinity-and-god` 1.2](../trinity-and-god/lessons/01-02-the-one-true-god-and-what-reason-can-reach.md) levels) | [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md) |
| *qui omnes cum suis propriis resurgent corporibus, quae nunc gestant* | all, "the reprobate and the elect alike", "will rise with their own bodies which they now bear" ([resurrection of the body](#resurrection-of-the-body)) | [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-resurrection-of-the-body.md) |
| *illi cum diabolo poenam perpetuam, et isti cum Christo gloriam sempiternam* | "the former perpetual punishment with the devil, the latter everlasting glory with Christ", said of the final state after the resurrection ([hell](#hell)) | [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md), [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-parousia-the-last-judgment-and-the-new-creation.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md) |
| *verum etiam coniugati, per rectam fidem et operationem bonam placentes Deo, ad aeternam merentur beatitudinem pervenire* | not only virgins but the married too, pleasing God by right faith and good work, merit to reach eternal beatitude | [1.2](lessons/01-02-and-god-saw-that-it-was-good.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md) |

**Level:** **rung 1** throughout: an ecumenical council's solemn profession, guarded by const. 3's anathema. *Dei Filius* ch. 1 repeats the creation clause word for word. What it leaves undefined: *simul*'s temporal sense (the "at once" reading is common teaching, **rung 4**). 7.3: it names the reprobate as a class without manifestly defining that the class has human members (can. 749 §3), so "that some human beings are damned" is taught by no act. 6.4: "perpetual punishment with the devil" is a punishment undergone, which is what excludes [annihilationism](#annihilationism). 2.1 uses its demons clause in a problem on the moment of the fall.

**Traps:** naming Vatican I as the act that fixes the beginning in time (it is *Firmiter*); quoting the creation clause as "invisible and visible" (the text has visible first); making *Firmiter* stand or fall with the historians' Cathars.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-creation-out-of-nothing.md), [1.2](lessons/01-02-and-god-saw-that-it-was-good.md), [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-angels.md), [2.1](lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-body-and-soul-one-substance.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-resurrection-of-the-body.md), [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-parousia-the-last-judgment-and-the-new-creation.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md)

### Cathars

The dualist "good men" of Languedoc and northern Italy, as their opponents described them, and the target usually read into *Firmiter*.

*Firmiter* names no one; its anti-Cathar framing comes from the council's context and from editors' headings (the 1911 Denzinger heads the council *contra Albigenses*). The dualism condemned in 1215 was fought with a crusade and inquisitors, and historians dispute whether the organized dualist church its prosecutors described existed (Mark Pegg and R. I. Moore against Bernard Hamilton): [`church-history` 5.3](../church-history/lessons/05-03-heresy-inquisition-and-the-jews.md)'s question.

**Level:** the historians' question carries **no magisterial level** and is no [dogmatic fact](#dogmatic-fact), since no act proposes it; its answer cannot move *Firmiter*'s **rung 1**. The level is the act's, not the target's.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-creation-out-of-nothing.md), [1.2](lessons/01-02-and-god-saw-that-it-was-good.md) (owner)

### Dei Filius

Vatican I's dogmatic constitution on the Catholic faith (Session III, 24 April 1870): four chapters, each with canons; the course reads chapter 1 and the canons "On God the Creator of all things", and uses chapters 2 and 4.

English: Schaff, *Creeds of Christendom* vol. 2, which prints Archbishop Manning's translation (*Petri Privilegium*, 1871). Where its canons stand behind a chapter it defines; for a chapter clause no canon repeats, see [chapters and canons](#chapters-and-canons).

| Text | What it says (Schaff, Manning's translation) | Level as the lessons mark it | Lessons |
|---|---|---|---|
| ch. 1 | God, "not for the increase or acquirement of his own happiness, but to manifest his perfection by the blessings which he bestows on creatures", and "with absolute freedom of counsel", created out of nothing "from the very first beginning of time" both creatures, "the angelical and the mundane"; no English word for *simul* | **at least rung 3** as chapter text; **rung 1** where another act stands behind it (*Firmiter*) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-creation-out-of-nothing.md), [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-angels.md) |
| ch. 1 | "God protects and governs by his providence all things which he hath made" | **rung 1** (universal providence) | [1.4](lessons/01-04-creation-and-evolution-in-church-teaching.md) |
| ch. 1 | God "incomprehensible" | **rung 1** as confessed (with *Firmiter*) | [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md) |
| can. 1 | anathema on denying "one true God, Creator and Lord of things visible and invisible" | **rung 1** | [1.1](lessons/01-01-creation-out-of-nothing.md) |
| cans. 2-4 | against materialism, one substance of God and things, and emanation (read in [`trinity-and-god` 1.2](../trinity-and-god/lessons/01-02-the-one-true-god-and-what-reason-can-reach.md)) | **rung 1** | [1.1](lessons/01-01-creation-out-of-nothing.md) |
| can. 3 | condemns saying "the substance and essence of God and of all things is one and the same" | **rung 1**: the guardrail of [divinization](#divinization) | [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md) |
| can. 4 | condemns finite things "emanated from the divine substance", and "the divine essence by the manifestation and evolution of itself becomes all things" | **rung 1**; about pantheism, not species ([emanation](#emanation)) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-creation-out-of-nothing.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-creation-and-evolution-in-church-teaching.md) |
| can. 5 | all things, "both spiritual and material", "in their whole substance, produced by God out of nothing"; God created "by his will, free from all necessity"; the world "made for the glory of God" | **rung 1**; it has **no temporal clause** | [1.1](lessons/01-01-creation-out-of-nothing.md), [1.2](lessons/01-02-and-god-saw-that-it-was-good.md) |
| ch. 2 | God "of his infinite goodness has ordained man to a supernatural end" | **at least rung 3** | [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md) |
| can. II.3 | condemns saying man cannot be raised by God above natural knowledge and perfection, but can and ought of himself, by continuous progress, to reach all truth and good | **rung 1**: no creature reaches the vision of itself | [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md) |
| ch. 4 | repeats Lateran V: "We define, therefore, that every assertion contrary to a truth of enlightened faith is utterly false" | **rung 1** ([double truth](#double-truth)) | [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md) |

**Traps:** "Vatican I defined the beginning of time" (no canon contains it: the act is *Firmiter*); "Vatican I defined the goodness of creation" (it never calls creatures good: *Firmiter* and Florence do); "*Dei Filius* condemned evolution" (can. 4's evolution is a self-evolving God). CCC 293's English renders *nec ad acquirendam* "nor for attaining his perfection"; Manning reads the implied noun as beatitude. Either construal is grammatical.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-creation-out-of-nothing.md), [1.2](lessons/01-02-and-god-saw-that-it-was-good.md), [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-angels.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-creation-and-evolution-in-church-teaching.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md)

### Freedom of creation

God created by choice, under no necessity: he loves himself necessarily, but did not have to make the world.

*Dei Filius* ch. 1: God created *liberrimo consilio*, "with absolute freedom of counsel" (Schaff/Manning); can. 5 condemns saying God created "not by his will, free from all necessity, but by a necessity equal to the necessity whereby he loves himself". It excludes creation by necessity of nature. The test case (1.1 Ex 1) is Plotinus's procession from the One, not by choice but as light from the sun (*Enneads* V.1.6), from a source "lacking nothing", with no generation in time ([`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 4.4](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/04-04-plotinus-the-one-intellect-and-soul.md)): it passes "one principle", "not out of need" and "no prior material", and fails "freely" and "from the beginning of time". Plotinus would sign *non ad augendam* and refuse *liberrimo consilio*. 4.4: chapter 1 joins God's "own goodness" to his freedom, so goodness is the motive of a free act.

**Level:** **rung 1** (*Dei Filius* can. 5). How a free act squares with divine simplicity (ST I q.19 a.3; [`philosophy-of-religion` 1.4](../philosophy-of-religion/lessons/01-04-eternity-immutability-and-simplicity.md)): a school question, **no magisterial level**.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-creation-out-of-nothing.md)

### Glory of God as end

The world was made for God's glory, which is his perfection shown in the goods he gives, not something he gains.

*Dei Filius* can. 5 condemns denying "that the world was made for the glory of God". Chapter 1 says how to read it: God made the world "not for the increase or acquirement of his own happiness, but to manifest his perfection by the blessings which he bestows on creatures" (*non ad augendam suam beatitudinem, nec ad acquirendam*). Glory is perfection shown in giving, nothing a God *in se et ex se beatissimus* could gain ([`trinity-and-god` 1.2](../trinity-and-god/lessons/01-02-the-one-true-god-and-what-reason-can-reach.md)). Bonaventure's gloss, quoted in CCC 293: God created not to increase his glory but to show it forth and to communicate it. The beginning ties to the end: what the world was made for is reached in the [new creation](#new-creation).

**Level:** that the world was made for God's glory, **rung 1** (can. 5). That God did not create to increase or acquire his beatitude, **at least rung 3** (chapter 1 alone, by the [chapter rule](#chapters-and-canons)); its denial would also contradict *Firmiter*'s *incommutabilis*, which is the argument for more. Bonaventure's gloss: a theologian's explanation, **no magisterial level**.

**Trap:** reading "for his glory" as divine self-interest. The chapter defines the glory as perfection shown in the goods given; God gains nothing.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-creation-out-of-nothing.md)

### Beginning in time

The world is not eternal: God made it "from the beginning of time".

*Firmiter* (1215): *simul ab initio temporis*; *Dei Filius* ch. 1 repeats it word for word ("from the very first beginning of time", Schaff/Manning). **No canon of Vatican I contains it**: can. 5 has three clauses (out of nothing, freely, for God's glory) and no fourth. Aquinas: "By faith alone do we hold, and by no demonstration can it be proved, that the world did not always exist" (ST I q.46 a.2). Bonaventure, on the second book of the *Sentences*, held it demonstrable (worked in [`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 7.3](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/07-03-the-five-ways-as-text.md), P3). Why no cosmology settles it is [`apologetics-foundations` 4.3](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/04-03-cosmology-what-a-beginning-would-and-would-not-show.md)'s; the kalam is [`philosophy-of-religion` 2.5](../philosophy-of-religion/lessons/02-05-cosmological-arguments-the-kalam.md)'s.

**Level:** **rung 1** through *Firmiter*, restated in *Dei Filius* ch. 1. Whether reason can demonstrate it: **rung 5**, with no act behind either answer.

**Traps:** naming Vatican I as the defining act (siblings write "ch. 1 with its canons"); promoting Aquinas's indemonstrability thesis to Church teaching, or demoting the beginning because its proof is disputed; reading "out of nothing" as "after nothing".

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-creation-out-of-nothing.md)

### Emanation

"Emanation" is Aquinas's own word for creation; what Vatican I condemns is finite things emanating *out of* the divine substance.

ST I q.45 a.1: creation is "the emanation of all being from the universal cause". *Dei Filius* can. 4 condemns saying that finite things, "both corporeal and spiritual, or at least spiritual, have emanated from the divine substance" (Schaff/Manning). The difference is the preposition: *by* God as cause, not *of* God as stuff, Augustine's "by Thee, not of Thee" (*Confessions* XIII.33.48). The paradigm the canon has in view is Plotinian procession: necessary, not by choice, without beginning ([`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 4.4](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/04-04-plotinus-the-one-intellect-and-soul.md)).

**Level:** the condemned sense: its denial **rung 1** (*Dei Filius* can. 4). Aquinas's usage: theology, **no magisterial level**.

**Trap:** treating "emanation" as always the condemned word.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-creation-out-of-nothing.md)

### Goodness of creation

Everything God made is good in what it is: no creature, matter and body included, is evil by nature.

*Firmiter* confesses "one principle of all things ... creator of all things visible and invisible, spiritual and corporeal", sealed by const. 3. Florence's [Decree for the Jacobites](#decree-for-the-jacobites) (4 February 1442): God made all creatures "good, because made by the highest good, but changeable, because made from nothing", and "asserts there is no nature of evil, since every nature, so far as it is a nature, is good" (lesson's translation; *nullamque mali asserit esse naturam*). Its clauses follow Augustine, *Nature of Good* ch. 1. **Vatican I never calls creatures good**: *Dei Filius* can. 5 has all things "spiritual and material" produced "in their whole substance" out of nothing, which leaves a dualist no uncreated stuff and is silent on goodness. The doctrine covers matter, body, sex and food, and even the devil's nature ([fall of the angels](#fall-of-the-angels)). Genesis 1 (DR): "good" six times (1:4, 10, 12, 18, 21, 25) and "very good" over "all the things that he had made" (1:31); day two has no verdict in the Hebrew, the Vulgate or the Douay (the Septuagint adds one at 1:8), and man gets none of his own; CCC 339 rounds ("each one of the works of the six days"). 1 Tim 4:4 (DR): "every creature of God is good". Applied in 2.1 Ex 1: a dog is a trace of God, not his image, and good because God made it; the image-bearer owes it a steward's care ([stewardship](#stewardship)).

**Level:** **rung 1** (*Firmiter*; Florence, with its anathema). GS 14's body "good and honorable" restates rung-1 content in a **rung-3** act: find the act that fixes a claim, not the book you read it in. What evil then is: [evil as privation](#evil-as-privation), **rung 4**. Whether the doctrine says anything about animal death before human sin: no act. Aquinas read "God made not death" (Wis 1:13, DR) of human death (ST I-II q.85 a.6) and held that sin changed no animal's nature, so the lion would not "then have lived on herbs" (ST I q.96 a.1 ad 2); a cosmic fall read from Rom 8:20-22 has no act behind it: open.

**Trap:** "Vatican I defined the goodness of creation" (wrong act).

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-and-god-saw-that-it-was-good.md) (owner), [2.1](lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md)

### Evil as privation

Evil is not a thing but the lack of a good that ought to be there, in something itself good: the tradition's grammar, not its dogma.

Augustine, *Concerning the Nature of Good* 4 (about 405, tr. Newman): evil "is nothing else than corruption, either of the measure, or the form, or the order, that belong to nature"; a corrupted nature, "so far as it is nature it is good, so far as it is corrupted it is evil". Ask *what* evil is before *whence* it comes. Aquinas: evil is the absence of a due good (ST I q.48 aa.1, 3), resting on the convertibility of good and being ([`thomistic-synthesis` 2.4](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/02-04-the-transcendentals.md)). A privation is a real lack in a real thing, not nothing at all: pain exists only in good natures (*Nature of Good* 20). The Catechism never calls evil a privation (CCC 405 and 417 use "deprivation" only of original sin). Why God permits privations: [`philosophy-of-religion` 4.1](../philosophy-of-religion/lessons/04-01-the-logical-problem-of-evil.md) to 4.3; how he governs through defectible causes: [`thomistic-synthesis` 5.2](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/05-02-providence-and-secondary-causes.md).

**Level:** the negative half, "no nature of evil", **rung 1** (Florence, 1442, the only act that states any part of it); the positive analysis, **common teaching, rung 4**: imposed by no act, yet not one free option among equals. 1.4's problems keep the two halves at their two levels.

**Traps:** calling "evil is a privation" dogma (overstated) or a mere opinion (understated); "evil is an absence, so it cannot hurt you".

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-and-god-saw-that-it-was-good.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-creation-and-evolution-in-church-teaching.md)

### Dualism

The view that reality has two first principles, one good and one evil, with matter or the body on the evil side.

Why condemned rather than tolerated as a school: schools dispute how to explain a doctrine they share; dualism denies the doctrine, the Creed's first article. 1 Tim 4:3-4 (DR) names its practices, "Forbidding to marry, to abstain from meats", and answers, "every creature of God is good". Its strength: the shortest answer ever given to the problem of evil, and an ascetic ethic that shamed lax Christians ([Manichaeism](#manichaeism)). It recurs: the Manichees, the [Priscillianists](#priscillianists), the dualism attributed to the [Cathars](#cathars), modern talk of the body as a cage.

**Level:** condemned; its contradictory **rung 1** (*Firmiter*'s one principle; Florence's anathema on the Manichees' "two first principles").

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-and-god-saw-that-it-was-good.md)

### Decree for the Jacobites

Florence's bull of union with the Copts, *Cantate Domino* (4 February 1442, 1441 in Florentine reckoning), issued by Eugene IV with the council.

What the course uses (lesson's translations): God made all creatures "good, because made by the highest good, but changeable, because made from nothing", and "asserts there is no nature of evil, since every nature, so far as it is a nature, is good"; an anathema on the Manichees' two first principles; "every creature of God good" (1 Tim 4:4), so that no food's nature is to be condemned, though much may be given up "for the exercise of virtue" and for discipline ([renunciation, not contempt](#renunciation-not-contempt)). Its clauses follow Augustine, *Nature of Good* ch. 1, on content. Its clause on salvation outside the Church is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 5.1](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/05-01-outside-the-church-there-is-no-salvation.md)'s.

**Level:** **rung 1** for one principle, no nature of evil, every creature good: an ecumenical council's decree, with its anathema. The lessons level the asserted content only, not the "because" clauses as such.

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-and-god-saw-that-it-was-good.md) (owner), [1.4](lessons/01-04-creation-and-evolution-in-church-teaching.md)

### Manichaeism

The religion of Mani (third century): two co-eternal principles, Light and Darkness, and the world a mixture of the two.

Faustus of Milevis, a Manichaean bishop, at his strongest (*Reply to Faustus* XXI.1, tr. Stothert, NPNF1 vol. 4): "It is true, we believe in two principles; but one we call God, and the other Hyle, or, to use common popular language, the devil". Two principles are no more two gods than health and sickness are two healths. The gain is God's innocence: "all the power of evil" belongs to Hyle, so no theodicy is needed. The ethic follows: he left family and money (V.1), and eats no meat, "I think all flesh unclean" (VI.1). Augustine distinguishes Mani's Hyle, a former of bodies, from the ancients' *hyle*, formless matter, not evil (*Nature of Good* 18). Florence anathematizes the Manichees' two first principles; Braga I names Manichaeus throughout. Augustine's own exit from the sect is [`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 5.2](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/05-02-augustine-evil-and-the-will.md)'s.

**Level:** condemned; its contradictory **rung 1** (*Firmiter*; Florence). Faustus and Augustine as authors: **no magisterial level**.

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-and-god-saw-that-it-was-good.md)

### Fall of the angels

Some angels, created good, made themselves evil by their own free choice, and that choice is final.

*Firmiter*: "the devil and the other demons were created by God good by nature, but they became evil through themselves" (*a Deo quidem natura creati sunt boni, sed ipsi per se facti sunt mali*; lesson's translation); its *enim* makes the sentence explain why the one principle stands. Braga I (561) had condemned making the devil "the principle and substance of evil" (can. 7). Scripture's direct witness: "the angels that sinned" (2 Pet 2:4; Jude 6); the name comes late (Job's *ha-satan* is a court title, [`old-testament` 5.3](../old-testament/lessons/05-03-job.md); Wis 2:24 and Rev 12:9 first tie Eden's serpent to the devil). For ever: perpetual punishment "with the devil" (*Firmiter*), the fire "prepared for the devil and his angels" (Matt 25:41). CCC 393 puts the cause in the choice, not in mercy, quoting John Damascene, *Exact Exposition* **II.4** (Salmond): "what in the case of man is death is a fall in the case of angels". Why the choice is fixed is the schools': an angel grasps at once and adheres "fixedly and immovably" (ST I q.64 a.2), while "God's mercy delivers from sin those who repent" (ad 2); or an angel has no bodily weakness for repentance to come from (Damascene II.3). The motive: pride first, envy after (Augustine, *City of God* XII.6; ST I q.63 a.2); several early Fathers made it lust, reading the "sons of God" of Gen 6:2 as angels; Augustine said the first evil will has no efficient cause, and seeking one "is as if some one sought to see darkness, or hear silence" (XII.7, Dods). Wis 2:24 says how death entered the world, not why the devil sinned. 1.2 Ex 1: better than a dog as a nature, worse as a will (Augustine, *Nature of Good* 5, ranks a rational spirit corrupted by an evil will above an incorrupt irrational one).

**Level:** created good, evil by their own doing, punished for ever: **rung 1** (*Firmiter*; Matt 25:41). Unforgivable because the choice is irrevocable, not because mercy is limited: **rung 3** (CCC 393). The motive, pride: **rung 4**. The moment of the fall (just after the first instant, q.63 a.6): **rung 5**; no council has said when. Why the will is fixed: school.

**Traps:** "the demons are evil by nature" (contrary to rung 1); "God cannot forgive the devil" (puts the cause in mercy); citing the Damascene's no-repentance sentence as II.3 (it is II.4; II.3 gives a different reason, incorporeality).

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-and-god-saw-that-it-was-good.md) (owner of the natural goodness), [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-angels.md) (owner of the fall), [2.1](lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md)

### Priscillianists

Followers of Priscillian, a fourth-century Spanish ascetic teacher, condemned for Manichaean dualism at the Council of Braga I (561).

Braga I's anathematisms (lesson's translations of the Latin): souls or angels from God's substance (can. 5); souls that sinned in heaven before their bodies (can. 6, which returns in 2.3 against [pre-existence](#pre-existence-of-souls)); the devil not created a good angel but "the principle and substance of evil" (can. 7); the devil the maker of creatures and weather (can. 8); condemning marriage (can. 11); the body's formation the devil's work (can. 12); all flesh made by evil angels (can. 13); meat held unclean, but only of one who abstains "not for the affliction of his body, but as thinking it uncleanness" (*quasi immunditiam putans*, can. 14). Can. 14 supplies 1.2's test for [renunciation, not contempt](#renunciation-not-contempt).

**Level:** a **provincial council, no rung of its own**; its content stands at **rung 1** through *Firmiter* and Florence. A later conciliar act carries an earlier local one's content.

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-and-god-saw-that-it-was-good.md)

### Renunciation, not contempt

Giving up a good for a better one presupposes that it is good; what is condemned is rejecting a creature as evil or unclean.

The criterion is the *reason*, not the practice. Braga I can. 14 condemns abstaining from meat only "as thinking it uncleanness"; Florence lets much be given up "for the exercise of virtue" and for discipline. The Church ranks goods: Trent XXIV can. 10 (11 November 1563) anathematizes placing marriage above virginity or celibacy, or denying it is better and more blessed to remain in virginity; *Firmiter* professes that the married, not only virgins, reach beatitude. GS 14 calls the body "good and honorable"; GS 49 calls conjugal acts "noble and worthy". The test case (1.2 Ex 2): Faustus abstains because "all flesh" is unclean, a verdict on a nature, and is condemned; Augustine's sensible things "are to be despised,--while, however, they are to be used as this life requires" (*Morals of the Catholic Church* 20.37, 388) orders love and keeps the nature good, "it is evil to use amiss that which is good" (*Nature of Good* 36), and passes. Where the doctrine stops: the tone of devotion. Augustine counted intercourse not sought for offspring a venial sin ([`moral-theology` 6.2](../moral-theology/lessons/06-02-humanae-vitae-the-argument.md)), and that reserve was never condemned; in 1679 the Holy Office condemned, as at least scandalous, the laxist claim that the marital act done for pleasure alone is free of all venial fault ([`moral-theology` 4.1](../moral-theology/lessons/04-01-casuistry-and-the-probabilism-controversy.md)).

**Level:** virginity's precedence and the married clause: **rung 1** (Trent XXIV can. 10; *Firmiter*). GS 49: **rung 3**. The 1679 decree: **rung 3**, a global censure ([in globo censure](#in-globo-censure)).

**Traps:** "fasting, celibacy and virginity deny creation's goodness"; "the Church abandoned Augustine's reserve" (never condemned; its laxist contrary was censured).

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-and-god-saw-that-it-was-good.md)

### Angels

Created spiritual persons, made by God out of nothing, who serve God and the salvation of human beings.

*Firmiter*: God made out of nothing both creatures, "the angelic and the earthly", and then the human, "made up of spirit and body" (lesson's translation); *Dei Filius* ch. 1 repeats it ("the angelical and the mundane", Schaff); CCC 328 calls their existence a truth of faith. Origen saw the grading around 230 (*On First Principles*, Preface 6 and 10, Crombie's translation of Rufinus): "laid down" that they exist and that the good serve our salvation; "held by most" that the devil was an angel who fell; "not clearly stated" when they were made, what they are, how they exist. When: *Firmiter*'s *simul ab initio temporis* leaves the temporal sense undefined; Aquinas, about fifty years after the council, calls creation with the material world "the more probable" opinion and the contrary "not to be deemed erroneous", since the Greek Fathers put the angels first (ST I q.61 a.3; for *simul*'s two senses see [beginning in time](#beginning-in-time) and 1.1 Ex 2). Persons: *Humani Generis* §26 counts doubting "whether angels are personal beings" among the day's errors (CCC 330). *Firmiter* distinguishes the angelic creature from the human, so the dead do not become angels.

| Claim (1.3's sorting) | Level |
|---|---|
| they exist, created by God out of nothing | **rung 1** (*Firmiter*; *Dei Filius* ch. 1) |
| they are spiritual creatures | **rung 1**; "pure spirits", **rung 3** ([pure spirit](#pure-spirit)) |
| they are persons with intellect and will | **rung 3** (HG §26; CCC 330) |
| made good, some evil by their own doing, punished for ever | **rung 1**; unforgivable because irrevocable, **rung 3** ([fall of the angels](#fall-of-the-angels)) |
| they guard human beings | **at least rung 3** ([guardian angels](#guardian-angels)) |
| made together with the material world | **rung 4** (common teaching) |
| each angel its own species (ST I q.50 a.4, against Scotus: [`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 8.3](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/08-03-scotus-common-nature-and-haecceity.md)) | **rung 5** |
| knowledge by infused ideas (qq.54-58); the [nine choirs](#nine-choirs); matter in angels; the moment of the fall | **rung 5** |

Ludwig Ott's notes (*Fundamentals of Catholic Dogma*), which the lesson reports, are a theologian's: **no magisterial level**; so are Aquinas, the Damascene and Origen as authors.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-angels.md)

### Pure spirit

A created being with no body at all: how much of that is defined is the question, and the answer comes in three layers.

(1) That angels are spiritual creatures, not of the bodily order: *Firmiter*'s own words, the angelic creature the spiritual one against man, made of spirit and body (Ott: *de fide*). What "spiritual" excludes, it does not say. (2) That they are "pure spirits", with no body of any kind: Paul VI's *Credo of the People of God* (30 June 1968) §8, "the pure spirits which are also called angels"; CCC 330, "purely spiritual creatures"; the *Credo* says of itself that it is not strictly a dogmatic definition (§3). Default down: *Firmiter*'s target was dualism, not angelic constitution; many Fathers gave angels a fine, airy body (Ott; ST I q.51 a.1); the Damascene calls them incorporeal only relative to us (*Exact Exposition* II.3). (3) The Thomist sense, a subsisting form with no matter whatever (ST I q.50 aa.1-2): a school thesis; after 1215 the Franciscans still composed angels of matter and form (rejected in q.50 a.2).

**Level:** spiritual, **rung 1**; pure spirits, **rung 3**; a form with no matter at all, **rung 5** (school).

**Traps:** calling "pure spirit" defined because a papal *Credo* says it; demoting the angels' spirituality because Fathers gave them subtle bodies.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-angels.md)

### Humani Generis

Pius XII's encyclical of 12 August 1950 on opinions threatening Catholic doctrine: the course reads five of its paragraphs, each doing a different job.

| Paragraph | What it does (paraphrased; the vatican.va English is in copyright) | Level as the lessons mark it | Lessons |
|---|---|---|---|
| §5 | rejects evolution as the monist explanation of the origin of all things, a philosophy rather than a science | rides on the rung-1 dogma of creation | [1.4](lessons/01-04-creation-and-evolution-in-church-teaching.md) |
| §20 | if the popes in their official documents purposely pass judgment on a matter until then disputed, it is no longer open to free discussion among theologians | the principle that makes §§26 and 37 closing acts ([`fundamental-theology` 4.3](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-03-religious-submission.md)) | [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md) |
| §26 | lists among errors: doubting "whether angels are personal beings"; destroying "the gratuity of the supernatural order" by holding that God cannot create intellectual beings without ordering and calling them to the beatific vision | **rung 3** for each | [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-angels.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md) |
| §36 | *non prohibet*: research and discussion of the human body's origin from pre-existing living matter permitted to experts, on conditions (reasons on both sides weighed with seriousness, moderation and measure; all ready to submit to the Church's judgment), with a rebuke for treating that origin as proved; and a parenthesis: the Catholic faith "obliges us to hold" that souls are immediately created by God (*animas enim a Deo immediate creari catholica fides nos retinere iubet*) | the permission **rung 3**; the soul clause **at least rung 3**, rung 1 only when argued ([immediate creation of the soul](#immediate-creation-of-the-soul)); the rebuke judges the evidence of 1950 | [1.4](lessons/01-04-creation-and-evolution-in-church-teaching.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md) |
| §37 | the faithful cannot embrace polygenism, since "it is in no way apparent how" it can be reconciled with original sin | **rung 3** as an act; never a definition, never withdrawn ([monogenism and polygenism](#monogenism-and-polygenism)) | [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md) |

§36's three operative verbs do three jobs (1.4): *non prohibet* permits; *retinere iubet* states an obligation the encyclical attributes to the faith, not to itself, and *retinere* is to keep what is already held (2.3's main evidence for a rung-1 reading); the rebuke estimates a science. §26 (4.4) names no one, never uses "pure nature", leaves innate and elicited desire undecided, and speaks of intellectual beings, not this human race; Henri de Lubac's removal from teaching two months earlier was his Jesuit superiors' discipline, no rung.

**Level:** an encyclical: authentic ordinary magisterium, **rung 3**, by default, each clause weighed by its verb ([`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md)).

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-angels.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-creation-and-evolution-in-church-teaching.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md)

### Apokatastasis

The final restoration of all rational creatures to God, the damned and even the devil included.

Origen: "the end is always like the beginning" (*On First Principles* I.6.2, Crombie from Rufinus). Gregory of Nyssa, *On the Soul and the Resurrection* (380): evil has no being of its own to last; what God inflicts is "not punishment chiefly and principally" but the separation of good from evil, until "nothing shall be left outside the world of goodness"; in the *Great Catechism* (385) ch. 26 Christ is "healing even the introducer of evil himself". The case argues from God's nature: infinite goodness, punishment as medicine, evil finite. Condemnations: Justinian's edict of 543, endorsed by a synod at Constantinople, condemned the claim that the punishment of demons (and, can. 9, of impious men) is temporary and that a restoration will follow; the fifteen anathemas associated with 553 condemn "the monstrous restoration which follows from" pre-existence ([Origenist anathemas](#origenist-anathemas), standing contested). Nyssa is never named, and the fifteen strike a restoration bound to pre-existence, which he rejected; but *Firmiter* excludes his conclusion.

**Level:** universal restoration *asserted* is excluded at **rung 1** by *Firmiter*'s *poenam perpetuam* (with Matt 25:41 for the devil), not by 553. The 543 canons: an emperor's edict endorsed by a local synod, **no conciliar rung of their own**.

**Keep apart:** the hope that all *human beings* may be saved is **rung 5**, freely disputed ([hope for all](#hope-for-all)); it does not reach the demons, whose restoration was condemned in 543. Universalism *asserted* in restorationist form contradicts the defined eternity, as apokatastasis did; Balthasar rejected a hell emptied at the end (7.3).

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-angels.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md) (owner), [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md)

### Guardian angels

Angels are sent to watch over human beings; that each person has his own is the tradition's reading of Matthew 18:10.

Matt 18:10 (DR): "their angels in heaven always see the face of my Father"; Heb 1:14; Ps 90:11 [91:11]; Acts 12:15. CCC 336 surrounds human life with their care and quotes Basil: each believer has an angel beside him; CCC 352. The Roman Catechism (1566, Part IV) has angels "accompanying every human being" (McHugh and Callan). Aquinas gives each person a guardian from birth, not baptism (ST I q.113 aa.2, 5, with Jerome on Matt 18:10). The memorial is 2 October; devotion is commended, never imposed (CCC 335).

**Level:** that angels guard human beings: **at least rung 3** (CCC 336, 352; the Roman Catechism; the liturgy). Ott's "*de fide* on the ground of general teaching" is a defensible rung-1 reading through the ordinary universal magisterium, but no act manifestly proposes it as revealed: default down. That each person has his own: **rung 4**, common teaching (Ott: each believer's angel certain, everyone's from birth the general view of theologians; CCC 336 gives it only in Basil's words). From birth or from baptism: **rung 5** (q.113 a.5 reports both opinions).

**Trap:** calling "everyone has a guardian angel" defined, or a nursery story.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-angels.md)

### Nine choirs

The ranking of the angels into nine orders in three triads.

Seraphim, cherubim, thrones; dominations, virtues, powers; principalities, archangels, angels. The names are scriptural (Col 1:16; Eph 1:21; seraphim, cherubim, archangels elsewhere); the system is Pseudo-Dionysius, *Celestial Hierarchy* (about 500), an anonymous author, not Paul's convert ([`patristics` 5.4](../patristics/lessons/05-04-maximus-and-john-of-damascus.md)). Gregory the Great ranks principalities and virtues differently (ST I q.108 a.6).

**Level:** **no magisterial act**: a free theological opinion, **rung 5** (Ott: "not a truth of Faith").

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-angels.md)

### Demythologizing

Reading the New Testament's spirits and demons as the mythical dress of an existential message: Rudolf Bultmann's programme.

Bultmann, "New Testament and Mythology" (1941), paraphrased (no public-domain English exists): no one who uses electric light, the radio and modern medicine can also believe in the New Testament's world of spirits and miracles. Its due: much the Gospels call possession we treat medically, and an exorcist must first rule out illness (CCC 1673). Where the doctrine bites (1.3 Ex 2): *Firmiter* gives the devil a nature God made good and an evil he made himself; a symbol has neither, so "no one fell" denies a **rung-1** clause, not a scholastic gloss. Paul VI's general audience of 15 November 1972 ("Liberaci dal male"), paraphrased: evil is not merely a lack but a living spiritual agent, perverted and perverting; refusing to acknowledge it leaves biblical and Church teaching, and so does making it a principle of its own. Where the doctrine stops: it names no event, illness or temptation as demonic; the exorcisms as history are [`new-testament` 3.3](../new-testament/lessons/03-03-what-historians-broadly-agree-on.md)'s. A Catholic may attribute almost nothing in particular to demons; he may not hold that nobody is there.

**Level:** the denied clause, **rung 1** (*Firmiter*); the 1972 audience, ordinary teaching, **rung 3 at most**; exorcism, **law and liturgical law, no rung** ([`sacraments-and-liturgy` 7.3](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md)); Bultmann as author, **no magisterial level**.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-angels.md)

### Hell

The state, after death, of those who die in unrepented mortal sin: definitive self-exclusion from God, with a punishment that is perpetual.

Scripture (DR): Mark 9:43-48 [DR 9:42-47], Gehenna, "where their worm dieth not" (echoing Isa 66:24); Matt 25:41, the "everlasting fire, which was prepared for the devil and his angels"; Matt 25:46, one adjective for "everlasting punishment" and "life everlasting"; Rev 20:10, 14-15 and 21:8, "the second death". The acts: the [Athanasian Creed](#athanasian-creed), "they that have done evil, into everlasting fire" (Schaff); *Firmiter*, "the former perpetual punishment with the devil, the latter everlasting glory with Christ", said of the final state after the resurrection; [Benedictus Deus](#benedictus-deus) (1336): those who die in actual mortal sin descend to hell at once (*mox*), with no *perpetuam* in that clause; Florence, [Laetentur caeli](#laetentur-caeli) (1439), under its *diffinimus*: at once into hell, to unequal punishments, repeating the [Lyons II profession](#lyons-ii-profession) (1274). Hell is "a state" (CCC 1033). 1.3 reaches it from the angels: the fall fixes an angel's will as death fixes a man's (Damascene II.4), and the fire was "prepared for the devil and his angels"; 6.1 places it as the third outcome at death.

**Level (6.4 owns):** existence and eternity, **rung 1** (*Firmiter*; the Athanasian Creed). Immediate descent of those dying in actual mortal sin, to unequal punishments, **rung 1** (Florence, with *Benedictus Deus*, whose *ex cathedra* standing is open, and the CDF's 1998 commentary, which counts "immediate recompense after death" among revealed truths). The lost lack the vision of God for ever, **rung 1**; that this loss is the chief punishment, **at least rung 3** (CCC 1035) ([pain of loss and pain of sense](#pain-of-loss-and-pain-of-sense)); a punishment besides it, named fire, **at least rung 3**; no one there but by his own unrepented mortal sin, **at least rung 3** ([self-exclusion](#self-exclusion)). Where hell is; real fire or conscience; why the will is fixed: **rung 5**. Lyons II's profession defines nothing of itself.

**Who is there:** that any named person is damned is **taught by no act**; the Church canonizes (**rung 2**) but declares no one damned (6.1, 6.4). 7.3: "that some human beings are damned" is taught by no act either; that damnation is a real possibility for every living person, **at least rung 3** (John Paul II, general audience, 28 July 1999; CCC 1036); that no one can be absolutely certain of his own perseverance without special revelation, **rung 1** (Trent VI can. 16). 7.4: medieval *infernus* covered the whole lower world, so Florence's "descend into hell" for those dying with original sin only does not of itself mean the hell of fire ([solo originali clause](#solo-originali-clause)).

**Trap:** grounding hell's eternity in the 553 anathemas.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-the-angels.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-death-and-the-particular-judgment.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md) (owner), [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md), [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

### Two-questions frame

The sciences ask how bodies came from earlier bodies; the doctrine asks why anything exists and who wills it, so the answers do not compete.

God is the cause of every cause's acting, not one more cause beside them: "One action does not proceed from two agents of the same order" (ST I q.105 a.5 ad 2; [`thomistic-synthesis` 5.2](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/05-02-providence-and-secondary-causes.md)). CCC 284: the meaning of origins is "a question of another order". John Paul II's 1996 message (§6) states it as method: the passage to the spiritual is not an object of observation. Benedict XVI at Auronzo (2007) states it popularly: the supposed antithesis of "creationism" and evolutionism is "absurd". So the Church has said almost nothing about biology: only how Scripture speaks of nature, what in us no biology makes (the soul), and what no process escapes (providence).

**Level:** the frame is a theologians' explanation, **common teaching, rung 4**, not a magisterial act; what it protects, creation and universal providence, is **rung 1**.

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-creation-and-evolution-in-church-teaching.md)

### Providentissimus Deus

Leo XIII's encyclical on Scripture (18 November 1893): the sacred writers did not set out to teach the inner constitution of visible things.

§18 (lesson's translation of the Latin): the Spirit of God who spoke through the writers "did not will to teach men these things" (the inner constitution of visible things) "which profit no one for salvation"; so they described things figuratively or as common speech bore, and "followed what sensibly appears". Two embedded quotations: Augustine, *On the Literal Meaning of Genesis* II.9.20; Aquinas, ST I q.70 a.1 ad 3 ("Moses describes what is obvious to sense, out of condescension to popular ignorance", English Dominican translation). It defends Augustine's rule: what is truly demonstrated about nature can be shown not to contradict Scripture. The claim concerns *intention*, what the Spirit willed to teach; it does not confine Scripture's truth to faith and morals, which §20 rejects ([`fundamental-theology` 3.2](../fundamental-theology/lessons/03-02-inerrancy.md)). It names neither Darwin nor Galileo; the Galileo application is John Paul II's address of 31 October 1992. Its phrase for the inner constitution of visible things is repeated in Response VII of the [Biblical Commission 1909](#biblical-commission-1909).

**Level:** the appearances principle, **rung 3** (encyclical teaching); inerrancy itself, **rung 1** ([`fundamental-theology` 4.4](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-04-the-ladder-of-doctrinal-authority.md), Ex 1).

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-creation-and-evolution-in-church-teaching.md)

### Biblical Commission 1909

The Pontifical Biblical Commission's eight answers of 30 June 1909 on the historical character of Genesis 1-3.

Response III (lesson's translation): the literal historical sense may not be doubted where the narrated facts touch "the foundations of the Christian religion", among others the creation of all things at the beginning of time, "the special creation of man" (*peculiaris creatio hominis*), the woman formed from the man, "the unity of the human race" (*generis humani unitas*), the original happiness in justice, integrity and immortality, the command, the transgression at the devil's persuasion under the form of a serpent, the casting down from the first state, and the promise of a Redeemer. Response V: not every word need be taken in its proper sense where it is plainly figurative. Response VII: Genesis 1 does not aim to teach the inner constitution of visible things scientifically ([Providentissimus Deus](#providentissimus-deus)'s phrase). Response VIII: *yom*, "day", may be a natural day or a period, freely discussed among exegetes.

**Level:** binding in conscience when issued (Pius X's motu proprio *Praestantia Scripturae*, 18 November 1907, covers decisions issued and to be issued); never formally revoked; the Commission advisory since 1971: "neither dogma nor a dead letter" ([`old-testament` 2.1](../old-testament/lessons/02-01-two-accounts-of-creation.md) owns their weight). Response VIII's freedom: **rung 5**, with an act behind it. Response III's facts keep their weight through other acts.

**Traps:** "void because the Commission is now advisory" (understates); "they still bind as in 1909" (overstates).

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-creation-and-evolution-in-church-teaching.md)

### Monogenism and polygenism

Monogenism: every human being descends from one first pair. Polygenism: the first human beings were many, or some true human beings did not descend from Adam.

*Humani Generis* §37 (paraphrased): polygenism is another conjectural opinion, and the children of the Church do not enjoy the liberty §36 grants to research on the body. The faithful cannot embrace either (a) that after Adam there existed on earth true men (*veros homines*) who did not take their origin by natural generation from him as the first parent of all, or (b) that "Adam" signifies a certain number of first parents, since "it is in no way apparent how" such an opinion can be reconciled with what the sources of revelation and the acts of the Magisterium propose about original sin: a sin truly committed by one Adam which, passed to all by generation, is in each as his own. Restated by Paul VI ([Paul VI 1966 address](#paul-vi-1966-address)); still cited by CCC 390 n. 265. In 1.4 it is the one origins question an act has closed head-on, and it is a question about original sin. Neither Trent V's "the first man, Adam" nor CCC 390's "our first parents" defines monogenism. Positions now argued (3.4): still required; a first pair within a larger population (Kemp; Swamidass); a first community (Rahner, 1967); neither monogenism nor descent needed (Schoonenberg). The ITC's "whether as individuals or in populations" (2004 §70) carries no level and settles nothing.

**Level (3.4 owns):** HG §37 is **rung 3** as an act, an encyclical's deliberate judgment on a disputed matter by its own §20 ([`fundamental-theology` 4.3](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-03-religious-submission.md), Ex 2): never a definition (can. 749 §3), never withdrawn. Its reason is **epistemic** ("in no way apparent how"), not a conditional, and not a claim that polygenism contradicts dogma. Whether the 1950 judgment still binds is disputed among theologians, but because a standing act has spoken, monogenism is **not rung 5**. "Conjectural" and "not demonstrated" estimate a science: **no rung**.

**Traps:** calling monogenism defined; treating HG §37 as rescinded.

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-creation-and-evolution-in-church-teaching.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md) (owner)

### John Paul II 1996 message

John Paul II's message to the plenary of the Pontifical Academy of Sciences, dated 22 October 1996, on the origins of life and evolution.

The original is French; vatican.va has no English (EWTN carries the amended *L'Osservatore Romano* English). §2 cites *Providentissimus Deus* for "truth cannot contradict truth"; §3 recalls *Humani Generis* and his 1992 Galileo address. §4: almost half a century after the encyclical (*près d'un demi-siècle*; the English still says "more than"), new knowledge leads to recognizing in the theory of evolution *plus qu'une hypothèse*, "more than a hypothesis", by the unsought convergence of independent results (the first English misprint "more than one hypothesis" is [`apologetics-foundations` 4.2](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/04-02-evolution-design-and-the-human-person.md)'s). There are *theories* of evolution, read in materialist or spiritualist ways, and judging them belongs to philosophy and theology. §5 quotes Pius XII's Latin on the soul and rejects theories that make spirit emerge from living matter or a mere epiphenomenon of it. §6: an "ontological leap" (*saut ontologique*), whose moment observation cannot reach.

**Level:** the lightest grade of **rung 3** for the doctrine it restates (the soul; the rejection of materialist readings); its estimate of the science, **no rung**. He revised the 1950 *estimate*, not the 1950 *doctrine*.

**Trap:** "1996 made evolution Church teaching" (or "1950 condemned it").

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-creation-and-evolution-in-church-teaching.md)

### Communion and Stewardship

The International Theological Commission's study *Communion and Stewardship: Human Persons Created in the Image of God* (2004).

On science (§§62-70, 1.4): §63 summarizes "the widely accepted scientific account", a universe 15 billion years old (now about 13.8) and common descent "virtually certain"; §64: the 1996 message blesses no theory denying providence a causal role; §69: design against chance "cannot be settled by theology", true contingency fits a purposeful providence, and an "unguided evolutionary process" outside providence "simply cannot exist"; §70: the first humans' emergence, "whether as individuals or in populations", has no purely natural explanation, and the soul is specially created. On the image (2.1): §9, the whole human being is created in the image, which no single aspect, the intellect included, exhausts; §10, man an essentially relational being; §16 reports Aquinas's three stages ([three grades of the image](#three-grades-of-the-image)); §25, communion and stewardship "the two great strands" of the doctrine; §31, bodiliness shares in the image; §46, the image disfigured, never destroyed, by sin; §57, rule as participation in the divine governance, "a form of stewardship"; §§72-73, the charge that the image doctrine caused the ecological crisis, answered.

**Level:** **no magisterial level** (an advisory commission). Its preliminary note: approved *in forma specifica* by the Commission's own written ballots, then publication permitted by its president, Cardinal Ratzinger: **not** papal approval *in forma specifica* of a dicastery's act ([`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md)). Its content keeps its own levels elsewhere: providence **rung 1** (*Dei Filius* ch. 1); stewardship and communion **rung 3** (CCC 373; GS 12, 24; CCC 1702).

**Traps:** "approved *in forma specifica*, so papal teaching"; "the Church teaches an age of the universe" (no act teaches any timetable; the ITC's figure summarized the science and has been revised).

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-creation-and-evolution-in-church-teaching.md) (owner), [2.1](lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md)

### Immediate creation of the soul

Each human soul is created directly by God, with no created cause (parents, bodily process, angel) producing it; "immediately" says nothing about when.

*Humani Generis* §36 (1950): the Catholic faith "obliges us to hold" it (*animas enim a Deo immediate creari catholica fides nos retinere iubet*); CCC 366: each spiritual soul is created immediately by God, not "produced" by the parents. Earlier witnesses (2.3): Anastasius II, *Bonum atque iucundum* (23 August 498), rejected the claim that parents pass on the soul with the body: what they hand on *per traducem* is guilt and penalty (*culpa poenaque peccati*), not the soul; Leo IX's profession (*Congratulamur vehementer*, 13 April 1053): the soul "not a part of God but created from nothing" (lesson's translation). Paul VI's *Credo* (1968) §8 confesses God creator of each man's soul **without** "immediately": cite it only for what it says. Aquinas: parents beget a human being, since "man begets his like" by disposing matter for its form (ST I q.118 a.2 ad 4), while a spiritual substance "cannot be caused through generation, but only through creation by God" (a.2). It excludes [traducianism](#traducianism) and [generationism](#generationism), and so hands 3.3 its problem: sin cannot travel with the soul ([transmission of original sin](#transmission-of-original-sin)). 1.4: wherever there is a human being there is a soul God created directly; nothing about when in the biological record, by what marker, or in how many first humans. 2.3 Ex 1: with IVF too, the parents beget the child and the laboratory serves that bodily process; the soul is from God alone.

**Level (2.3 owns):** **at least rung 3**; nothing defines it. **Rung 1 by the ordinary universal magisterium only when so argued.** For: HG ascribes the obligation to "the Catholic faith", not to itself; *retinere* means keeping what is already held; the teaching is constant since 498, universal in the schools, and follows from defined truths. Against: no act proposes it as revealed; HG says it in a parenthesis; the CDF's 1998 commentary (§11) lists immortality among revealed truths but not immediate creation; can. 749 §3 defaults down. Anything below rung 3 is wrong. That no spirit can be generated (q.118 a.2): **rung 4**.

**Keep apart:** immediate creation (no created cause) from the [time of ensoulment](#time-of-ensoulment) (**rung 5**). "The Church teaches the soul is created at conception" overstates; the soul's creation is no gap in embryology or the fossil record (1996 §6; ITC 2004 §70).

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-creation-and-evolution-in-church-teaching.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-body-and-soul-one-substance.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md) (owner)


**The human person (Module 2)**

### Image of God

That every human being is made by God as a copy pointing back to him (Gen 1:26-27): the root of human dignity.

An image is a copy that points back to its original; one egg exactly like another is not its image, not having been copied from it (ST I q.93 a.1). Scripture says it of fallen men too: Adam "begot a son to his own image and likeness" (Gen 5:3, DR); murder is forbidden "for man was made to the image of God" (9:6); Jas 3:9; Wis 2:23. *Gaudium et Spes* 12 reports it as Scripture's teaching: man, made to God's image, can know and love his Creator and is set over earthly creatures (CCC 355-357). It survives sin: "present in every man" (CCC 1702); disfigured by sin, man remains in the image though deprived of God's glory, his "likeness" (CCC 705); GS 22; *Dignitas Infinita* §22. Where it lies: the Church names faculties, not a location: the soul is that by which man is most especially in God's image (CCC 363), freedom its outstanding manifestation (CCC 1705; GS 17); the body "shares in the dignity" of the image (CCC 364; DI §18), because the soul is its form (2.2). It grounds dignity and equality (CCC 1700, 1934; GS 29), and communion (GS 12, 24; CCC 1702) and [stewardship](#stewardship) are what the ITC calls the doctrine's "two great strands" (*Communion and Stewardship* §25). The words *tselem* and *demut* and their readings are [`old-testament` 2.1](../old-testament/lessons/02-01-two-accounts-of-creation.md)'s; Augustine's triad of memory, understanding and will is [`trinity-and-god` 4.2](../trinity-and-god/lessons/04-02-augustine-the-image-in-the-mind.md)'s. "In no way is God in man's image" (CCC 370). Said plainly in 2.1: Aquinas gave the image "in its principal signification" to both sexes, yet put a secondary sense of it in the man and not the woman (I q.93 a.4 ad 1); the Catechism teaches one and the same dignity for both (CCC 369).

| Claim (2.1's sorting) | Level |
|---|---|
| every human being is made in God's image | **rung 1**: asserted formally in Scripture and always proposed as revealed by the ordinary universal magisterium; GS 12 reports it, and no definition was needed |
| it survives sin and is present in every human being | **at least rung 3** (CCC 705, 1702; GS 22; DI §22); its core, free will not "lost and extinguished", **rung 1** (Trent VI can. 5) |
| intellect and freedom manifest it; the body shares its dignity | **rung 3** (GS 12, 17; CCC 356, 363, 364, 1705) |
| equal, indelible ontological dignity | **rung 3**: a rung-3 act ([Dignitas Infinita](#dignitas-infinita)) has spoken, so not rung 5; none proposes it as revealed, so not rung 1 (the lesson's table words it "at least rung 3") |
| Aquinas's grades of nature, grace and glory | **rung 4** ([three grades of the image](#three-grades-of-the-image)) |
| the mind alone, the body a trace | **rung 5** ([image and trace](#image-and-trace)) |

**Traps:** "the Church has defined what the image is" (only *that* we bear it is rung 1); "the image is the soul, defined at Vienne" (Vienne defines the soul as the body's form, not the image); "image" said of Christ and of us in one sense ([Christ the Image](#christ-the-image)); a capacity test for the image (newborns and the comatose have it: CCC 1702; DI §24). GS 24 is often quoted without its "on earth": man is the one creature *on earth* God willed for itself.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md) (owner), [2.2](lessons/02-02-body-and-soul-one-substance.md)

### Three grades of the image

Aquinas's scheme: one image in three grades, by nature in all, by grace in the just, by glory in the blessed.

ST I q.93 a.4 (English Dominican translation): "a natural aptitude for understanding and loving God", which "consists in the very nature of the mind, which is common to all men"; "the conformity of grace"; "the likeness of glory". "The first is found in all men, the second only in the just, the third only in the blessed." One image measured by one act, knowing and loving God. The first grade is an *aptitude* of nature, not an exercised capacity: a newborn has it whole. Sin reaches the second and third grades, never the first: the objection from sin concerns only "the image consisting in the conformity of grace and glory" (ad 2-3); mortal sin loses grace ([`moral-theology` 3.4](../moral-theology/lessons/03-04-sin-mortal-and-venial.md)) while the first grade remains. The second grade is [sanctifying grace](#sanctifying-grace) (4.3), the third the [beatific vision](#beatific-vision) (6.2). The labels "creation", "re-creation" and "likeness" are not Aquinas's but those of the gloss on Ps 4:6 [DR 4:7] he quotes; ITC 2004 §16 blends the two.

**Level:** **rung 4**: Aquinas's account, common teaching of the schools; no act proposes it.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md)

### Sanctifying grace

The lasting, created gift by which the soul shares in God's own life and is made holy and pleasing to him: habitual grace.

CCC 1999-2000 (paraphrased): "sanctifying or deifying grace", a habitual gift, a stable supernatural disposition perfecting the soul itself, distinct from [actual graces](#actual-grace). For Aquinas a quality, an accidental form of the soul, the root of the infused virtues, "a participation of the Divine Nature" (ST I-II q.110 aa.2-3), seated in the soul's essence (a.4); the argument against "favour only" is q.110 a.1 (God's love causes the good it loves, man's presupposes it). How the course uses it: the second grade of the image, "the conformity of grace", found "only in the just" (2.1); the root of [original justice](#original-justice), the holiness and justice of Trent V can. 1 read as the grace restored in justification (3.1); what the child of Adam lacks, "the grace whereby they are made just" given in the new birth (Trent VI ch. 3; 3.3); habitual grace heals the mind but not yet the flesh, so the justified still need God's motion for each good act, and perseverance needs no new habit (ST I-II q.109 aa.9-10; 4.2); the state of grace as the condition of merit (5.3). Its relation to the indwelling and to [uncreated grace](#uncreated-and-created-grace) is 4.3's.

**Level (4.3 owns):** that justifying grace inheres, not favour only: **rung 1** (Trent VI can. 11, 13 January 1547). Stable and habitual: **at least rung 3** (CCC 2000). Adam's holiness and justice identified with this grace: **at least rung 3** (CCC 375; Pius V against Baius, props. 21, 23); whether he had it from the first instant: **rung 5** ([constitutus not creatus](#constitutus-not-creatus)). A quality, an accidental form: **rung 4**. Seated in the essence rather than the will; really distinct from charity or one with it: **rung 5**.

**Trap:** the English Dominicans' "sanctifying grace" in I-II q.111 a.1 renders *gratia gratum faciens*, a division by purpose and wider than CCC 2000's habitual grace, a division by duration ([gratum faciens and gratis data](#gratum-faciens-and-gratis-data)).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md) (owner), [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md)

### Beatific vision

The created mind's direct sight of God's own essence, which makes it blessed.

Scripture (DR): 1 Cor 13:12; 1 John 3:2; Matt 5:8. Heaven is named by its object, not first as a place or a prize: CCC 1023-1029 (a communion of life and love with the Trinity and all the blessed, 1024; God seen only if he gives the capacity, 1028). Augustine: God "has promised Himself" (*City of God* XXII.30). What the acts say of it, and its precisions, are under [Benedictus Deus](#benedictus-deus) and [Laetentur caeli](#laetentur-caeli); the [light of glory](#light-of-glory), [degrees of glory](#degrees-of-glory) and [incomprehensibility](#incomprehensibility) have their own entries. In 2.1 it is the third grade of the image, "the likeness of glory", found "only in the blessed"; in 4.4 it is unowed to any nature, and no created nature reaches it by its own progress (*Dei Filius* can. II.3); in 6.1 it is the first of the three outcomes at death, for those with nothing to purge; in 7.4 original sin unremitted excludes from it.

**Level (6.2 owns):** the essence seen at once by the fully purified, before the resurrection: **rung 1** (Florence under its *diffinimus*, with the CDF's 1998 commentary on the *Professio fidei* §11, "immediate recompense after death"); *Benedictus Deus*'s *ex cathedra* status is open and the rung does not wait on it ([`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-04-infallibility-in-exercise-and-the-ordinary-universal-magisterium.md), Ex 1). No creature reaches it of itself: **rung 1** (*Dei Filius* can. II.3). God could create intellectual beings without calling them to it: **rung 3** (*Humani Generis* §26; 4.4). A light of glory is needed: **at least rung 3**. The blessed see God without comprehending him: **rung 4**. Degrees of glory by merit: **rung 1**. Christ's earthly vision is [`christology` 4.3](../christology/lessons/04-03-the-beatific-vision-in-christ.md)'s.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-death-and-the-particular-judgment.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md) (owner), [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

### Image and likeness

The pair *tselem* (a carved figure) and *demut* (likeness) of Gen 1:26, and the patristic reading that they name two different gifts.

The words and their exegesis (the royal statue; the modern near-synonyms view) are [`old-testament` 2.1](../old-testament/lessons/02-01-two-accounts-of-creation.md)'s, whose card has its own entry. Irenaeus splits them: man has the image "in his formation" (*in plasmate*) but receives the likeness "through the Spirit" (*Against Heresies* V.6.1, ANF, tr. Rambaut, whose English says "similitude"); man easily lost it, and the incarnate Word re-established it (V.16.2). John Damascene: "after His image" is mind and free will, "after His likeness" likeness in virtue so far as possible (*Exact Exposition* II.12). Aquinas gives likeness two senses, as preamble to the image and as its perfection (ST I q.93 a.9). CCC 705 uses the pair: disfigured by sin, man remains in the image but is deprived of God's glory, his "likeness"; *Dignitas Infinita* §22 reports the Irenaean and Damascene distinction.

**Level:** as exegesis of Genesis, a patristic reading, **no magisterial level**; the doctrine it carries (sin disfigures, grace restores) is **rung 3**.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md)

### Image and trace

Aquinas's distinction: an image represents by likeness in species, which only an intellectual nature has; a trace represents by way of an effect, as every creature does.

ST I q.93 a.2: "intellectual creatures alone, properly speaking, are made to God's image". a.6: the image is found even in the rational creature only in the mind; the rest of us, like a dog or a star, is a trace, as "ashes are a trace of fire"; the upright body represents the image in the soul by way of a trace (ad 3). So, absolutely speaking, the angels are more in God's image than man (a.3), who is more like God only in some secondary respects (man from man as God from God; the whole soul in the whole body and in every part). Rivals: Irenaeus puts the image in the moulded flesh too (AH V.6.1); the ITC will not confine it to the intellect (*Communion and Stewardship* §9) and has bodiliness share in it (§31). The Magisterium says only that the body "shares in the dignity" of the image (CCC 364; DI §18). 2.1 Ex 1: an old sheepdog is a trace, not an image, and good because God made it ([goodness of creation](#goodness-of-creation)).

**Level:** the mind alone, the body a trace: **rung 5**, a disputed school thesis. The body's share in the image's dignity: **rung 3**.

**Trap:** "the angels, lacking bodies, image God less than we do" (inverted by q.93 a.3, and school theology either way).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md)

### Dignitas Infinita

The Dicastery for the Doctrine of the Faith's declaration on human dignity, dated 2 April 2024.

Approved by Francis at the audience of 25 March 2024, who ordered its publication: ordinary approval (*in forma communi*), so the act is the dicastery's ([`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md)). Paraphrased (in copyright): §1, an infinite dignity, inalienably grounded in the person's being; §§7-8, the [four senses of dignity](#four-senses-of-dignity); §18, the Creator has imprinted the indelible features of his image on every person, and the body participates in its own way in being in God's image; §22, Irenaeus and the Damascene distinguished image and likeness, and sin can never cancel the fact that the human being is created in God's image and likeness; §24, dignity cannot hang on a judgement of the person's ability to understand and act freely; the only prerequisite is membership of the human species.

**Level:** **rung 3**: taught, not defined, by a declaration with ordinary papal approval. It closes a question once arguable from Aquinas (II-II q.64 a.2 ad 3), so indelible ontological dignity is not rung 5; no act proposes it as revealed, so it is not rung 1.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md)

### Four senses of dignity

*Dignitas Infinita*'s distinction of ontological dignity, which is indelible, from moral, social and existential dignity.

DI §§7-8, paraphrased. *Ontological*: the person's simply because she exists and is willed, created and loved by God; indelible. *Moral*: how freedom is exercised; it can in fact be "lost" by grave evil. *Social*: the quality of one's living conditions ("undignified" poverty judges the situation, not the person). *Existential*: one's experience of one's own life as dignified or not. Its use (2.1 Ex 2): it separates what Aquinas ran together, the grave sinner who "falls away from the dignity of his manhood" and may be killed "even as it is to kill a beast" (II-II q.64 a.2 ad 3), from his own image of nature "found in all men" (I q.93 a.4). The resolution is the declaration's distinction, not Aquinas's. Reaching from indelible dignity to the death penalty's being "inadmissible" (CCC 2267, revised 2018) took a further judgement about what punishment is for ([`moral-theology` 5.4](../moral-theology/lessons/05-04-the-death-penalty-and-ccc-2267.md)).

**Level:** the distinction is the declaration's analytic tool; the teaching it serves, indelible ontological dignity, is **rung 3**.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md)

### Christ the Image

The Son is the Image of the Father in the same nature; man is made "to" the image, and is restored and revealed in Christ.

Col 1:15 (DR): "the image of the invisible God, the firstborn of every creature"; 2 Cor 4:4; Heb 1:3; Rom 8:29, "conformable to the image of his Son". Aquinas: the Son is "the perfect Image", called Image and never "to the image"; man is both, in an alien nature, "as the image of the king is in a silver coin" (ST I q.93 a.1 ad 2). Image is the Son's proper name for the Latin Doctors (I q.35 a.2; [`trinity-and-god` 4.5](../trinity-and-god/lessons/04-05-common-proper-appropriated.md)). Irenaeus: man was made after the image of the Word, and the image went unshown until the Word became flesh (AH V.16.2); what we lost in Adam we recover in Christ (III.18.1). GS 22: Christ, the final Adam, "fully reveals man to man himself" and restores the likeness disfigured since the first sin (CCC 1701). Col 1:15-20 as a primacy text is [`christology` 1.3](../christology/lessons/01-03-why-the-incarnation.md)'s.

**Level:** Christ is the image of the invisible God: **rung 1** (Scripture, always proposed as revealed). He restores the image and reveals man to himself: **rung 3** (GS 22). Image as the Son's proper name: **rung 4**.

**Trap:** "image" meaning the same of Christ and of us. The Son *is* the Image; we are made *to* it, like the coin; grace makes us sharers in the divine nature, never one substance with God ([divinization](#divinization)).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md)

### Stewardship

Man's dominion over creation as a share in God's providence, exercised as God's steward and not as an arbitrary owner.

CCC 373: man and woman are to "subdue" the earth "as stewards of God", not by "arbitrary and destructive domination", sharing in his providence toward other creatures. GS 12: man set over earthly creatures. *Communion and Stewardship* (2004): rule as participation in the divine governance, "a form of stewardship" (§57); kingship as service (§59); the charge that the image doctrine caused the ecological crisis, answered (§§72-73); communion and stewardship "the two great strands" of the doctrine of the image (§25). 2.1 Ex 1: the image-bearer owes his old sheepdog a steward's care.

**Level:** as Church teaching, **rung 3** (CCC 373); the ITC's treatment, **no magisterial level**.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md)

### Soul as form of the body

The rational soul is itself, by its very substance, what makes this matter a living human body: not a passenger in it.

The soul clause of *Fidei catholicae* ([Council of Vienne](#council-of-vienne), 1312; lesson's translation of the Latin). First a reprobation (*reprobamus*): with the council's approval, the pope rejects as erroneous every position rashly asserting or doubting "that the substance of the rational or intellective soul is not truly and of itself (*vere ac per se*) the form of the human body". Then a definition (*definientes*), "so that the truth of the pure faith may be known to all": whoever henceforth obstinately asserts, defends or holds that the rational soul "is not the form of the human body of itself and essentially (*forma corporis humani per se et essentialiter*)" is to be regarded as a heretic. *Per se*: of itself, not through another form between soul and body. *Essentialiter*: by what it is, not by an operation, as a mover is joined to what it moves. *Substantia animae*: the soul itself, not a power or part of it. Absent: any name, argument, or word on other forms. Lateran V repeats the clause as Clement V's canon at Vienne (1513); GS 14: "Though made of body and soul, man is one"; CCC 365, citing Vienne, glosses "form": by the spiritual soul matter becomes a living human body, one nature, not two (its quotation marks around "form" mark a technical term, not a metaphor). The unity was confessed before Vienne said how: "the reasonable soul and flesh is one man" (Athanasian Creed, Schaff). Aquinas reverses the tenant's picture: spiritual things "contain those things in which they are; as the soul contains the body" (ST I q.8 a.1 ad 2).

**Excludes:** a soul joined to a body by operation, not essence (Plato's soul using a body, as Aquinas reports him, ST I q.75 a.4; Descartes's two complete substances); an intellect joined only through a sensory form (the view historians tie to [Peter John Olivi](#peter-john-olivi)); and, with *Firmiter*'s human "constituted of spirit and body" and Lateran V, a human with no spiritual soul.
