Pius IX applied it against Günther ([one principle of life](#one-principle-of-life)). **Leaves open:** whether the soul is the body's *only* substantial form ([unicity and plurality of forms](#unicity-and-plurality-of-forms)).

**Level (2.2 owns):** **rung 1**: an ecumenical council's act "with the approval of the sacred council", *definientes*, for "the truth of the pure faith", received by Lateran V. The heresy formula alone does not settle it: the same council *decreed* (*decernimus*) a heretic's punishment for denying that usury is sinful, which [`fundamental-theology` 5.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/05-05-the-loci-theologici.md) caps at "certain"; the verb, its stated object and the reception do. GS 14 and CCC 365's "one nature, not two": **at least rung 3** as worded.

**Uses:** why the body shares the image's dignity (CCC 364; 2.1); the premise of 2.3, since an understanding that is no bodily process can be neither generated nor corrupted; Aquinas's ground for rejecting [pre-existence](#pre-existence-of-souls) (q.118 a.3); and in 7.1 the ground of the resurrection's necessity and of the form-based reply to the [sameness objection](#sameness-objection).

**Traps:** "Vienne made Aquinas's psychology dogma" (unicity is rung 5), and the opposite error, demoting the definition for its scholastic vocabulary; "the soul sits in the brain" ([death](#death)); "Vienne defined the image" (it defined the form).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-body-and-soul-one-substance.md) (owner), [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-resurrection-of-the-body.md)

### Divinization

The graced creature is made a sharer in the divine nature, becoming by grace what the Son is by nature, never what God is by essence.

2 Pet 1:4 (DR): "partakers of the divine nature" (Greek *theias koinonoi physeos*; Vulgate *divinae consortes naturae*). Irenaeus: the Word became "what we are, that He might bring us to be even what He is Himself" (AH V pref.). Athanasius: "For He was made man that we might be made God" (*On the Incarnation* 54.3); we, servants by nature, "call Him by grace Father, who is by nature our Lord" (*Orations against the Arians* II.51). Aquinas: God alone deifies, "bestowing a partaking of the Divine Nature by a participated likeness" (ST I-II q.112 a.1). CCC 460 (citing 2 Pet 1:4, Irenaeus, Athanasius, Aquinas), 1988, 1999 ("deifying"), 2009; CCC 460 does not state the "by grace, not by nature" guardrail. In 2.1 the second grade of the image, grace, is this divinization, and *Dei Filius* can. 3 keeps "image" from meaning divine by nature.

**Level (4.3 owns):** partakers of the divine nature, sons by adoption: **rung 1** (Scripture; [adoptive sonship](#adoptive-sonship)). Never one substance with God: **rung 1** (*Dei Filius* can. 3, which condemns "the substance and essence of God and of all things is one and the same"); *Mystici Corporis* §78 applies it to mystical union (**rung 3**). Participation as "a participated likeness": **rung 4**. The Fathers as authors: **no magisterial level**, and CCC 460 quoting them adds none.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-the-image-of-god.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md) (owner)

### Council of Vienne

Clement V's council at Vienne (opened 16 October 1311, last session 6 May 1312), counted the fifteenth ecumenical council; its decree *Fidei catholicae* holds the Church's only definition of *how* soul and body are joined.

*Fidei catholicae*, handed down in the *Clementines* (the 1911 Denzinger heads it from their title *De summa Trinitate et fide catholica*), has three doctrinal parts, weighed one by one: (i) a confession (*confitemur*) that the Word assumed the parts of our nature united together, a passible body and an intellective soul truly informing it of itself and essentially: content **rung 1** through Chalcedon; with it, a declaration (*declaramus*) on the order of John's narrative of the lance-thrust; (ii) the soul clause, a reprobation plus a definition ([soul as form of the body](#soul-as-form-of-the-body)): **rung 1**; (iii) the baptism clause, which chose an opinion on infants' grace and virtues "as more probable" (*tanquam probabiliorem*): it **defines nothing** ([`sacraments-and-liturgy` 2.3](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/02-03-confirmation.md)). Never write "the decree is defined": say which clause. Transmission (Tanner's introduction, paraphrased): the acts are lost; the last session promulgated constitutions whose text and number we do not know; Clement V and then John XXII corrected them before their publication in 1317; which are the council's own is uncertain. The soul clause's standing rests on its "with the approval of the sacred council" and on Lateran V's citing it as Clement V's canon. The same council's usury decree *Ex gravi ad nos* "decrees" (*decernimus*) a heretic's punishment for obstinately denying that usury is sinful, a claim [`fundamental-theology` 5.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/05-05-the-loci-theologici.md) and [`theology-of-debt` 5.4](../theology-of-debt/lessons/05-04-did-the-usury-doctrine-change.md) cap at "certain".

**Traps:** "Vienne condemned Olivi by name" (it names no one); "the whole decree is defined".

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-body-and-soul-one-substance.md) (owner), [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md)

### Peter John Olivi

Franciscan theologian of Languedoc (about 1248-1298) whose view of the soul historians match to Vienne's condemned proposition.

His position (questions on *Sentences* II, q.51; paraphrased from Juhana Toivanen and Robert Pasnau, *Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy*): intellect and will must be spiritual and incorporeal, or they could not be immortal, intellectual and free, and no form of corporeal matter is that; so the soul has its own spiritual matter and several forms; its sensory and nutritive part informs the body, and its intellective part is joined to the body substantially through that part, not as form to matter; the whole soul may be called the body's form, as we say a person talks, not a tongue; to call the intellective part itself the body's form is "not only contrary to reason but also dangerous to the faith" (their rendering). One soul with parts, not "two souls". The decree names no one: the 1911 Denzinger's heading "Errors of Peter John Olivi" is an editor's, and Toivanen and Pasnau find his view "far more nuanced" than the decree's wording. The Spiritual Franciscans are [`church-history` 5.2](../church-history/lessons/05-02-friars-and-universities.md)'s.

**Level:** "Vienne condemned Olivi" is a historians' judgment, **no level**, and **not a [dogmatic fact](#dogmatic-fact)**: no act attributes the condemned sense to him, as one did to Jansen (5.6). Vienne binds a conclusion, not an argument. Olivi and Aquinas as authors: **no magisterial level**.

**Trap:** "Olivi taught two souls".

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-body-and-soul-one-substance.md)

### Apostolici regiminis

Leo X's bull at the Fifth Lateran Council (Session VIII, 19 December 1513) on the soul and on philosophy.

Read as Source in 2.3 (lesson's translation of Denzinger's excerpt). Two errors are named, "that it is mortal, or one in all human beings", and a stance: some assert this "true at least according to philosophy" (*secundum saltem philosophiam*). "With the approval of this sacred council we condemn and reprobate" all who assert the intellective soul mortal or one in all, and those who doubt it; the doctrine comes in the *since* clause: the soul is not only "truly, of itself and essentially" the form of the body (Vienne's canon, cited as Clement V's) but also immortal (*immortalis*) and "individually multipliable (*singulariter multiplicabilis*) according to the number of bodies into which it is infused". The one *definimus* falls on the logic: "since truth in no way contradicts truth, we define that every assertion contrary to the truth of enlightened faith is altogether false". A penal clause follows, then provisions Denzinger omits: philosophy teachers must make the faith's truth plain and answer contrary arguments, and clerics may not study philosophy past five years without theology or canon law. The bull names no one, and came three years before Pomponazzi's book ([mortalism](#mortalism)). 2.2: its repeating Vienne's clause is the reception that confirms Vienne's text as conciliar, the acts being lost.

**Level (2.3 owns):** immortality and one soul for each body: **rung 1**: an ecumenical council condemns the contrary as heresy, on a truth of faith (unlike the *monti* of [`theology-of-debt` 5.1](../theology-of-debt/lessons/05-01-the-monti-di-pieta-and-lateran-v.md)), and the CDF's 1998 commentary §11 classes immortality as revealed; the verb is "condemn and reprobate", not "define". The two-truths claim: its denial **rung 1**, by the *definimus*, repeated word for word in *Dei Filius* ch. 4 ([double truth](#double-truth)). The rules for teachers: **law, no rung**.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-body-and-soul-one-substance.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md) (owner)

### Unicity and plurality of forms

Whether the rational soul is the body's only substantial form (Aquinas), or there is also a lower form beneath it, such as a form of corporeity (Franciscans, Scotus and his school).

Vienne's *per se* excludes a soul that informs *through* another form; it does not say no other form exists. Aquinas held the rational soul the body's only substantial form (ST I q.76 a.4), "virtually containing" the sensitive and nutritive souls, and sharing one act of existing with the body (q.76 a.1 ad 5). Scotus (died 1308) and his school held a form of corporeity beneath the soul, which keeps the corpse the same body, uncensured after 1312. Pius IX's [one principle of life](#one-principle-of-life) (1860) excludes a second life-principle, and a form of corporeity is no life-principle (the lesson's reading of the letter's object). The Twenty-Four Thomistic Theses (Sacred Congregation of Studies, 27 July 1914) state unicity as thesis 16; in 1916 they were proposed as safe directive norms ([`thomistic-synthesis` 6.2](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/06-02-leo-xiii-and-the-thomist-revival.md)), which bind no one's assent.

**Level:** **rung 5**, freely disputed; so are virtual containment, one act of existing, and spiritual matter.

**Trap:** "Vienne made Aquinas's unicity dogma".

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-body-and-soul-one-substance.md)

### One principle of life

Pius IX's two letters applying Vienne to nineteenth-century dualisms: the rational soul is the body's form and its only life-principle.

*Eximiam tuam* (brief, 15 June 1857, to Cardinal von Geissel, archbishop of Cologne), after Anton Günther's works were put on the Index (decree of 8 January 1857; he submitted): Günther made man a synthesis of spirit and a natural soul, and his books injure the Catholic doctrine that the rational soul is "the true, per se and immediate form of the body" (lesson's translation; note the added word *immediata*). *Dolore haud mediocri* (30 April 1860, to the bishop of Breslau), against Johann Baptist Baltzer, a Güntherian who gave the body a life-principle of its own and called the contrary heretical: the opinion placing one principle of life in man, the rational soul, "seems so joined with the Church's dogma that it is its legitimate and only true interpretation" (lesson's translation), and so cannot be denied without error in faith.

**Level:** both letters **rung 3** (papal letters, each to one bishop); the second's *videri* ("seems") is a reason to default down. A form of corporeity is no life-principle, so the 1860 letter does not close the [unicity](#unicity-and-plurality-of-forms) dispute.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-body-and-soul-one-substance.md)

### Separated soul

The soul between death and resurrection, existing without its body: what remains of a human being, not a human being.

The Thomist account (2.3): it keeps "a natural inclination to be united to the body" (ST I q.76 a.1 ad 6) and is no person, being part of the human species (q.75 a.4 ad 2); subsistent, but not complete in species (q.75 a.2 ad 1): the schools' "incomplete substance". It cannot be called an individual substance, "as neither can the hand nor any other part of man" (I q.29 a.1 ad 5; 7.1). "My soul is not I" (*anima mea non est ego*; *Super I Cor.* 15, lect. 2): hence the [resurrection of the body](#resurrection-of-the-body). The bodiless state is unnatural, the result of the body's corruption, itself a result of sin (q.118 a.3 ad 3). The doctrine: the CDF's *Letter on Certain Questions Concerning Eschatology* (17 May 1979, approved by John Paul II and ordered published), point 3: a spiritual element endowed with consciousness and will survives and subsists after death, so that the "human self" subsists; the Church keeps the word "soul". In 6.1 this surviving self is what is judged at death ([intermediate state](#intermediate-state)).

**Level:** a conscious self survives and subsists: **rung 3** (CDF 1979, ordinary papal approval), with the soul's immortality, **rung 1**, beneath it. An incomplete substance, not a person: **rung 4**. Whether *I* survive in it (survivalism against corruptionism, [`metaphysics` 6.5](../metaphysics/lessons/06-05-what-a-person-is.md)): **rung 5**, which no doctrine decides.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-body-and-soul-one-substance.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md) (owner), [6.1](lessons/06-01-death-and-the-particular-judgment.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-resurrection-of-the-body.md)

### Resurrection of the body

At Christ's coming all the dead, just and unjust, rise in their own bodies, reunited to their souls and changed.

The creeds: "the resurrection of the body [flesh]" (Apostles' Creed, Schaff), "of the dead" (Nicene); the Athanasian Creed: at his coming "all men shall rise again with their bodies". *Firmiter* (lesson's translation): Christ "will come at the end of the age", and all, "the reprobate and the elect alike", "will rise with their own bodies which they now bear" (*qui omnes cum suis propriis resurgent corporibus, quae nunc gestant*). Why the creed says "flesh": the soul does not die and so cannot rise (Roman Catechism, Article XI). Why it is necessary, not decorative (2.2): the separated soul is what remains of a man, not a man; Aquinas on 1 Cor 15:19, "my soul is not I; so even if the soul attains salvation in another life, still not I, nor any man" (*Super I Cor.* 15, lect. 2, lesson's translation; a theologian's reading, **no magisterial level**). The CDF's 1979 letter: the resurrection of the whole person (point 2); Christ's glorious manifestation distinct and deferred with respect to the situation right after death (point 5).

**Level (7.1 owns):** that the dead rise with their own bodies, and just and unjust alike: **rung 1** (*Firmiter*'s *qui omnes*; the Athanasian Creed). The whole person rises, not the soul alone: **rung 1**. It waits for Christ's coming and is not each person's at death: **at least rung 3** (1979, points 5-6), rung 1 arguable. The identical particles are not required: **rung 3** (DDF reply, 2023). The just rise glorified: **at least rung 3**; the four gifts, **rung 4** ([glorified body](#glorified-body)). What makes it the same body: **rung 5** ([sameness objection](#sameness-objection)). Burial and cremation: [cremation and burial](#cremation-and-burial), **law, no rung**.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-body-and-soul-one-substance.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-resurrection-of-the-body.md) (owner)

### Death

The separation of the soul from the body: natural to an animal body, and for this person the penalty of sin.

CCC 997 and 1005: in death the soul leaves the body, to be reunited with it at the resurrection; CCC 1006: in a sense bodily death is natural, but for faith it is "the wages of sin"; CCC 1008. Scripture (DR): "God made not death" (Wis 1:13); by the devil's envy "death came into the world" (Wis 2:24); "by sin death" (Rom 5:12); "It is appointed unto men once to die" (Heb 9:27). Trent V cans. 1-2: Adam incurred "death, with which God had previously threatened him", and passed on not death alone but sin; Carthage 418 can. 1 excludes death "by natural necessity". Human nature is mortal; the first man was able not to die only by a gift ([immunity from death](#immunity-from-death)). Vienne makes death a substantial change, the form's departure, but supplies no empirical sign (2.2). John Paul II, to the Transplantation Society (29 August 2000, §§4-5, paraphrased): death, the separation of the life-principle or soul from the body, is an event no scientific method identifies directly, only its signs; "the Church does not make technical decisions"; and the irreversible cessation of all brain activity, rigorously applied, does not seem to conflict with a sound anthropology (2.2 Ex 2).

**Level:** death as the soul's separation from the body: **at least rung 3** (CCC 1005; 2.2). Death the penalty of sin, not natural necessity: **rung 1** (Carthage 418 can. 1; Trent V cans. 1-2; 6.1 owns). Immortality not owed to nature: **rung 3**, the contrary of Baius's prop. 78, censured *in globo* (3.1). The 2000 address: rung 3 at its lightest. Whether the neurological criterion tracks the organism's death: **no level**, argued among physicians and philosophers, Catholics included.

**Trap:** "the soul sits in the brain, so brain death is the soul leaving the head": it informs every living part; the brain is evidence of the organism's integration, not the soul's address.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-body-and-soul-one-substance.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-death-and-the-particular-judgment.md) (owner)

### Original sin

The lack of the holiness and justice God meant for the whole race, contracted by every child of Adam by descent, and committed by no one but Adam.

[Trent Session V](#trent-session-v) (17 June 1546; Waterworth): Adam's sin passed to the race not only death and pains of the body but "sin also, which is the death of the soul" (can. 2); a sin "which in its origin is one, and being transfused into all by propagation, not by imitation, is in each one as his own" (can. 3); infants "contracted" it "by generation" (can. 4); its guilt is remitted in baptism, and [concupiscence](#concupiscence) in the reborn is not truly and properly sin (can. 5). Trent VI ch. 3: by propagation men "contract through him, when they are conceived, injustice as their own". The earlier acts: [Carthage 418](#council-of-carthage-418) can. 2 and [Orange II](#orange-ii) cans. 1-2, which Trent V reuses (3.2). CCC 404-405 (paraphrased): "sin" only analogically; contracted, not committed; a state, not an act; proper to each; a deprivation of original holiness and justice; the transmission a mystery we cannot fully understand. Aquinas: "original sin is concupiscence, materially, but privation of original justice, formally" (ST I-II q.82 a.3). What was had and lost is 3.1's ([original justice](#original-justice); [preternatural gifts](#preternatural-gifts)). 2.3: [traducianism](#traducianism) would explain it at a stroke, and is excluded. 7.4: it is the second premise of the limbo problem, and its penalty is the lack of the vision.

**Level (3.3 owns):** sin, not only death, passes from Adam to all; by propagation, not imitation; in each as his own: **rung 1** (cans. 2-4). Adam lost holiness and justice for us also: **rung 1** (cans. 1-2). "Injustice as their own": **at least rung 3** (VI ch. 3). Its formal essence, the privation of original justice: **rung 4**. Its mechanism: **rung 5** ([transmission of original sin](#transmission-of-original-sin)). The modern pressures, polygenism and the situational reinterpretations, are 3.4's ([monogenism and polygenism](#monogenism-and-polygenism); [sin of the world](#sin-of-the-world)).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md) (owner), [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md), [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

### Traducianism

The view that the soul is handed on from the parents with the body (*tradux*, a vine-shoot).

Tertullian, *On the Soul* (early third century; tr. Holmes, ANF 3): the soul a subtle body, sown with the flesh (ch. 27); "Every soul, then, by reason of its birth, has its nature in Adam until it is born again in Christ" (ch. 40). Its power: a shoot of Adam's soul would carry his sin as a branch carries sap. Augustine, who rejected a bodily soul, never decided: in 415 he pressed Jerome, who held that God makes each soul at birth, on where infants' sin could then be committed (Letter 166 §10); four years on, Scripture still did not tell him "whether by inbreathing them as new, or by deriving them from the parents" (*On the Soul and its Origin* IV.15); his *Retractations* (II.45) note the letter gave no answer of his own. Anastasius II (498) rejected the claim that parents pass on the soul with the body. Aquinas calls the view "heretical" (ST I q.118 a.2): a theologian's censure, **no magisterial level**.

**Level:** excluded with the [immediate creation of the soul](#immediate-creation-of-the-soul), **at least rung 3**; bodily traducianism also contradicts the soul's spirituality. Its failure hands 3.3 the transmission problem.

**Trap:** "Augustine taught traducianism" (he never decided).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md)

### Time of ensoulment

The question at what moment in development God creates and infuses the rational soul: left open.

Aquinas put the soul's creation "at the end of human generation" (ST I q.118 a.2 ad 2), after nutritive and sensitive stages. The question is left unresolved by the Magisterium ([`moral-theology` 5.2](../moral-theology/lessons/05-02-abortion.md), on the CDF's 1974 Declaration on Procured Abortion; its card has delayed animation). Vienne says what ensoulment *is*, not *when* (2.2).

**Level:** **rung 5**.

**Keep apart:** "immediately" excludes created causes, not a moment. "The Church teaches that the soul is created at conception" overstates.

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md)

### Origenist anathemas

The two sixth-century sets of condemnations of Origenism: Justinian's nine canons of 543, and the fifteen anathemas associated with Constantinople II (553).

543: Justinian's edict against Origen, addressed to Patriarch Menas and endorsed by the home synod of Constantinople: nine canons plus an anathema on Origen. Can. 1 anathematizes the Origenist claim that souls pre-existed, grew sated with God and were embodied as punishment (2.3); can. 9, a punishment "of demons and of impious men" that is only temporary, and their restoration ([apokatastasis](#apokatastasis); 1.3, 6.4). 553: Constantinople II, opened 5 May 553 over the Three Chapters, names Origen in its list of heretics (capitulum 11, whose text some suspect of insertion and others defend). The fifteen condemn "the fabulous pre-existence of souls" and "the monstrous restoration which follows from it": the bodiless minds of sixth-century monastic Origenism. Who passed them is **contested**: the council, the synod of 543 (Hefele), or the bishops gathered before the opening. For the council: a manuscript heading, the historian Evagrius, later literature; against: nothing about Origen in the council's summons or letters. Percival judged a dogmatic answer "the height of rashness". The fifteen neither name nor describe Gregory of Nyssa. Weigh them as [`patristics` 2.5](../patristics/lessons/02-05-clement-of-alexandria-and-origen.md) does.

**Level (6.4 owns):** the 543 canons, an emperor's edict endorsed by a local synod: **no conciliar rung of their own**. The fifteen, of uncertain authorship: **add no level** (can. 749 §3, default down). The propositions are excluded anyway: the eternity of punishment **rung 1** by *Firmiter* ([hell](#hell)); pre-existence, its denial **at least rung 3** ([pre-existence of souls](#pre-existence-of-souls)).

**Trap:** grounding hell's eternity, or the rejection of pre-existence, in 553.

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md) (owner)

### Generationism

The view that the child's soul is produced from the parents' souls by a spiritual generation, not with the bodily seed.

It fails because a spiritual substance has no part to give off, and "cannot be caused through generation, but only through creation by God" (ST I q.118 a.2). Anastasius II (498) had said that what parents hand on *per traducem* is guilt and penalty (*culpa poenaque peccati*), not the soul.

**Level:** excluded with the [immediate creation of the soul](#immediate-creation-of-the-soul), **at least rung 3**; that no spirit can be generated, **rung 4**.

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md)

### Trent Session V

The Council of Trent's decree on original sin (17 June 1546): a preamble, five numbered paragraphs each a canon with its reasons, and a closing paragraph on Mary; no chapters.

Text: Waterworth's translation (1848), as Schaff prints it beside the Latin (*Creeds* vol. 2). The preamble: the council "ordains, confesses, and declares". Can. 1: Adam "immediately lost the holiness and justice wherein he had been constituted" (*constitutus*, not *creatus*: [constitutus not creatus](#constitutus-not-creatus)), incurred "death, with which God had previously threatened him" and captivity to the devil, and "the entire Adam ... was changed, in body and soul, for the worse" (from Orange II can. 1). Can. 2: condemns saying his sin injured himself alone, or passed on only death and pains of the body, "but not sin also, which is the death of the soul" (Orange II can. 2, with "pains" added). Can. 3: the sin "in its origin is one", "transfused into all by propagation, not by imitation", "in each one as his own", taken away by no remedy but the merit of the one mediator, applied in baptism to adults and infants. Can. 4: infants, "even though they be sprung from baptized parents", are to be baptized, so that what "they have contracted by generation" may be cleansed by regeneration (Carthage 418 can. 2, enlarged with "from a tradition of the apostles"). Can. 5: the guilt of original sin is remitted; [concupiscence](#concupiscence) remains, not truly and properly sin in the reborn, "but because it is of sin, and inclines to sin"; the declaration closes with its own anathema, which Schaff prints and the Hanover web copy of Waterworth drops. The closing paragraph says it is not the council's intention to include the Blessed Virgin ([`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 6.3](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/06-03-the-immaculate-conception.md)). New text: cans. 3 and 5. 3.4: the canons' subject is "the first man, Adam", singular, and none defines monogenism as such. 5.2 measures the Joint Declaration's *simul* against can. 5.

**Level (3.3 owns):** what each canon condemns has its contradictory at **rung 1**; the "for" and "whereas" reasons after it are conciliar teaching, not each a definition (can. 749 §3; [chapters and canons](#chapters-and-canons)). Can. 3's description of "this sin": **rung 1**. The concupiscence declaration: **rung 1** for its contrary; its reason, "of sin, and inclines to sin": **at least rung 3** (the footing 3.1 gives integrity). The Marian paragraph states an intention. The mechanism of transmission is fixed by no clause ([transmission of original sin](#transmission-of-original-sin)).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md) (owner), [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)

### Propagation, not imitation

Original sin passes to each human being by descent from Adam, not by copying his example.

Trent V can. 3: *propagatione, non imitatione transfusum omnibus, inest unicuique proprium*; can. 4: "contracted by generation". The imitation view is the Pelagian one (3.2); the phrase came through Orange from Augustine's anti-Pelagian writings, aimed at the bad-example account. Paul VI's *Credo of the People of God* (1968) §16 restates it "with the Council of Trent". "Propagation" names the route, descent, not a mechanism, and not genetics: the subject of original sin is the soul (ST I-II q.83 a.1). With each soul created immediately by God, the sin cannot travel with the soul (2.3). 3.3 Ex 2 strains it with Aquinas's own case, a human formed by God from human flesh, who "would not contract original sin" (q.81 a.4): no clause says what propagation consists in. 3.4 uses it as the test for the situational reinterpretations.

**Level:** **rung 1** (Trent V can. 3, with cans. 2 and 4). How it reaches a created soul: **rung 5**.

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md) (owner), [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md)

### Transmission of original sin

How descent from Adam passes on a lack in each soul: never defined, and answered differently by the schools.

No clause of Session V states the mechanism: "propagation", "generation" and "contracted" name the fact and the route. The openness is by the decree's **silence**, not by a clause leaving it open. The schools: Augustine, concupiscence in the act of generation passing "the bond of sin" by natural birth (*On Marriage and Concupiscence* I.23.25, I.24.27), while conceding "it is no easy matter to unravel this intricate point" (I.19.21); Aquinas, "all men born of Adam may be considered as one man", whom Adam moves by the movement of generation as the will moves the members (ST I-II q.81 a.1), an explanation that presupposes descent from one first parent; original sin in Adam as principal cause, in the semen as instrumental cause, in the soul as subject (q.83 a.1); Catharinus and Pighius, Adam's own act imputed ([imputation theory of original sin](#imputation-theory-of-original-sin)); Rahner's co-determination by guilt and Schoonenberg's being-situated (3.4; [situation of guilt](#situation-of-guilt)). CCC 404: a mystery we cannot fully understand. [Traducianism](#traducianism) was the cheap explanation, and the immediate creation of each soul excludes it (2.3). 4.2 meets the question again over genetics ([genealogical and genetic ancestry](#genealogical-and-genetic-ancestry)).

**Level:** every mechanism **rung 5**.

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md) (owner), [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md)

### Monopsychism

The view that there is one intellect, or one intellective soul, for all human beings.

Averroes's reading of *De anima* III: one separate receiving intellect for all ([`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 3.5](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/03-05-soul-and-intellect.md); the Paris quarrel is [`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 7.1](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/07-01-faith-reason-and-the-paris-crisis.md)'s). Lateran V condemns asserting the intellective soul "one in all human beings": it is "individually multipliable ... according to the number of bodies into which it is infused". My understanding is *my* act, not a share in one world-mind. The bull names no one; "monopsychism" is the historians' word.

**Level:** condemned; its denial, one soul for each body, **rung 1** ([Apostolici regiminis](#apostolici-regiminis)).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md)

### Mortalism

The view that the human soul perishes with the body ("Alexandrism").

After Alexander of Aphrodisias (about 200): the soul the perishable form of a body, the undying intellect God's alone. Pietro Pomponazzi, *Tractatus de immortalitate animae* (Bologna, 1516): on Aristotle's principles the soul is mortal, immortal only in a qualified sense; he held immortality by faith. Lateran V condemned asserting the soul mortal, even "at least according to philosophy", in 1513, **three years before** the book: it struck positions already argued in Italian universities, and names no one. Its denial is the [immortality of the soul](#immortality-of-the-soul).

**Level:** condemned; its denial **rung 1**.

**Trap:** "Lateran V condemned Pomponazzi's book".

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md)

### Immortality of the soul

The human soul does not perish when the body dies: immortal by nature, and held in being by God.

Lateran V, [Apostolici regiminis](#apostolici-regiminis) (1513): the soul is "immortal (*immortalis*), and individually multipliable", in the *since* clause grounding the condemnation of all who assert it mortal. The CDF's *Doctrinal Commentary* on the concluding formula of the *Professio fidei* (29 June 1998, §11) lists "the doctrine on the immortality of the spiritual soul" among truths to be believed as divinely revealed. Also Paul VI's *Credo* (1968) §8; CCC 366. Aquinas: "It is impossible for a subsistent form to cease to exist" (ST I q.75 a.6), and reduction to nothing would imply "not in the creature a potentiality to non-existence, but in the Creator the power of ceasing to sustain existence" (ad 2). So it is natural, **not** a [preternatural gift](#preternatural-gifts) like the bodily [immunity from death](#immunity-from-death) Adam lost, and not the independent title of the *Phaedo*'s soul, deathless as kin to the divine and pre-existent ([`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 2.3](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/02-03-the-soul-its-parts-and-immortality.md)). 6.1: the floor under the particular judgment; 6.4: the answer to conditional immortality ([annihilationism](#annihilationism)).

**Level (2.3 owns):** **rung 1** (Lateran V's conciliar condemnation as heresy, on a truth of faith; the CDF's 1998 classing). Whether reason proves it (Aquinas) or cannot (Scotus; Cajetan doubted Aristotle proves it): **rung 5**. The theses put to Augustin Bonnetty (1855) claim proof of the soul's *spirituality*, not its immortality.

**Keep apart:** holding the conclusion while finding no proof that works is free (2.3 Ex 2); asserting that philosophy shows the soul mortal is what the *definimus* calls false. "A gift of the creator" means created and conserved, as all existence is, not supernatural.

**Trap:** "the soul is immortal because it is divine, a spark of God": Leo IX professed it "not a part of God but created from nothing".

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md) (owner), [6.1](lessons/06-01-death-and-the-particular-judgment.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md)

### Preternatural gifts

Gifts beyond what human nature can claim that perfect it in its own order (unlike grace, which lifts it above its order): integrity, freedom from death, freedom from suffering, and infused knowledge.

The term and the list of four are the manuals' (Pohle-Preuss, 1912); Scripture never names a preternatural gift. On 3.1's account the first man was given [original justice](#original-justice), whose root is grace; the gifts hang from that root, since they depend on reason's subjection to God (ST I q.95 a.1), and so fell together with it: the traveller "was stripped of his gifts, and wounded in his nature" ([stripped and wounded](#stripped-and-wounded)). None was owed: Pius V's *Ex omnibus afflictionibus* (1 October 1567) condemned Baius's props. 26 (integrity "its natural condition") and 78 (immortality "a natural condition"). 2.3: the soul's immortality is **not** one of them. 6.1: human nature is mortal; the first man was able not to die only by a gift.

| Gift (3.1 decides) | Level |
|---|---|
| freedom from death: that death came as the penalty of sin ([immunity from death](#immunity-from-death)) | **rung 1** (Carthage 418 can. 1; Trent V can. 1) |
| integrity, desire held under reason ([gift of integrity](#gift-of-integrity)) | **at least rung 3** (inferred from Trent V can. 5's "of sin"; presupposed in 1567; CCC 377) |
| freedom from suffering: that suffering, like death, entered by sin | **at least rung 3**; what impassibility consisted in, **rung 4** |
| infused knowledge (ST I q.94 a.3) | **rung 4 at most**, no act behind it; its extent, **rung 5** |
| integrity and immortality not owed to nature | **rung 3**: the contraries of propositions censured [in globo](#in-globo-censure) (Pius V, props. 26 and 78), with no precise note (3.1's table writes "at least rung 3") |

Pohle-Preuss's notes ("strictly of faith", *fidei proximum*) are a manual's: **no magisterial level**.

**Trap:** "the preternatural gifts are all dogma, or all decoration".

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md) (owner), [6.1](lessons/06-01-death-and-the-particular-judgment.md)

### Immunity from death

The first man was able not to die: had he not sinned he would not have died, not because his body could not die but because God kept it.

Carthage (1 May 418) can. 1 (African Code can. 109 in its Latin numbering; Percival) condemns saying that Adam "was created mortal, so that whether he had sinned or not, he would have died in body ... by natural necessity". Trent V can. 1: he incurred "death, with which God had previously threatened him" (Gen 2:17). Augustine: "the first immortality was to be able not to die" (*Rebuke and Grace* 12.33), the last not to be able to die. Aquinas: the body was kept "by reason of a supernatural force given by God to the soul" (ST I q.97 a.1), not by any vigour of its own. Not natural: Baius's prop. 78, that the first man's immortality was a natural condition, was condemned (1567). 2.3 sets it against the soul's immortality: the soul's is natural to a subsistent form; the body's in paradise was an added gift, lost by sin.

**Level (3.1 owns):** death the penalty of sin, not natural necessity: **rung 1** (Carthage 418 can. 1, by Zosimus's confirmation and its reception, 3.2; Trent V can. 1). Not owed to nature: **rung 3**, the contrary of Baius's prop. 78, censured *in globo* (3.1's table writes "at least rung 3"). How the body was kept: the schools' (I q.97 a.1).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md) (owner)

### Double truth

The charge that someone holds a proposition true in philosophy while faith teaches its contrary.

A charge, not a school: no Latin master is known to have taught a "double truth" as a doctrine ([`ancient-medieval-philosophy` 7.1](../ancient-medieval-philosophy/lessons/07-01-faith-reason-and-the-paris-crisis.md)). Lateran V names the stance, "true at least according to philosophy" (*secundum saltem philosophiam*), and defines: "since truth in no way contradicts truth, we define that every assertion contrary to the truth of enlightened faith is altogether false". *Dei Filius* ch. 4 (1870) repeats the sentence word for word, again "we define" (Schaff: "utterly false"). The bull took from philosophers the leave to call a conclusion true in philosophy while the faith taught its opposite. What it does not forbid: saying that no proof of a revealed conclusion succeeds (Scotus on immortality; 2.3 Ex 2). It binds conclusion and logic, not arguments.

**Level:** the two-truths claim is condemned; its denial **rung 1** (Lateran V's *definimus*, repeated in *Dei Filius* ch. 4).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md)

### Pre-existence of souls

The view that souls exist before their bodies: in the Origenist form, minds sated with God, cooled, and embodied as a punishment.

The acts: Justinian's edict of 543, endorsed by a synod at Constantinople, can. 1 ([Origenist anathemas](#origenist-anathemas)): a local act. The fifteen anathemas associated with 553, no. I, against "the fabulous pre-existence of souls": standing disputed ([`patristics` 2.5](../patristics/lessons/02-05-clement-of-alexandria-and-origen.md); 6.4 owns 553). Braga I (561) can. 6, against souls that sinned in a heavenly dwelling and were cast into bodies ([Priscillianists](#priscillianists)): local. The condemned Origenism is sixth-century monastic Origenism ([`patristics` 2.5](../patristics/lessons/02-05-clement-of-alexandria-and-origen.md)). The rejection follows from Vienne: Aquinas argues that if the union with the body were accidental, man would be a being by accident; that the human soul understands through the senses; and that the soul is without its body only through the body's corruption, a result of sin (ST I q.118 a.3 and ad 3). Embodiment is no penalty, and the soul begins with its body.

**Level (2.3 decides):** its denial **at least rung 3**, by default down (543 and Braga I local, 553 contested), though the rejection has been taught universally since and follows from Vienne (**rung 4** as an inference); rung 1 by the ordinary universal magisterium only if argued.

**Trap:** treating the 553 anathemas as the defining act against pre-existence.

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md)

**Original sin (Module 3)**

### Original justice

The state in which the first man was constituted: grace at the root, with integrity, freedom from death, freedom from suffering and (for the schools) infused knowledge hanging from it, given for the whole race and owed to none.

Trent V can. 1 (17 June 1546): Adam "immediately lost the holiness and justice wherein he had been constituted" (Waterworth); can. 2 calls it "received of God" and condemns saying he lost it "for himself alone, and not for us also". CCC 375 keeps "constituted" and calls this holiness a share in divine life; CCC 374 makes it a state surpassed only by the glory of the new creation, so not glory itself; CCC 388 (paraphrased): we know Adam as the source of sin only by knowing Christ as the source of grace. Aquinas, ST I q.95 a.1 (EDP): the first rectitude was reason subject to God, the lower powers to reason, the body to the soul; the first subjection caused the other two; since the lower two were lost by sin they were not from nature, so the root was "a supernatural endowment of grace". His own hedge: the rectitude "seems to require" creation in grace, "as others say".

**Level:** that Adam had it, received it from God, and lost it for himself and for us, **rung 1** (Trent V cann. 1-2). That the holiness was supernatural grace, **at least rung 3** (Pius V against [Baius](#baius) props. 21 and 23; CCC 375). Whether it was given at the first instant, **rung 5** ([constitutus, not creatus](#constitutus-not-creatus)). What hung from it: [preternatural gifts](#preternatural-gifts), [gift of integrity](#gift-of-integrity), [immunity from death](#immunity-from-death).

**Does not assert:** that Adam was created in glory or as a finished sage. Irenaeus's infant Adam (*Against Heresies* IV.38.1) and Aquinas's Adam made "in his perfect state" (I q.94 a.3) both keep the defined line, a holiness had and lost; choosing between the portraits is **rung 5** ([3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md) Ex 2). "First parents" (plural) is CCC 390's and the *Credo*'s wording; Trent's canons say "the first man, Adam" ([3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md)).

**Its lack is original sin:** the holiness and justice meant for each child of Adam and missing in each as his own ([original sin](#original-sin); [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md)).

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md)

### Constitutus, not creatus

Trent says Adam lost the holiness and justice in which he "had been constituted", not "created", so as not to decide whether he had grace from the first instant of his existence.

Trent V can. 1: *sanctitatem et iustitiam, in qua constitutus fuerat*. The drafting fact rests on Pohle-Preuss, *God the Author of Nature and the Supernatural* (1912), who, citing Pallavicino's history of the council (not checked directly), report that the draft read "created" (*in iustitia creatus*) and that the council substituted "constituted" to avoid the controversy. The parties: the Thomists, created in grace (ST I q.95 a.1, offered as what the first rectitude "seems to require"), against Peter Lombard, Bonaventure and the Scotists (with Hugh of St Victor, on Pohle's account), created in natural gifts with grace given afterward. CCC 375 also says "constituted".

**Level:** that Adam was constituted in holiness and justice and lost it, **rung 1** (can. 1); created in grace or given it later, **rung 5**, deliberately left open.

**Does not assert:** "Trent defined that Adam was created in grace" overstates the canon; Aquinas's "seems to require" is the better opinion, not faith.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md)

### Gift of integrity

Before sin the first man's desire was wholly under reason, as reason was under God: no disordered concupiscence.

Inferred, not stated. Trent V can. 5 says Paul calls [concupiscence](#concupiscence) sin, not as truly sin in the reborn, "but because it is of sin, and inclines to sin" (Waterworth): if disordered desire is *of* sin, the first man before sin was without it. Can. 1, borrowing [Orange II](#orange-ii) can. 1, has "the entire Adam ... changed, in body and soul, for the worse". Genesis 2:25 (naked "and were not ashamed") and 3:7 (the aprons), DR. CCC 377: free of the triple concupiscence (1 John 2:16). Aquinas: integrity hung on reason's subjection to God, so it fell with grace (I q.95 a.1). The manuals' Latin: *donum integritatis*.

**Level:** **at least rung 3**, not rung 1: an inference from the council's reason clause is not itself a definition, and [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md) puts that reason at at least rung 3 (the penal clause manifestly reaches only the declaration's contrary). Presupposed by Pius V's condemnation of [Baius](#baius) prop. 26 (1567), which also fixes its gratuity: the contrary of a censure *in globo* is **rung 3**, with no precise note ([in globo censure](#in-globo-censure)).

**The neighbouring gifts** ([preternatural gifts](#preternatural-gifts)): that suffering, like death, entered by sin, **at least rung 3** (Trent V can. 2 presupposes that "pains of the body" passed from Adam; *Credo of the People of God* §16; CCC 376); what impassibility consisted in, **rung 4** (I q.97 a.2). Infused knowledge, **rung 4 at most**, its extent **rung 5** (I q.94 a.3). A manual's "doctrina catholica" or *fidei proximum* (Pohle-Preuss) is not a rung.

**Twin:** Christ had no *fomes* ([`christology` 3.4](../christology/lessons/03-04-the-grace-of-the-head.md)) and still suffered and died, so sinlessness does not entail exemption from death.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md)

### Stripped and wounded

The scholastic summary of the fall's damage: the gratuitous gifts lost entire, the natural goods kept but hurt.

The text is a gloss, not a council. ST I-II q.85 a.1 *sed contra* (EDP): the traveller of Luke 10:30 "was stripped of his gifts, and wounded in his nature" (*expoliatur gratuitis et vulneratur in naturalibus*). Aquinas credits Bede; the English Dominicans' note traces it to the *Glossa Ordinaria*; Bede's own commentary was not checked. The often-quoted *spoliatus gratuitis, vulneratus in naturalibus* is a later summary, not Aquinas's wording. The *respondeo* sorts three goods of nature: its principles and powers, "neither destroyed nor diminished"; the inclination to virtue, diminished; original justice, "entirely destroyed". I-II q.85 a.3 adds the four wounds (ignorance, malice, weakness, concupiscence). Paul VI's *Credo of the People of God* (1968) §16 restates the gloss in its own words (paraphrase: nature stripped of the grace that clothed it, injured in its natural powers).

**Defined nearby:** free will, "attenuated as it was in its powers, and bent down, was by no means extinguished" (Trent VI ch. 1, Waterworth, reusing Orange II's "bent and weakened"); can. 5 condemns calling it "lost and extinguished", **rung 1**. CCC 405 (paraphrased): nature has not been totally corrupted.

**Level:** the gloss, the three goods and the four wounds, a school's analysis, **no magisterial level**; the *Credo*, by its own account not strictly a dogmatic definition (§3), **rung 3** for what it adds.

**Does not license:** "wounded, not corrupted" as a text. It is a slogan: Aquinas himself speaks of "corrupt nature" (I-II q.109 a.2); what is denied is *total* corruption (CCC 405) and an extinguished will (Trent VI can. 5).

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md)

### Baius

Michael Baius (Michel de Bay, 1513-1589) of Louvain, whose propositions made the first state's gifts, and grace itself, nature's due, and left fallen nature able only to sin; Pius V condemned them in 1567.

Pius V, *Ex omnibus afflictionibus* (1 October 1567): seventy-nine propositions (Denzinger's numbering; some editions count 76 or 80), drawn from his works and unnamed, censured "respectively" (*respective*) with notes from heretical down to offensive, no note paired with any proposition; the unpunctuated "Pian comma" clause was disputed. Confirmed by Gregory XIII (*Provisionis nostrae*, 1579) and Urban VIII (*In eminenti*, 1641). The propositions the lessons use (lesson's translations where quoted):

| Prop. | Condemned thesis | Lessons |
|---|---|---|
| 3 | the first man's happiness, had he persevered, "would be a wage, and not grace" | [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md) |
| 11 | eternal life was owed to obedience by natural law | [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md) |
| 21 | the raising of human nature into fellowship with the divine "was owed to the integrity of its first condition", and so is natural | [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md) |
| 23 | it is absurd to say man was raised from the beginning by a supernatural, gratuitous gift above his nature | [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md) |
| 25 | "All works of unbelievers are sins, and the virtues of the philosophers are vices" | [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md) Ex 2 |
| 26 | "The integrity of the first creation was not an unowed exaltation of human nature, but its natural condition" | [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md) |
| 27 | free will, without the help of God's grace, avails only for sinning | [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md) |
| 39 | what is done voluntarily is free even if done necessarily (revived as *Cum occasione* prop. 3, 1653) | [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md) |
| 55 | God could not have created man as he is now born (53 in some editions) | [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md) |
| 78 | "The immortality of the first man was not a benefit of grace, but a natural condition" | [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md) |

**Level:** a global censure, so the contrary of any one proposition is **rung 3** with no precise note, never rung 1: "heresy" overstates, "open question" understates ([in globo censure](#in-globo-censure)). Where another act stands with 1567 the lessons write **at least rung 3**: the elevation unowed even to unfallen nature (1567 with *Humani Generis* §26, [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md); [gratuity of the supernatural](#gratuity-of-the-supernatural)); integrity and immortality not owed (3.1's table). A manual's *fidei proximum* for that gratuity (Pohle-Preuss) is not a rung.

**Two errors, one bull:** Baius erred on both sides of the seam: the first state's gifts owed to nature (21, 26, 78; eternal life a wage, 3, 11), and fallen nature able only to sin (25, 27), the line Jansen revived ([Jansenism](#jansenism); [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)). Sin did not make grace a gift; it always was one.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md), [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)

### Pelagianism

The view that human nature can do what God commands, with grace as the nature God made, the law, teaching, Christ's example and forgiveness, never a power working on the will itself.

**In Pelagius's words** (B. B. Warfield's translations, NPNF1 vol. 5, Introductory Essay): "man is always able both to sin and not to sin" (confession of faith to Pope Innocent); universal sin is habit, "the long custom of sins which begins from childhood" until it "seems to have in some degree the force of nature" (Letter to Demetrias). Pelagius, a lay ascetic at Rome, was a moral reformer before he was a heretic, refusing to let Christians excuse sin by weakness; his triad of ability, volition and actuality, and the crux with Augustine (whether God's help reaches the willing itself or only the power to will), are [`patristics` 4.5](../patristics/lessons/04-05-augustine-against-the-pelagians.md)'s. Caelestius was his sharper follower.

**The fence against it:** [Carthage (418)](#council-of-carthage-418) (death a penalty; infants need remission; grace more than forgiveness, instruction or an easier road), [Orange II](#orange-ii) (even the first stirring is grace), [Trent Session V](#trent-session-v) cann. 1, 2 and 4 (re-issuing Orange and Carthage: the preamble's "old dissensions") and can. 3's "not by imitation" against sin by Adam's example ([propagation, not imitation](#propagation-not-imitation)), and [Trent Session VI](#trent-session-vi) cann. 1-3.

**Uses later in the course:** grace as facility, the one feature [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md) targets (Carthage can. 5; Trent VI can. 2's free will able "though hardly indeed and with difficulty"); Session VI's first front, so ch. 5's co-operation is grace-moved consent ([5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md)); the claim that eternal life is rendered to our preceding merits, which Augustine says "would not deserve reprobation" if the merits are owned as God's gifts (*On Grace and Free Will* 6.15; [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md)); man as author of both outcomes, the mirror of a predestination to sin ([5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md)); unbaptized infants promised "eternal life", though not the kingdom ([7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)).

**Level:** the thesis is excluded; its contradictories are **rung 1** (Carthage and Orange as received, and Trent). Pelagius and Caelestius as authors, **no magisterial level**.

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md), [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

### Council of Carthage (418)

The African plenary council of 1 May 418 whose eight canons condemned Pelagianism on death, infant baptism and grace.

**Numbering:** cann. 1-8 in the Latin Denzinger texts; cann. 109-116 of the African Code in Percival (NPNF2 vol. 14, Latin numbering; the Greek numbers differ). 3.1 quotes Percival's public-domain English; 3.2 gives its own translation of the same Latin (*necessitate naturae*: Percival's "by natural necessity", 3.2's "by necessity of nature").

| Can. | Condemns saying (lesson's translations) | Re-taught at |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Adam was "created mortal, so that whether he had sinned or not, he would have died in body" (Percival), "not by the desert of sin but by necessity of nature" (*non peccati merito, sed necessitate naturae*) | Trent V can. 1 |
| 2 | infants baptized "for the remission of sins" draw no original sin from Adam | Trent V can. 4, nearly verbatim |
| 3 | grace only remits past sins | the *Indiculus* ch. 7 |
| 4 | grace only shows us what to seek and avoid, and not so that what we know we should do "we may also love to do and be able to do" (*diligamus atque valeamus*) | the *Indiculus* ch. 7 |
| 5 | grace only makes the commandments easier: the Lord "does not say: without me you can do it with more difficulty, but: without me you can do nothing" (John 15:5) | Trent VI can. 2 |
| 6-8 | the saints confess their sins humbly but not "truly" (*veraciter*), or pray "forgive us" only for others | Trent VI can. 23; ch. 11's "both humble and true" |

**Level (3.2 decides):** the doctrine is **rung 1**, not by an African council's rank but through Zosimus's confirmation ([Tractoria](#tractoria)), Rome's adoption (the [Indiculus](#indiculus) embraces cann. 3-5 "as the Apostolic See's own") and Trent's re-definition (can. 1 via V can. 1, can. 2 via V can. 4, can. 5 via VI can. 2, cann. 6-8 via VI can. 23). Exactly what can. 1 fixes is 3.1's: death the penalty of sin, not a natural necessity; not that the body was immortal by nature ([immunity from death](#immunity-from-death)).

**Translation trap:** Percival's English of can. 4 (his CXII) puts "and also that we should love to do so" among what the condemned party grants; the Latin puts *diligamus* among what is denied. Use the Latin or 3.2's translation.

**The "middle place" canon:** a canon on a happy middle place for unbaptized infants stands in some manuscripts only: **no level**; it is [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)'s ([limbo](#limbo)).

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

### Tractoria

Pope Zosimus's circular letter of summer 418 condemning Pelagius and Caelestius and requiring the bishops' assent.

Zosimus, Innocent I's successor, had reopened the case in Caelestius's favour in 417. After Carthage (1 May 418) he condemned both in the *Tractoria* (Denzinger's heading: between June and August 418), which survives only in fragments, one of which speaks of a bond contracted by propagation. Eighteen Italian bishops, Julian of Eclanum among them, refused it and were deposed (Percival).

**Next on the route:** Ephesus (431) deposed clergy holding "the doctrines of Celestius" (cann. 1, 4) and, writing to Pope Celestine, upheld the Western depositions of Pelagius, Caelestius, Julian and others. Its canons are **law and discipline, no rung**; its letter receives a sentence on persons; it defined nothing about grace. Then the [Indiculus](#indiculus) (about 435-442).

**Level:** a papal act on the route by which Carthage's doctrine reaches **rung 1**; not graded on its own (it survives in fragments).

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md)

### Indiculus

A Roman collection (about 435-442, probably by Prosper of Aquitaine) of earlier papal and African rulings on grace, once taken for Pope Celestine I's.

Denzinger heads it "Capitula pseudo-Caelestina"; it is often cited as Celestine's chapters. Its prologue presents sentences of the African councils as ones the apostolic bishops made their own by approving them. Ch. 7 embraces Carthage's cann. 3-5 "as the Apostolic See's own". Ch. 8: "that the law of praying establish the law of believing" ([lex orandi](#lex-orandi); [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 6.1](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md)). Ch. 10 declines to assert "the deeper and more difficult parts" of the questions, which it does not dare to despise either. And the sentence on merit: God wills "our merits to be what are his own gifts" (*ut nostra velit esse merita, quae sunt ipsius dona*), which Trent VI ch. 16 takes over almost verbatim: God "will have the things which are His own gifts be their merits" (Waterworth; [merit](#merit)) (lesson's translations).

**Level:** **no magisterial level** of its own: a compilation, a witness to Rome's mind ([3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md)); it weighs by reception ([5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md)). Its merit sentence, as ch. 16 adopts it, has that chapter's weight, **at least rung 3**, its pairing of gift and merit **rung 1** inside can. 32 (5.3).

**Does not license:** calling it a papal decree of Celestine I; or reading ch. 10 as a definition that the deeper questions must stay open (declining to assert defines nothing, either way).

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md)

### Semi-Pelagianism

The Massilians' view that the beginning of a good will, or of faith, can come from us, with grace meeting and strengthening it afterwards.

**The Massilians:** monks of Marseilles and Lérins who accepted Carthage but resisted Augustine's late books, which seemed to make exhortation idle. John Cassian, *Conference* XIII.8 (tr. Edgar Gibson, NPNF2 vol. 11): God, "when He sees in us some beginnings of a good will", strengthens it, "increasing that which He Himself implanted or which He sees to have arisen from our own efforts". The weight is on "or": sometimes grace plants the beginning (Paul, Matthew), sometimes the will does and grace meets it (Zacchaeus and the good thief, XIII.11; Cornelius shown the way "by way of recompense", XIII.15). Faustus of Riez defended the same line in his *On Grace*. Cassian is venerated as a saint.

**The label is sixteenth-century**, not an ancient party name. Earliest attestation found: the Lutheran Formula of Concord (Epitome art. II; German written 1576-77, Book of Concord 1580) rejects the error of the "half-Pelagians" (*Halbpelagianer*; Latin *Semipelagiani*), who make man able by his own powers to begin his conversion; the quarrel over Molina then made it a Catholic charge ([de auxiliis](#de-auxiliis); [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)). The ancient names: "remnants of the Pelagians" (Augustine's friends) and "the remnants of the old error" (Boniface II, 531). Aquinas, without the label, ascribes the thesis "that the beginning of doing well came from us" to "the Pelagians" (ST I q.23 a.5).

**Level:** excluded; its contradictory is **rung 1** (Orange II can. 5 and its conclusion, "in every good work we do not begin"; Trent VI can. 3; [beginning of faith](#beginning-of-faith)). Cassian and Faustus as authors, and the label itself, **no magisterial level**.

**As a later charge:** "God helps those who help themselves; make the first move" has the Massilian shape and inverts [prevenient grace](#prevenient-grace) ([4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md)). Thomists threw the word at Molinists; Benedict XIV (1748) reports the charge and says Rome has not judged the Molinist system ([5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)). *Cum occasione* (1653) props. 4 and 5 use it as a label for opponents, prop. 4 misdescribing the Massilians as history ([five propositions](#five-propositions); [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)).

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md), [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)

### Orange II

The Gallic synod under Caesarius of Arles (3 July 529) whose canons and conclusion, confirmed by Boniface II in 531, became the Church's charter on grace.

**The synod:** fourteen bishops, met for a church's dedication, working from "a few chapters sent to us by the Apostolic See" (prooemium). Twenty-five *capitula*: cann. 1-8 run "if anyone says" but end in censures other than anathema (can. 1, "deceived by the error of Pelagius"; can. 4, he "resists the Holy Spirit"), never "let him be anathema"; cann. 9-25 are sentences of the Fathers, chiefly Augustine; then a conclusion drafted by Caesarius, holding the council's **only** anathema, against predestination to evil.

| Text | Content (lesson's translations) | Lessons |
|---|---|---|
| can. 1 | the whole man "changed, in body and soul, for the worse"; Trent V can. 1 borrows it | [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md) |
| can. 2 | sin, "the death of the soul", passed to all, not death alone; Trent V can. 2 re-issues it almost verbatim | [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md) |
| cann. 3-8 | grace prevenient to every salutary act: in can. 4 even the will to be cleansed is worked in us (Phil 2:13); can. 6 cites 1 Cor 4:7 | [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md) |
| can. 5 | the [beginning of faith](#beginning-of-faith) is not in us "naturally" but from the Spirit's inspiration | [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md) |
| can. 7 | no thinking or choosing any good "that pertains to the salvation of eternal life", or consenting to the gospel, "by the vigour of nature" (*per naturae vigorem*) without the Spirit's illumination (John 15:5; 2 Cor 3:5) | [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md) |
| can. 10 | God's help to be implored "even by the reborn and healed" (*etiam renatis ac sanatis*) | [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md) |
| can. 18 | "Grace is preceded by no merits": a reward is owed to good works if they are done, but "grace, which is not owed (*quae non debetur*), goes before, that they may be done" | [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md) |
| can. 22 | Augustine word for word (*Tractates on John* 5.1): "No one has anything of his own except falsehood and sin", with its second sentence, that any truth and justice is "from that fountain" | [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md) |
| conclusion | free will "bent and weakened" (*inclinatum et attenuatum*; Trent VI ch. 1 reuses the pair); Cassian's examples answered (the thief, Cornelius, Zacchaeus: their faith "was not from nature but given by the bounty of divine grace"); "in every good work we do not begin" (*non nos incipimus*); faith and love inspired with no good merits going before; all the baptized, with Christ's help, "can and ought to fulfil what belongs to the soul's salvation"; anathema "with all detestation" on any who believe some "predestined to evil by divine power" (*divina potestate*), near its end | [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md) |

**Boniface II, *Per filium nostrum* (25 January 531):** Caesarius asked Rome to "confirm by the authority of the Apostolic See" his confession that right faith and "the beginning of all good will" come by prevenient grace. Boniface names Augustine above all the Fathers, marvels that some still labour under "the remnants of the old error", and approves the confession as "in agreement with the Catholic rules of the Fathers". What he approves is the confession; the letter as Denzinger prints it names no canon.

**Level (3.2 decides):** the doctrine of the canons and conclusion (grace prevenient to every salutary act, the beginning of faith included; unmerited; no predestination to evil) is **rung 1**: Rome at both ends (chapters sent, confession approved), reception, and Trent defining the same (VI cann. 1-3, 17; V cann. 1-2). Not by the synod's rank: it is provincial. That Boniface's letter is itself *ex cathedra* is not manifest, so default down (can. 749 §3), and nothing needs it. Each sentence carries its doctrine in the sense received: can. 22 read flat (every act of an unbeliever a sin) collides with Baius's condemned prop. 25 and Trent VI can. 7; its second sentence blocks that reading. Later lessons take the level from here: no merit precedes the first grace (can. 18), **rung 1** (5.1, 5.3); no predestination to sin, **rung 1**, independently through Trent VI can. 17, which reuses "by the divine power ... predestined unto evil" (5.4).

**Said plainly:** on the historians' standard account its canons dropped out of circulation in the tenth century and came back with Peter Crabbe's *Concilia omnia* (Cologne, 1538): six centuries, not a thousand years. Aquinas's *Summa* never cites it (only the *first* council of Orange, of 441, through Gratian, III q.80 a.9); he reached its doctrine through Augustine and 2 Cor 3:5 (I q.23 a.5).

**Does not license:** calling it an ecumenical council or a solemn papal definition (the 1913 *Catholic Encyclopedia*'s "œcumenical authority" overstates); "only a local council, so optional" (understates); "twenty-five anathemas" (one); "Aquinas built his treatise on Orange".

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)

### Beginning of faith

The first movement of the will toward believing God (*initium fidei*): the Massilians held it could be ours, with grace answering; Orange II taught that it is grace's gift.

Orange II can. 5, the beginning-of-faith canon (lesson's translation): "as the increase, so also the beginning of faith" (*sicut augmentum, ita etiam initium fidei*), and the very disposition of belief, are in us not "naturally" but from "the inspiration of the Holy Spirit correcting our will from unbelief to faith". The conclusion takes Cassian's own examples, the thief, Cornelius and Zacchaeus: their faith "was not from nature but given by the bounty of divine grace". Boniface II approves Caesarius's confession that "the beginning of all good will" comes by prevenient grace. Trent VI can. 3: no one can believe, hope, love or repent as he ought without "the prevenient inspiration of the Holy Ghost"; ch. 5 begins adults' justification from God's call "without any merits existing on their parts"; ch. 8 calls faith "the beginning of human salvation, the foundation, and the root of all Justification".

**Level:** **rung 1** (Orange II as confirmed and received; Trent VI can. 3). The Massilian thesis ([semi-Pelagianism](#semi-pelagianism)) is excluded; its contradictory is **rung 1**.

**Keep apart:** the crux between Augustine and Pelagius, whether God's help reaches the willing itself, is [`patristics` 4.5](../patristics/lessons/04-05-augustine-against-the-pelagians.md)'s; the Massilians had already granted Carthage. *How* grace moves the will at the beginning, and why one receives it and another not, the fence leaves open: **rung 5** ([de auxiliis](#de-auxiliis)).

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md)

### Prevenient grace

Grace that goes before every salutary movement of the will, the first included, so that the first turn toward God is God's doing and still the person's free act.

**The acts:** Orange II cann. 3-8 and 18, and its conclusion ("in every good work we do not begin"); Boniface II's "the beginning of all good will" by prevenient grace (*per praevenientem Dei gratiam*). Trent VI ch. 5: in adults the beginning of justification "is to be derived from the prevenient grace of God", from his call, "without any merits existing on their parts"; they convert "by freely assenting to and co-operating with that said grace", being "also able to reject it", yet unable without it to move themselves to justice. Ch. 5 reads "Turn ye to me" (Zech 1:3) as admonishing our liberty and "Convert us, O Lord" (Lam 5:21) as confessing that "we are prevented by the grace of God". Can. 3: no believing, hoping, loving or repenting as one ought without "the prevenient inspiration of the Holy Ghost". Can. 4: the will moved by God can "refuse its consent, if it would", and is not "merely passive".

**The schools' vocabulary:** Aquinas, ST I-II q.111 a.3: grace has five effects (to heal, to desire good, to carry it into effect, to persevere, to reach glory); "prevenient" and "subsequent" are relative along that chain, a division "not ... in its essence, but only in its effects" (ad 2). Preparing for grace is itself grace, a motion and not a prior habit (I-II q.109 a.6; [necessity of grace](#necessity-of-grace)). Aquinas reached the doctrine without Orange's text, through 2 Cor 3:5, the verse Orange can. 7 cites (I q.23 a.5).

**Level:** the necessity and priority of prevenient grace, **rung 1** (Orange II as received; Trent VI can. 3); that the consent is free and can refuse, **rung 1** (can. 4); the five-effects division, a school's analysis, **no magisterial level**; how prevenient grace becomes efficacious, **rung 5** ([sufficient and efficacious grace](#sufficient-and-efficacious-grace); [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)). CCC 2001 locates.

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)

### Concupiscence

The disordered inclination toward sin that remains in the baptized: Trent says it is not truly and properly sin, but is called sin because it comes from sin and inclines to it.

Trent V can. 5 (Waterworth as Schaff prints it): baptism remits "the guilt of original sin"; there remains "concupiscence, or an incentive (to sin)" (*concupiscentiam vel fomitem*: *fomes*, tinder), "left for our exercise", which "cannot injure those who consent not, but resist manfully by the grace of Jesus Christ". Then the declaration: the Church has never understood it to be called sin "as being truly and properly sin in those born again, but because it is of sin, and inclines to sin" (*quia ex peccato est et ad peccatum inclinat*). A penal clause follows: *Si quis autem contrarium senserit, anathema sit* (Schaff prints it; the Hanover web copy of Waterworth drops it). The grammar is Augustine's: concupiscence "is called sin, since it arose from sin, and, when it has the upper hand, produces sin" (*On Marriage and Concupiscence* I.23.25, tr. Holmes).

**The target:** Melanchthon's *Apology of the Augsburg Confession* (1531): Luther held "that Baptism removes the guilt of original sin, although the material ... of the sin, i.e., concupiscence, remains" (II.35), and "Luther maintains that it is a sin" (II.38), not imputed to those in Christ (*Concordia Triglotta*, 1921). So the Lutheran position was not that baptism leaves the guilt (the 1913 *Catholic Encyclopedia*'s version); it was that what remains is truly sin, the precise target of can. 5's condemned "only rased, or not imputed" and of the declaration. Leo X had condemned Luther's thesis in *Exsurge Domine* (15 June 1520), prop. 2: to deny that sin remains in a child after baptism is to "trample on Paul and Christ together" (lesson's translation).

**Level (3.3 decides):** that concupiscence in the reborn is not truly and properly sin, **rung 1** (the penal clause condemns the contrary). The stated reason, "of sin, and inclines to sin", **at least rung 3**: whether the penal clause reaches the reason is not manifest (can. 749 §3); 3.1 rests [integrity](#gift-of-integrity) on it. *Exsurge Domine* condemned its propositions "respectively", fixing no note for prop. 2 ([in globo censure](#in-globo-censure)): the defining act is Trent V can. 5 (1546), not 1520.

**Keep apart:** Trent's *fomes* from Aquinas's concupiscence as original sin's material element (I-II q.82 a.3, **rung 4**); and from the two senses the Joint Declaration's partners use: for Lutherans, self-seeking desire regarded as sin in the light of the law; for Catholics, an inclination from sin toward sin (JD 30; Annex 2B; [simul iustus et peccator](#simul-iustus-et-peccator); [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)). Baptism's sacramental theology is [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 2.1](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/02-01-baptism-the-rite-and-its-effects.md)'s.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)

### Imputation theory of original sin

The view that original sin is Adam's own sin imputed to his descendants, with nothing of sin inhering in them.

**Exclusive form:** Ambrosius Catharinus (Ambrogio Catarino Politi, Dominican, d. 1553; *Opusculum de lapsu hominis*) and Albert Pighius (Albert Pigge, d. 1542; *Controversia* I, on original sin): Adam's descendants are called sinners by a divine imputation of his act, not by anything in them. Pohle-Preuss (1912) call it "highly improbable". **Milder form** (Salmeron, Toletus and especially Juan de Lugo): Adam's act persisting morally in each, with the lack of grace its penalty rather than its essence; Pohle finds it "less objectionable". The common alternative is the Thomist one: formally the privation of original justice, materially concupiscence (I-II q.82 a.3).

**Level:** never condemned by name; the manuals reject it, and their verdict has **no magisterial level**. That original sin is "in each one as his own" (*inest unicuique proprium*) is **rung 1** (Trent V can. 3); reading "as his own" is the theologians' task ([3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md)). The Thomist essence is **rung 4**.

**Keep apart:** the lesson calls it the imputation theory, not "federal" (a Reformed covenant term) or a "pact" theory. It is an account of what original sin *is*, with consequences for [how it is transmitted](#transmission-of-original-sin). Not to be confused with the *Apology*'s "not imputed" ([concupiscence](#concupiscence)) or with the imputation of Christ's justice in justification ([imputation](#imputation); [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md)).

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md)

### Trent Session VI

Trent's decree on justification, 13 January 1547: a proem, sixteen chapters and thirty-three canons (Waterworth, 1848), fighting Pelagius first and the Reformers after, without naming any Reformer.
