**Chapters** (the lessons that read them in parentheses): 1-3, nature and the Law cannot free fallen man, whose free will was "by no means extinguished"; Christ died for all, but only the reborn receive the benefit (3.3, 4.2, 5.1). 4, a translation into "the state of grace, and of the adoption of the sons of God"; its "or the desire thereof" is [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 2.2](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/02-02-necessity-infants-and-baptism-of-desire-and-blood.md)'s. 5-6, preparation of adults by prevenient grace, freely assented to and refusable; the dispositions (4.2, 5.1). 7, what justification is and its [causes](#causes-of-justification), with one [formal cause](#formal-cause) (5.1). 8, faith "the beginning of human salvation, the foundation, and the root of all Justification", and "freely", since nothing that precedes merits it (5.1, 5.3). 9, no certainty of faith of one's own grace (4.1, 5.1). 10, increase (5.1). 11, the commandments not impossible; the just's "Forgive us our trespasses" "both humble and true" (3.2, 5.6). 12-13, predestination and perseverance (5.4). 14, the fallen and penance (`sacraments-and-liturgy` 4.1). 15, grace lost by every mortal sin, not faith (`moral-theology` 3.4). 16, [merit](#merit) (5.3).

**Canons:** 1-3 against Pelagius; 4-9 preparation and free will; 10-11 the formal cause; 12-14 faith and certainty; 15-17 predestination; 18-21 the commandments; 22-23 perseverance and venial sin; 24 increase; 25-28 works, mortal sin, faith without charity; 29-30 recovery, temporal punishment; 31-32 reward and merit; 33 the decree derogates nothing from God's glory.

**The weight of the chapters (5.1 decides):** the canons are where the definition bites ([chapters and canons](#chapters-and-canons)): what a canon condemns has its contradictory at **rung 1**. The chapters are fenced unusually strongly: the proem forbids anyone to "believe, preach, or teach, otherwise than as by this present decree is defined and declared", and ch. 16 closes by calling them "Catholic doctrine on Justification, which whoso receiveth not faithfully and firmly cannot be justified" (*fideliter firmiterque*). That binds all to their doctrine without making every sentence, proof-texts and all, a definition: a chapter claim no canon repeats is **at least rung 3**, rung 1 only where its proposal as revealed is shown (can. 749 §3). Ch. 4's desire clause: presupposed, not rung 1.

**What the lessons use** (canons **rung 1**; chapters no canon repeats **at least rung 3**):

| Text | Content | Lessons |
|---|---|---|
| ch. 1; can. 5 | free will "attenuated as it was in its powers, and bent down, was by no means extinguished" (Orange's pair); can. 5 condemns "lost and extinguished" | [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md) |
| ch. 3 | by propagation men "contract through him, when they are conceived, injustice as their own"; "though He died for all" | [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md), [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md) |
| cann. 1-3 | no justification by works without grace; grace not only to make easier ("though hardly indeed and with difficulty", can. 2); no believing, hoping, loving or repenting as one ought without the Spirit's prevenient inspiration (can. 3) | [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md) |
| ch. 5; can. 4 | the call "without any merits existing on their parts"; the moved will can "refuse its consent, if it would", not "merely passive" (against *Cum occasione* prop. 2) | [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md), [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md) |
| can. 6 | condemned: God works evil "not permissively only, but properly" | [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md) |
| can. 7 | condemned: all works before justification "truly sins" | [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md) |
| can. 11 | condemned: justifying grace "only the favour of God" (*tantum favorem Dei*), excluding the grace and charity "inherent in them"; the decree never calls grace a habit or a quality | [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md) |
| ch. 9; cann. 13-14 | no certainty of faith that one has obtained grace | [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md) |
| ch. 12; cann. 15-16 | "except by special revelation, it cannot be known whom God hath chosen unto Himself"; no absolute certainty of one's own perseverance ([certainty of election](#certainty-of-election)) | [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md) |
| can. 17 | condemned: grace only for those "predestined unto life", the rest "by the divine power, predestined unto evil" | [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md) |
| ch. 11; can. 18 | "God commands not impossibilities" | [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md) |
| ch. 13; can. 22 | perseverance a gift; no persevering "without the special help of God" ([final perseverance](#final-perseverance)) | [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md) |
| can. 23 | the justified can lose grace; even the just cannot avoid all venial sin without a special privilege | [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md) |
| ch. 16; cann. 24, 26, 31-32 | increase and merit; God "will have the things which are His own gifts be their merits" (from the [Indiculus](#indiculus)) | [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md) |
| cann. 4, 9, 11, 12, 24; chs. 5, 8 | what the Joint Declaration's §41 is measured against; the canons unrevoked | [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md) |

**Does not assert:** a judgment on any named text. The decree condemns propositions; whether a given Lutheran text falls under a canon is a further question ([application of a condemnation](#application-of-a-condemnation); [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)). It defines no mode of grace's efficacy, which is why both schools could claim it ([5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)).

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md), [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md)

### Paul VI 1966 address

Paul VI's address of 11 July 1966 to a symposium on the mystery of original sin: after the Council, the 1950 line restated, with its evidential qualifier.

Paraphrased (vatican.va, Italian only; AAS 58 [1966] 649-655). Explanations that start from polygenism, which *non è stato dimostrato* ("has not been demonstrated"), and deny that original sin was first the disobedience of Adam, "first man" (*primo uomo*), committed at the beginning of history, are irreconcilable with Catholic doctrine. A theory of evolution will not be acceptable unless it accords decisively with the immediate creation of each human soul and holds decisive the disobedience of Adam, *protoparente universale* ("universal first parent"), which made him lose the holiness and justice in which he was constituted. CCC 390's footnote cites it (AAS 58 [1966] 654) beside Trent and *Humani Generis*.

**Level:** an address to a symposium: the lightest grade of **rung 3** for what it restates. Its "not demonstrated", like *Humani Generis*'s "conjectural", estimates a science: **no rung**.

**Does not assert:** a definition of monogenism; nor does it withdraw *Humani Generis* §37, which it repeats ([Humani Generis](#humani-generis); [monogenism and polygenism](#monogenism-and-polygenism)).

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md)

### Population genetics and the first pair

What human genetic variation does and does not exclude about a first couple, and over what time window.

- **Effective population size:** about ten thousand over the long term (Takahata, 1993). It is an average dominated by the smallest generations, not a floor ([`evolution-ecology` 1.4](../evolution-ecology/lessons/01-04-drift-ne-gene-flow.md)), so "never fewer than ten thousand" misreads it.
- **The tightest current window:** from the age of the four most recent ancestral variants genome-wide (TMR4A, median about 500,000 years), a strict two-person bottleneck, the pair our sole ancestors with no other lineage interbreeding, is excluded within roughly the last **500,000 years**, but not earlier (S. Joshua Swamidass, 2022).
- **A minority:** Ola Hössjer and Ann Gauger (*BIO-Complexity*, 2019) argue that a single couple that recent fits the data.
- **What the data do not touch:** an older pair; a pair among many ([biological and theological species](#biological-and-theological-species)); descent in the family tree ([genealogical and genetic ancestry](#genealogical-and-genetic-ancestry)).

**Level:** science, **no rung**. The doctrinal question is what Trent's clauses and *Humani Generis* §37 require ([clause-by-clause test](#clause-by-clause-test); [monogenism and polygenism](#monogenism-and-polygenism)).

**Does not license:** "genetics has disproved Adam" (it excludes only a recent sole pair, on stated assumptions); or "genetics confirms one pair" (overstated the other way).

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md)

### Genealogical and genetic ancestry

Being someone's ancestor in the family tree is not the same as having left them DNA, and genealogical ancestry spreads far faster.

Rohde, Olson and Chang (*Nature*, 2004): in their models the most recent common genealogical ancestor of everyone alive lived only a few thousand years ago. S. Joshua Swamidass, *The Genealogical Adam and Eve* (2019): a first couple could be genealogical ancestors of everyone without any genetic bottleneck, leaving the theology of people "outside the garden" to be worked out. A universal family-tree ancestor need not have left a given descendant any DNA.

**What it bears on:** "by propagation" (Trent V can. 3, **rung 1**) needs descent by generation, which genealogy supplies, not a gene; no act defines how original sin passes, **rung 5** ([transmission of original sin](#transmission-of-original-sin); [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md)). Where such a model strains is everyone outside the couple's line before the lines met; no act defines what makes a "true man" at the edges of the record, or when the first soul was created ([3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md) Ex 2).

**Level:** science, and a scientist's proposal, **no magisterial level**.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md)

### Biological and theological species

A biological species is an interbreeding population; a theological species is the set of beings with rational souls, which can begin with one pair inside a larger biological population.

Kenneth W. Kemp, "Science, Theology, and Monogenesis" (*American Catholic Philosophical Quarterly* 85, 2011; not *The Thomist*), defends monogenesis against those who say genetics excludes it by this distinction, which he credits to Andrew Alexander (1964). Swamidass adds that such a couple could be everyone's genealogical ancestors ([genealogical and genetic ancestry](#genealogical-and-genetic-ancestry)). 3.4 Ex 1 runs an invented proposal "of Kemp's kind" (two of some ten thousand hominids given rational souls, their descendants having children with the rest until every living human descends from the pair) through Trent's clauses and *Humani Generis* §37 as worded, and finds it inside both. Its costs: "true man" becomes a rational-soul criterion ([2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md)), not a biological kind, and the proposal must defend the first humans' having children with creatures who were not human.

**Level:** opinion, **no magisterial level**.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md)

### Situation of guilt

Karl Rahner's account of original sin as the co-determination of every human freedom by a situation shaped by others' guilt, reaching back to a first sin.

Three stages, as the lesson reports them through Kevin McMahon (*The Thomist*, 2002) and James Connor (*Theological Studies*, 1968):

1. **1954**, "Theologisches zum Monogenismus" (English in *Theological Investigations* I): a situation of damnation preceding every personal choice, yet arising in history, is inconceivable unless it began in one real individual's act; monogenism, never infallibly taught, "must be affirmed with inner (but not in itself irreformable) assent".
2. **1967**, "Evolution and Original Sin" (*Concilium* 26): genetic unity is found in a population, not an individual; God meant the first humans to hand grace on to one another, so a first sin in the first community, by one or several, tore that web for all; he saw "no reason" for the Magisterium to intervene against polygenism.
3. **1976**, *Foundations of Christian Faith* (English 1978, pp. 110-11): every free act is co-determined by others' guilt, as a banana's buyer profits, unchosen, from injustice to the pickers. Original sin is that situation, reaching back to the first sin.

**Level:** a theologian's theory, **no magisterial level**; how propagation transmits is **rung 5** ([transmission of original sin](#transmission-of-original-sin)). That the 1950 reason has lapsed (Rahner, 1967) is disputed among theologians; the act stands ([Humani Generis](#humani-generis)).

**Does not license:** "Rahner always rejected monogenism" (he defended it in 1954 and reversed in 1967); "Rahner dropped the first sin" (in 1967 he gave up one first *man*, not the first sin).

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md)

### Sin of the world

John 1:29's phrase ("Behold him who taketh away the sin of the world", DR), used two ways: by the Catechism for the sinful condition all sins produce, and by Schoonenberg as an account of original sin itself.

1. **CCC 408** (paraphrased): the consequences of original sin and of all personal sins put the world in a sinful condition that John's phrase aptly describes; the phrase can also mean the negative influence of communal situations and social structures that are the fruit of sins (citing *Reconciliatio et Paenitentia* 16). A description; CCC 404 still has a sin transmitted by propagation. Structures are not moral subjects ([`moral-theology` 3.5](../moral-theology/lessons/03-05-social-sin-and-cooperation-in-the-sins-of-others.md)).
2. **Piet Schoonenberg** (Dutch Jesuit; *Man and Sin*, English 1965; through Connor's survey, 1968): the fall is not one catastrophic act but the whole history of sins from the first sinner on, which together are "the sin of the world". To be human is to be situated, an inner condition of freedom there before any choice (an "existential"). It works by bad example, missing good example and missing grace others were meant to hand on, so each is born lacking grace. Birth alone puts one in this "world", so original sin passes by generation, and neither monogenism nor physical descent from Adam is required. He first made the situation universal only through the world's rejection of Christ, and later dropped that.

**Level:** identifying original sin with the sin of the world is Schoonenberg's theology, **no magisterial level**; CCC 408 locates a description and adopts no theory.

**Does not license:** "the Catechism adopted the sin of the world": CCC 408 uses John's phrase for the effects of original *and* personal sins, and for structures; CCC 404 keeps propagation.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md)

### Clause-by-clause test

The course's grading rule for any restatement of original sin: check it against each clause of Trent Session V in turn, neither waving it through as modern nor dismissing it as new, and name each clause it strains.

**The four points** ([3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md), from [Trent Session V](#trent-session-v)):

1. **An event:** "the first man, Adam" transgressed and lost the holiness and justice in which he was constituted (can. 1).
2. **A loss for posterity:** not "for himself alone"; sin, not death only, passed into "the whole human race" (can. 2).
3. **A transmission:** "in its origin is one", "by propagation, not by imitation" (can. 3); "contracted by generation" (can. 4) ([propagation, not imitation](#propagation-not-imitation)).
4. **A state:** "in each one as his own" (can. 3); "contracted" and not "committed", "a state and not an act" (CCC 404).

Then the rung-3 check: *Humani Generis* §37's two disjuncts, (a) true human beings after Adam not descended from him by natural generation, (b) "Adam" signifying a number of first parents, and its epistemic reason, "it is in no way apparent how" ([monogenism and polygenism](#monogenism-and-polygenism)).

**What the test leaves open:** the mechanism of transmission, **rung 5** ([transmission of original sin](#transmission-of-original-sin)); what makes a "true man" at the edges of the record, and when the first soul was created (no act defines either).

**Where the lessons run it:** 3.4 Ex 1, a pair within a population ([biological and theological species](#biological-and-theological-species)); Ex 2, a genealogical couple with people outside the garden ([genealogical and genetic ancestry](#genealogical-and-genetic-ancestry)); P3, Schoonenberg against "not by imitation" ([sin of the world](#sin-of-the-world)). Boss 3(d) applies it to a first-community proposal ([syllabus](syllabus.md)).

**Level:** a method, **no level**. The clauses it applies are **rung 1** (Trent V cann. 1-4); the 1950 check is **rung 3**.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md)

**Grace (Module 4)**

### Grace

God's favour, which always makes something in the creature: himself given, and a gift in the soul.

Aquinas's turn (ST I-II q.110 a.1): a man's love presupposes the good it loves, God's causes it, so "every love of God is followed at some time by a good caused in the creature". God's grace is never a favour that leaves us as we were. Trent, Session VI (13 January 1547), can. 11, condemns calling justifying grace "only the favour of God" (*tantum favorem Dei*) and excluding the grace and charity "inherent in them". The Latin says *inhaereat* of charity (can. 11) and of the justice by which we are justified (ch. 16); the decree never calls grace a habit or a quality. Three questions sort the kinds ([4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md)): who is given ([uncreated and created grace](#uncreated-and-created-grace)); for whom ([gratum faciens and gratis data](#gratum-faciens-and-gratis-data)); does it last or pass ([sanctifying grace](#sanctifying-grace), [actual grace](#actual-grace)). Operating and cooperating, prevenient and subsequent, sufficient and efficacious are not further kinds: they name one grace by what it does.

**Two senses of "unowed"** ([4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md)): to sinners, who forfeited a claim (a pardon); and to any nature, which never had one (a king's friendship). The second is the [gratuity of the supernatural](#gratuity-of-the-supernatural).

**Level:** inherence, not favour alone, **rung 1** (VI can. 11); a stable, habitual gift, **at least rung 3** (CCC 2000); an accident in the category of quality, **rung 4** (q.110 a.2).

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md)

### Three senses of grace

In ordinary speech "grace" means favour, a free gift, and thanks: the second springs from the first, the third from the second.

ST I-II q.110 a.1: grace is taken "for anyone's love" (the soldier "in the good graces of the king"), "for any gift freely bestowed", and for "the recompense of a gift given 'gratis'", as we are "grateful"; Aquinas's Latin for the third is *agere gratias*, to give thanks. Greek *charis* carries all three: Mary "found grace with God" (Luke 1:30, DR); the "diversities of graces" of 1 Cor 12:4 are *charismata*, gifts; "thanks be to God" (1 Cor 15:57) is *charis*. Saying grace before a meal is the third sense.

**Level:** Aquinas's analysis, **no magisterial level** (common teaching).

**Keep apart:** the third sense is thanks, not "the effect of the gift".

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md)

### Uncreated and created grace

Uncreated grace is God himself, given and dwelling in the just; created grace is the gift he makes in the soul; both are given together.

"The charity of God is poured forth in our hearts, by the Holy Ghost who is given to us" (Rom 5:5, DR): a gift, and the Giver. The Spirit's invisible mission ([`trinity-and-god` 6.1](../trinity-and-god/lessons/06-01-the-missions-of-the-son-and-the-spirit.md)) is made "according to the gift of sanctifying grace" (ST I q.43 a.3), by which the creature can "enjoy also the divine person Himself" (ad 1): the Spirit is possessed in the gift. Leo XIII: the Spirit gives not only his gifts but himself, the supreme Gift ([*Divinum Illud Munus*](#divinum-illud-munus) §9). Peter Lombard merged the two, making our charity the Spirit himself (*Sentences* I d.17, reported at II-II q.23 a.2); [`moral-theology` 3.2](../moral-theology/lessons/03-02-faith-hope-and-charity.md) answers him.

**The school question: which comes first** ([4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md)). Thomist: the Persons dwell on the ground of sanctifying grace, which disposes the soul to possess them (I q.43 a.3), **rung 4**. Karl Rahner ("Some Implications of the Scholastic Concept of Uncreated Grace", *Zeitschrift für katholische Theologie* 63, 1939; paraphrased): Scripture and the Fathers make created grace the *consequence* of God's self-gift, Scholasticism made it the *basis*; reverse the order ([quasi-formal causality](#quasi-formal-causality)), **rung 5**. The question sits inside the two limits of [*Mystici Corporis*](#mystici-corporis) §78.

**Level:** inherence of the created gift, **rung 1** (VI can. 11); the Spirit himself given, **at least rung 3** (DIM §9; 4.3 puts the indwelling itself at rung 1, from John 14:23); the order of the two, **rung 5 inside two rung-3 limits**.

**Does not assert:** that "grace is God's self-gift" ([`trinity-and-god` 6.3](../trinity-and-god/lessons/06-03-rahners-rule-and-the-immanent-trinity.md)) makes the created gift optional; nor that the Scholastics put a created thing in place of God. Rahner's quarrel is over the order, not the fact.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md)

### Charisms

Graces given to some for the good of others and the building up of the Church, not to make the recipient holy.

A charism is a *gratia gratis data* ([gratum faciens and gratis data](#gratum-faciens-and-gratis-data)): the Spirit's manifestation is given "unto profit" (1 Cor 12:7, DR), of others. Grace that makes pleasing is the nobler, uniting to the end itself (ST I-II q.111 a.5, from 1 Cor 12:31). A charism can exist without it, as prophecy can without charity (II-II q.172 a.4, from Matt 7:22-23). The seven gifts of the Holy Spirit are not charisms: they are in all the just, for their own salvation ([`moral-theology` 3.3](../moral-theology/lessons/03-03-the-cardinal-virtues-and-the-gifts.md)); where "wisdom" is on both lists, the gift makes the mind movable by the Spirit and the charism is the *word* of wisdom, for instructing others (q.111 a.4 ad 4). *Lumen Gentium* 12 finds special graces among the faithful of every rank, for building up the Church; the Catechism orders charisms to sanctifying grace and the common good (CCC 2003). Their discernment is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 3.3](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/03-03-the-laity-charisms-and-the-sensus-fidei.md)'s. One instance: the [grace of the word](#grace-of-the-word).

**Level:** special graces for building up the Church, **at least rung 3** (LG 12; CCC 2003); Aquinas's scheme, and prophecy without charity, **rung 4**.

**Does not assert:** that a charism certifies holiness or gauges how "full" someone is; no one knows with certainty of faith that he is in grace ([certainty of grace](#certainty-of-grace)).

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md)

### Gratum faciens and gratis data

Aquinas's first division of grace, by purpose: grace by which a person is himself united to God, and grace given him to help lead others to God.

ST I-II q.111 a.1: *gratia gratum faciens*, "whereby man himself is united to God", against *gratia gratis data*, by which "one man cooperates with another in leading him to God". The English Dominican translation renders them "sanctifying grace" and "gratuitous grace". The second is the [charisms](#charisms).

**Two "sanctifying graces".** q.111 a.1 divides by purpose, so God's help moving one's own will (an [actual grace](#actual-grace)) falls on the *gratum faciens* side. The Catechism's sanctifying grace (CCC 2000) is a division by duration: the habit against actual graces. Do not read the translation's "sanctifying grace" in q.111 a.1 as CCC 2000's.

**Level:** the division, **rung 4** (Aquinas's scheme); its modern counterpart, charisms ordered to sanctifying grace and the common good, **at least rung 3** (CCC 2003).

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md)

### Grace of the word

A charism by which the Spirit makes a speaker's words teach, please and sway hearers more excellently than skill could.

ST II-II q.177 (on the gratuitous grace "consisting in words"), a.1: the Spirit makes speech teach, please and sway, effecting more excellently what the art of rhetoric does less well. Being a *gratia gratis data*, it is given for the hearers, and says nothing of the speaker's holiness. The syllabus calls it "the grace of speech".

**Level:** Aquinas's analysis, **rung 4**.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md)

### Actual grace

A passing help by which God moves mind and will toward a particular salutary act, as against the lasting habitual gift.

Aquinas: God helps by moving the soul, a help that "is not a quality, but a movement of the soul", and by infusing "a habitual gift" (ST I-II q.110 a.2). The name "actual grace" is not his: his *Summa* speaks of God's help moving mind and will, and the label is a later schools'. The Catechism sets sanctifying grace, a habitual gift and stable supernatural disposition, against actual graces, God's interventions in conversion and sanctification (CCC 1999-2000). The Dicastery for the Doctrine of the Faith uses it: blessings ask for aids from the Spirit's impulses, "what classical theology calls 'actual grace'" (*Fiducia Supplicans* §31, 18 December 2023); Mary's intercession can ask for actual graces, which prepare even sinners for justification, while infusing sanctifying grace is the Lord's work alone (*Mater Populi Fidelis* ¶69, dated 4 November 2025, approved by Leo XIV).

**Where it is needed** ([4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md)): to prepare for grace, a motion and not a habit (q.109 a.6); for each good act of the justified, since habitual grace heals the mind but not yet the flesh (a.9); to persevere (a.10; [final perseverance](#final-perseverance)). **Named by outcome** ([5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)): refused, it was [sufficient](#sufficient-and-efficacious-grace); reaching the act, efficacious. How it yields a free consent is the [*de auxiliis*](#de-auxiliis) question.

**Level:** needed, refusable prevenient help, **rung 1** (Trent VI cans. 3-4); the habitual/actual distinction, **at least rung 3** (CCC 2000); *Fiducia Supplicans* and *Mater Populi Fidelis*, **rung 3**, defining no term; the name and its subdivisions, **rung 4**.

**Keep apart, both ways:** the Catechism's and the Dicastery's use does not make the scholastic division dogma; the label's lateness does not make transient helps a school invention.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)

### Operating and cooperating grace

One grace named by its effect: operating where God alone moves and the mind is moved, cooperating where the mind also moves.

ST I-II q.111 a.2, after Augustine (*On Grace and Free Will* 17.33, as [`patristics` 4.5](../patristics/lessons/04-05-augustine-against-the-pelagians.md) reads him; CCC 2001): grace is operating where the mind is moved and God "is the sole mover", cooperating where the mind "both moves and is moved". The pair divides God's motion (moving the will to begin willing good is operating) and the habitual gift: operating as it heals and justifies, cooperating as the principle of meritorious works, as heat makes its subject hot and also gives heat outwardly ([4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md) Flashback). "Operating and cooperating grace are the same grace" (a.2 ad 4); prevenient and subsequent likewise divide grace "only in its effects" (a.3 ad 2; [prevenient grace](#prevenient-grace)). Trent's form: adults' justification begins from prevenient grace, with which the one called freely co-operates, being "also able to reject it" (VI ch. 5). Merit is "the effect of cooperating grace" (I-II q.114, prologue; [merit](#merit)).

**Level:** the division, **rung 4**; real, free, refusable cooperation with prevenient grace, **rung 1** (VI can. 4).

**Does not assert:** further kinds of grace. The pairs time-stamp one grace.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md)

### Sufficient and efficacious grace

Sufficient grace gives a real power to act well that is not followed; efficacious grace actually brings about the free consent.

Named in [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md), argued in [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md). One sermon, two hearers: one is converted, one shrugs. The second received a real grace he did not follow (sufficient); the first, a grace that brought consent (efficacious). Both are [actual graces](#actual-grace) named by outcome. The open question, **what makes efficacious grace efficacious**: its own power (intrinsic efficacy; [physical premotion](#physical-premotion); Báñez, the Dominicans) or the consent God foresees (extrinsic efficacy; [middle knowledge](#middle-knowledge); Molina, the Jesuits).

**Jansen's defenders** claimed he taught nothing but efficacious grace, Augustine's and the Thomists': the will "always may, but never will, resist his grace" (Pascal, *Provincial Letters* XVIII, 24 March 1657). Rome never condemned efficacious grace as such; the Thomists were never reprobated ([*Dum praeterito*](#dum-praeterito-1748)).

**Level:** that grace can be resisted, **rung 1** (Trent VI can. 4; Innocent X, *Cum occasione*, 31 May 1653, condemned as heretical "in the state of fallen nature interior grace is never resisted", prop. 2); that grace infallibly brings consent where God wills, held by both schools as following from predestination, **rung 4**; how grace is efficacious, or truly sufficient yet unused, **rung 5**.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md), [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)

### Certainty of grace

No one can know with the certainty of faith that he is in the state of grace; he may not doubt God's mercy, and signs allow a conjecture.

Trent VI ch. 9: "no one can know with a certainty of faith, which cannot be subject to error, that he has obtained the grace of God" (Waterworth); cans. 13-14 condemn making such certainty necessary for forgiveness or justification. Grace escapes experience (CCC 2005). Aquinas allows only imperfect knowledge "conjecturally by signs" (ST I-II q.112 a.5), a school's account. A felt movement of grace proves nothing about one's state, since actual graces are given even to sinners to dispose them (VI ch. 5; *Mater Populi Fidelis* ¶69). Between floor (cans. 13-14) and ceiling (ch. 9) the decree is silent, and leaves the measure of a penitent's peace to pastoral judgment ([5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md) Ex 2). Of one's election: [certainty of election](#certainty-of-election).

**Level:** certainty not required for justification, **rung 1** (cans. 13-14); no certainty of faith that one has grace, **at least rung 3** (ch. 9, a chapter no canon repeats; CCC 2005 likewise); conjecture by signs, **no magisterial level**.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md)

### Necessity of grace

Grace is needed for every salutary act, from the first stirring of faith to perseverance at death; it is not needed for every naturally good act.

**The line** runs between the natural and the [salutary](#salutary-act), not between good acts and sins. [Orange II](#orange-ii) (529) can. 7 condemns saying that "by the vigour of nature" (*per naturae vigorem*) one can think or choose any good "that pertains to the salvation of eternal life", or consent to the gospel, without the Spirit's illumination (lesson's translation; citing John 15:5 and 2 Cor 3:5). Trent VI can. 1: no justification by works "without the grace of God through Jesus Christ"; can. 3: no one can "believe, hope, love, or be penitent as he ought" without the Spirit's prevenient inspiration.

**Two fronts.** Against grace as a convenience: [Carthage (418)](#council-of-carthage-418) can. 5, the Lord "does not say: without me you can do it with more difficulty, but: without me you can do nothing" (lesson's translation), reading John 15:5 of "the fruits of the commandments"; Trent VI can. 2 ("though hardly indeed and with difficulty"). Against total corruption: free will, "attenuated as it was in its powers, and bent down, was by no means extinguished" (VI ch. 1, reusing Orange's *inclinatum et attenuatum*); cans. 5 and 7; [Baius](#baius) props. 25 and 27 (1567), the Holy Office's prop. 8 (7 December 1690) and Quesnel's props. 38-40 ([*Unigenitus*](#unigenitus-1713), 1713), all [*in globo*](#in-globo-censure).

**Preparation and perseverance.** Preparing for grace is being *turned* to God, which only God's motion can do (q.109 a.6; Orange II cann. 3-7; Trent VI ch. 5, reading Zech 1:3 as admonishing liberty and Lam 5:21 as confessing "we are prevented by the grace of God"). The justified still need God's motion for each good act (a.9); Christ's power "always precedes and accompanies and follows" their good works (VI ch. 16); help must be implored "even by the reborn and healed" (*etiam renatis ac sanatis*, Orange II can. 10). [Final perseverance](#final-perseverance) is itself a gift. The school form is ST I-II q.109 aa.1-10 ([the three helps](#the-three-helps)).

**Level:** Trent VI cans. 1-5, 7 and 22, **rung 1**; Orange II and Carthage, **rung 1** by confirmation and reception ([3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md)); ch. 16's line, **at least rung 3**; "all works of unbelievers are sins", condemned with no precise note, its contrary **rung 3**; q.109, including a.8's thesis that without healing grace no one avoids mortal sin for long, **rung 4**.

**Does not assert:** that "nature is wounded, not destroyed" is a conciliar formula: it summarizes Trent VI ch. 1, q.109 a.2 and CCC 405.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md)

### The three helps

Aquinas's count of what a human being needs from God: his motion as First Mover for any act, a strength above nature for supernatural good, and, after the fall, healing.

ST I-II q.109 a.2, end of the respondeo: in the state of perfect nature man needs a strength superadded to nature "for one reason", in the state of corrupt nature "for two reasons"; "Beyond this, in both states man needs the Divine help, that he may be moved to act well." So three: **God's motion** as First Mover, needed by any created agent in any state (from a.1); **a strength above nature**, to do and will supernatural good, needed in both states, unfallen Adam included (a healthy runner still cannot run to the moon); **healing**, needed only after the fall (Aquinas's sick man, who makes some movements but not as the healthy do). The same respondeo faces both errors: nature is "not altogether corrupted", so it can "build dwellings, plant vineyards, and the like", yet "cannot do all the good natural to it". [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md) places the [*de auxiliis*](#de-auxiliis) quarrel at the first: how God's motion of the will squares with freedom.

**Level:** Aquinas, **common teaching, rung 4**. What it explains is defined: grace is necessary for every salutary act (**rung 1**; [necessity of grace](#necessity-of-grace)).

**Keep apart:** grace is no repair kit for the fall. Unfallen Adam needed the First Mover and the elevating strength (a.2, a.5); the fall added healing. The article says "dwellings", not "houses".

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md)

### Salutary act

An act ordered to eternal life (believing, hoping, loving God and repenting as one ought), as against a natural act aimed at a natural good, like building a house or keeping a promise.

Orange II can. 7's "any good that pertains to the salvation of eternal life" and Trent VI can. 3's "as he ought" draw the line; nothing in either says natural goods need grace. Fallen nature can still "work some particular good" (ST I-II q.109 a.2). An unbeliever who has nursed her brother for twenty years ([4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md) Ex 1): her acts are not sins (calling them all sins is condemned), and they do not merit eternal life, which needs strength above nature (a.5; Trent VI can. 1). Whether grace is at work in her, the doctrine cannot see; if she begins to pray, that beginning is grace (a.6).

**Level:** grace needed for every salutary act, the beginning of faith included, **rung 1** (Trent VI cans. 1, 3; Orange II); the contrary of "every unbeliever's work is a sin", **rung 3**; a.8's thesis that unaided nature cannot keep the law for long, **rung 4**.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md)

### Facere quod in se est

The scholastic axiom that God does not deny grace to one who does what is in him.

Aquinas states it as an objection (ST I-II q.109 a.6 obj. 2) and accepts it only if what is in us is in our power "according as he is moved by God" (ad 2). Read as natural effort that earns grace, it meets Orange II can. 18, "grace, which is not owed, goes before" (*gratia, quae non debetur, praecedit*; lesson's translation), and Trent VI can. 3. The person did truly consent, and could have refused (can. 4). The doctrine forbids both "my part was mine alone" and "I did nothing"; it does not say *how* one consent is wholly God's gift and wholly the person's act ([physical premotion](#physical-premotion), [middle knowledge](#middle-knowledge)).

**Level:** the axiom, **no magisterial level**; that grace is unowed and goes first, and that consent can refuse, **rung 1**; how the two are one act, **rung 5**, left open in 1607.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md)

### Final perseverance

Remaining in grace until death: a gift no one can give himself, which needs God's special help and cannot be merited.

Trent VI ch. 13: a gift that "cannot be derived from any other but Him"; let no one promise himself anything "as certain with an absolute certainty", though all ought to place "a most firm hope in God's help". Can. 22 condemns saying the justified can persevere "without the special help of God", or cannot with it. Aquinas: perseverance to the end is a guarding assistance, not a further habit (ST I-II q.109 a.10); in this life it is not merited, though prayer obtains it (q.114 a.9 and ad 1). Can. 16, on certainty of perseverance, is [`moral-theology` 3.2](../moral-theology/lessons/03-02-faith-hope-and-charity.md)'s; why some receive the gift is [predestination](#predestination). Since predestination includes final perseverance, Trent's ch. 12 rule against presuming one's election reaches rung 1 at its core through can. 16 ([certainty of election](#certainty-of-election)).

**Level:** special help needed, **rung 1** (can. 22); the gift from God alone, **at least rung 3** (ch. 13); unmerited, obtained by prayer, not a habit, **rung 4** (q.114 a.9; q.109 a.10).

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md)

### De auxiliis

The Roman hearings of 1598-1607 on how efficacious grace and free will fit, closed by Paul V without a decision.

**The question:** what makes [efficacious grace](#sufficient-and-efficacious-grace) efficacious: its own power ([physical premotion](#physical-premotion); Báñez, the Dominicans) or the consent God foresees ([middle knowledge](#middle-knowledge); Molina, the Jesuits)? Each side charged the other with heresy, Calvinism and semi-Pelagianism. [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md) places it at the first of [the three helps](#the-three-helps): how God's motion of a free will works.

**The closure.** Paul V's formula, sent to the Dominican and Jesuit generals on 5 September 1607, does three things ([5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)): it **dismisses** the disputants and consultors; **reserves** the "declaration and determination" to the pope, to be promulgated "at a suitable time" (*opportune*), at no named date; and **forbids** anyone to "qualify" the opposing side or "brand it with any censure" (*aut qualificaret aut censura quapiam notaret*), asking both to avoid harsh words. To qualify is to attach any theological note, "rash" or "erroneous", so the ban is wider than "do not call it heresy". It never says either view is true; both orders kept teaching because nothing was decided. The promised determination has not come in four centuries.

**After.** A Holy Office decree (1 December 1611) forbade publishing on the question without its leave (renewed under Urban VIII; later lapsed). Paul V gave his reasons privately to the Spanish ambassador (26 July 1611): time reveals the truth; both sides agree in substance and "differ only in the mode"; and both orders' good name had to be kept amid heresies, which is prudence, not theology. In 1654 the Holy Office declared an alleged constitution of Paul V condemning Molina unworthy of credence. Then [*Dum praeterito*](#dum-praeterito-1748) (1748).

**The ceiling both schools must meet, rung 1:** grace comes first, the beginning of faith included (Orange II can. 5 and conclusion, *non nos incipimus*; Trent VI can. 3); the moved will can refuse (can. 4); God works evil only permissively (can. 6); God knows future free acts (*Dei Filius* ch. 1).

**Level:** the 1607 formula, a decision not to define plus a precept, **law, no rung**: it lifts neither school above **rung 5**; the 1611 ban, the 1654 decree and *Dum praeterito*, **discipline, no rung**; Paul V's speech, **no magisterial level**.

**Keep apart:** the predestination question ([before or after foreseen merits](#before-or-after-foreseen-merits)). The axes cross: Bellarmine and Suárez held middle knowledge and also predestination before foreseen merits.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)

### Physical premotion

The Dominican thesis that efficacious grace is a real divine motion applying the will to its act, prior in the order of causes, not of time, so that grace is efficacious of itself.

Domingo Báñez (1528-1604) of Salamanca, answering Molina in the Dominicans' *Apologetica* (1595): whatever passes from not acting to acting is moved by the first cause, and the will is no exception: God applies it to its act (the First Mover of [the three helps](#the-three-helps); [`thomistic-synthesis` 5.2](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/05-02-providence-and-secondary-causes.md)). Also called predetermination; "physical" means real, as against merely moral, not bodily or coercive. The will consents because grace moves it, not the reverse. Freedom is untouched because God moves each thing according to its nature: his will extends "also to its being done in a way which is fitting to the nature of that thing" (ST I-II q.10 a.4 ad 1). The moved will keeps the power to dissent in the divided sense ([composed and divided sense](#composed-and-divided-sense)). Sufficient grace gives a real power; efficacious grace gives the act. Opponents charged it with Calvinism and with making God the author of sin; the Molinist presses that, without the premotion, the refusal looks caused by its withholding.

**Level:** **rung 5**, a permitted school; Báñez as author, **no magisterial level**.

**Does not assert:** that premotion is doctrine because Aquinas is the Church's doctor: the strongest papal praise of Aquinas reserves disputed questions to freedom ([`thomistic-synthesis` 6.5](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/06-05-what-the-church-has-said-about-thomism.md)).

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)

### Middle knowledge

Molina's thesis that God knows, prior to any decree of his, what each free creature would freely do in any order of circumstances.

Luis de Molina (1535-1600), *Concordia* (Lisbon, 1588), Part IV. A free agent is one that, with everything required for acting in place, can act or not act; yet providence is certain. So between God's natural knowledge of all that could be and his free knowledge of what he decrees lies a middle knowledge: comprehending each created will, God knows what it *would* freely do in any circumstances, though it could do otherwise. Grace is gratuitous and comes first; God concurs in the act itself ([simultaneous concurrence](#simultaneous-concurrence)), not by a prior determination; knowing who would consent to which grace, he chooses which graces to give, so the plan is infallible and the act free. Efficacy is extrinsic, from the foreseen consent; [congruism](#congruism) refines it. Opponents charged it with semi-Pelagianism; Benedict XIV (1748): the popes "have not yet passed judgment on this Molinist system". The Thomist presses 1 Cor 4:7, "who distinguisheth thee?" (DR).

**Level:** **rung 5**. Do not confuse it with the **rung 1** doctrine that God knows future free acts, "even those which are yet to be by the free action of creatures" (*Dei Filius* ch. 1, Schaff), as [`thomistic-synthesis` 5.2](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/05-02-providence-and-secondary-causes.md) marks it.

**Keep apart:** Molina's God does not only watch. He gives the grace and concurs in the act; middle knowledge is how he knows, not a withdrawal of causality.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)

### Merit

A free act to which a reward is owed; before God, owed only because God ordained and promised it, and only within grace.

**Defined.** Lateran IV's [*Firmiter*](#firmiter) (1215): the married too, "pleasing God by right faith and good work, merit to attain eternal beatitude" (lesson's translation). Trent VI can. 32 (13 January 1547) condemns denying that the good works of the justified are both "the gifts of God" and "also the good merits of him that is justified", or that he "truly merit[s]" increase of grace, eternal life and its attainment (if he departs in grace), and an increase of glory. Can. 24: works done in grace increase justice; cans. 26 and 31: hoping for "an eternal recompense" is no sin.

**The ground.** Ch. 16 offers eternal life "both as a grace mercifully promised ... and as a reward", rendered "according to the promise of God Himself"; Christ's virtue flows into the justified "as the head into the members" and "precedes and accompanies and follows" their works; God "will have the things which are His own gifts be their merits", the [*Indiculus*](#indiculus)'s sentence. A reward, not a wage ([`theology-of-debt` 2.4](../theology-of-debt/lessons/02-04-the-bond-and-the-ransom.md), [3.2](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-02-the-fathers-alms-satisfaction-and-ransom.md)). Aquinas's account: [divine ordination](#divine-ordination), [condign and congruous merit](#condign-and-congruous-merit); charity the chief principle of merit (q.114 a.4).

**The hinge.** "None of those things which precede justification—whether faith or works—merit the grace itself of justification" (ch. 8), with Orange II can. 18 behind it: grace, "which is not owed, goes before". No one merits his own first grace (q.114 a.5), his restoration after a fall (a.7) or his perseverance in this life (a.9); increase of grace is merited (a.8). Unfallen Adam could merit heaven only as already raised by grace ([4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md) Ex 1). An unbeliever's natural goods do not merit eternal life, which needs a strength above nature (q.109 a.5; [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md) Ex 1).

**Augustine and the liturgy.** "It is His own gifts that God crowns, not your merits" is conditional in *On Grace and Free Will* 6.15 ("if, at least, your merits are of your own self"), and the full passage keeps "your good merits", crowned as gifts. The first Preface of the Saints: "in crowning their merits" God crowns "your own gifts", the Catechism's epigraph before CCC 2006; the Roman Canon asks admission "not as an assessor of merit but as a lavish giver of pardon" (lesson's translations; [*lex orandi*](#lex-orandi)).

**The other side.** Melanchthon's *Apology* (May 1531), art. III: works merit "other rewards", not grace, justification or eternal life; *meritum condigni* takes from Christ his perpetual mediation and leaves consciences without peace. The 1999 texts on merit (eternal life an unmerited reward; the Response's "grace and reward together"; the Annex's reward of grace) are [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)'s, as is can. 24's "merely the fruits and signs" set beside §39's "fruits and signs".

**Level:** that the justified truly merit, their works both gifts and merits, **rung 1** (can. 32; *Firmiter*); cans. 24, 26 and 31, **rung 1**; no merit precedes the first grace, **rung 1** (Orange II can. 18; Trent VI cans. 1-3, ch. 8); ch. 16's grounds, **at least rung 3** (its pairing of gift and merit is rung 1 inside can. 32); condign, congruous and merit for others, **rung 4 at most**, words no act uses (CCC 2010 locates without raising).

**Does not assert:** that merit is a late concession or "a pious way of speaking"; that a work is worth heaven by itself.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md)

### Jansenism

The seventeenth- and eighteenth-century movement named for Cornelius Jansen's *Augustinus* (1640), which read Augustine's grace as an irresistible delight and bred a sacramental rigorism.

**The system** ([5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)): after the fall the will follows whichever delight is stronger; grace is the heavenly delight, which wins when stronger and, when weaker, was never enough. So no one is given enough grace and refuses it, and a just man who falls simply lacked it. Grace received becomes a mark of election to be guarded; communion, a reward for the proven, not food for the weak. Condemned in [the five propositions](#five-propositions) (1653); the quarrel over the book in [droit and fait](#droit-and-fait), [respectful silence](#respectful-silence) and the [formulary](#formulary); the pastoral face in [rigorism](#rigorism). [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md) cites its corollary on fallen nature (Quesnel's props. 38-40: without grace the sinner is free only for evil).

**People and dates.** Jansen (1585-1638), bishop of Ypres, died before the *Augustinus* appeared (Louvain, 1640); *In eminenti* banned the book (1642). Antoine Arnauld (1612-1694), *On Frequent Communion* (1643), letters of 1655; Pascal's *Provincial Letters* (1656-57). Port-Royal's nuns dispersed in 1709, the house razed in 1711. Quesnel and [*Unigenitus*](#unigenitus-1713) (1713); its French sequel is [`church-history` 8.1](../church-history/lessons/08-01-enlightenment-revolution-and-napoleon.md)'s. The Jansenist Synod of Pistoia (1786) called limbo a Pelagian fable, a charge *Auctorem fidei* (1794) censured ([7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md); [limbo](#limbo)).

**Said plainly:** an attempt to be more Augustinian than the Church. The Holy Office's decree of 7 December 1690 condemned holding a doctrine clearly grounded in Augustine "without regard to any papal bull" (prop. 30, lesson's translation).

**Level:** the contradictories of props. 1-3, the denial of prop. 4's charge, and the denial that Christ died for the predestined only, **rung 1**; that the five are in the *Augustinus* in Jansen's sense, **rung 2** ([dogmatic fact](#dogmatic-fact)); the globally censured propositions of 1690 and 1713, **at least rung 3**, no precise note; the oath, *In eminenti*, and the decrees of 1905 and 1910, **law, no rung**; Thomist efficacious grace, **rung 5**, never condemned with Jansen.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md), [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

### Unigenitus (1713)

Clement XI's bull *Unigenitus Dei Filius* (8 September 1713), condemning 101 propositions of Pasquier Quesnel's *Moral Reflections on the New Testament*.

[4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md) cites props. 38-40 against total corruption: the sinner is free only for evil without the Liberator's grace; without grace we can love nothing except to our condemnation. The bull condemns all 101 by [global censure](#in-globo-censure), notes from "false" to "heretical" applied "respectively", as renewing Jansen's heresies ([5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)). Its French sequel is [`church-history` 8.1](../church-history/lessons/08-01-enlightenment-revolution-and-napoleon.md)'s.

**Level:** each proposition excluded by an authentic act, **at least rung 3**, with no precise note on any one (5.6; 4.2 writes the contrary of props. 38-40 as **rung 3**). "All 101 heretical" overstates; "settles nothing" understates.

**Keep apart:** Clement VI's *Unigenitus* (1343) teaches the treasury ([`theology-of-debt` 3.5](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-05-the-christian-jubilee-and-the-treasury.md)). Always give pope and year.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md), [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)

### Adoptive sonship

Being made a son of God by grace: not a legal fiction but a gift that makes the adopted fit for the inheritance and like the natural Son.

"You have received the spirit of adoption of sons" (Rom 8:15, DR); "if sons, heirs also" (8:17; cf. Gal 4:5-7); Greek *huiothesia*. Trent VI ch. 4: justification is a translation into "the state of grace, and of the adoption of the sons of God". Aquinas: a human parent can only choose someone already fit; God's adoption makes the adopted fit (ST III q.23 a.1). We are begotten "by grace, not by nature" (a.2); adoption is the whole Trinity's work, [appropriated](#appropriation) to the Father as author, the Son as exemplar, the Spirit as imprinting the likeness (ad 3). The dignity of the grace that makes us adopted heirs is what makes merit condign (q.114 a.3; [condign and congruous merit](#condign-and-congruous-merit)). The Son is Son by nature; we are sons by a gift that makes us like him ([divinization](#divinization)).

**Level:** the fact, **rung 1** (Scripture's plain statement, proposed as revealed by the ordinary universal magisterium); ch. 4, **at least rung 3**; Aquinas's analysis, **rung 4**.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md)

### Appropriation

Ascribing to one divine Person a work common to all three, because of a likeness to what is proper to that Person.

The rule is [`trinity-and-god` 4.5](../trinity-and-god/lessons/04-05-common-proper-appropriated.md)'s; [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md) applies it. Adoption is appropriated to the Father, Son and Spirit (ST III q.23 a.2 ad 3). The [indwelling](#indwelling): Leo XIII calls it the whole Trinity's work, "attributed" especially to the Spirit (DIM §9); Thomists read this as appropriation, charity likening the soul to the Spirit, who is Love (I q.43 a.5 ad 2). Contrast a mission, which is proper: the Father dwells by grace as truly as the Son and the Spirit but is never *sent*, since sending says origin from another (I q.43 a.5; [`trinity-and-god` 6.1](../trinity-and-god/lessons/06-01-the-missions-of-the-son-and-the-spirit.md)). The rival: relations of the graced soul proper to each Person (Scheeben; Rahner, [quasi-formal causality](#quasi-formal-causality)).

**Level:** the indwelling as the Trinity's work, attributed to the Spirit and common "insofar as they have God as supreme efficient cause", **rung 3** (DIM §9; MC §78); reading it as appropriation, **rung 4**; proper relations to each Person, **rung 5**. Whether God's relation to the graced soul is exhausted by efficient causality, no act says.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md)

### Indwelling

The divine Persons themselves present in the soul in grace, as the known in the knower and the loved in the lover, not merely God's presence everywhere as cause.

"We will come to him and will make our abode with him" (John 14:23, DR); 1 Cor 3:16; Rom 5:5. Aquinas (ST I q.43 a.3): beyond God's presence in all things as cause, a special mode, "as the object known is in the knower, and the beloved in the lover", God dwelling "as in His own temple"; its only ground is sanctifying grace. The whole Trinity dwells; the Father is not sent (a.5; [appropriation](#appropriation)). [*Divinum Illud Munus*](#divinum-illud-munus) §9: the Spirit gives himself, and this union differs from the blessed's only in degree or state. [*Mystici Corporis*](#mystici-corporis) §§78-80: explanations are not censured, within two limits.

**The explanations** ([4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md)'s map): Thomist, founded on created grace and appropriated to the Spirit, **rung 4**; Rahner, uncreated grace first, [quasi-formal](#quasi-formal-causality), **rung 5**; Matthias Joseph Scheeben (1835-1888), now usually read as each Person present in his own character, **rung 5**; Petavius (Denis Pétau, 1583-1652), a union proper to the Spirit alone, **rung 5**. Excluded, **rung 1**: becoming one substance with God (*Dei Filius*, canons on God the Creator, can. 3).

**Level:** the fact, **rung 1** (John 14:23, Scripture's plain statement proposed as revealed by the ordinary universal magisterium); the encyclicals' precisions, **rung 3**; the school question, **rung 5 inside two rung-3 limits**.

**Keep apart:** omnipresence turned up. God is in a stone as its cause; he dwells in the just as the known in the knower, a mode only grace gives ([`trinity-and-god` 1.4](../trinity-and-god/lessons/01-04-immutability-eternity-immensity.md)). And the Thomist account is not the Church's because MC §79 uses its formula and cites q.43 a.3: §78 declines to censure the others.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md)

### Divinum Illud Munus

Leo XIII's encyclical on the Holy Spirit (9 May 1897).

§9 (paraphrased): the Spirit gives not only his gifts but himself, the supreme Gift; this union differs from the blessed's only in degree or state; it is the whole Trinity's work, attributed especially to the Spirit. [*Mystici Corporis*](#mystici-corporis) §80 quotes it. Used for [uncreated grace](#uncreated-and-created-grace), the [indwelling](#indwelling) and [appropriation](#appropriation).

**Level:** an encyclical, authentic ordinary teaching, **rung 3**; it defines no term. It is quoted against the picture of grace as "spiritual energy" held in a reservoir apart from souls ([4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md) P3).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md)

### Mystici Corporis

Pius XII's encyclical on the Mystical Body (29 June 1943); this course uses only §§78-80, on the indwelling.

Paraphrased: the mystery is veiled; those who explain it by various reasonings are not censured, within two limits. **No union** takes the faithful beyond the order of creatures ([`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 1.3](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/01-03-the-body-of-christ.md), which owns the encyclical as ecclesiology, reads this limit). **These activities are common** to the Trinity "insofar as they have God as supreme efficient cause". §79 restates and cites ST I q.43 a.3; §80 quotes Leo XIII.

**Level:** **rung 3**. Because it has spoken, the school question of the indwelling is **rung 5 inside two rung-3 limits**, not simply "freely disputed". It condemned no theory, Petavius's included.

**Does not assert:** that the Thomist account is the Church's: borrowing a school's formula in §79 does not lift it to rung 3; it stays **rung 4**.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md)

### Quasi-formal causality

Rahner's term for God giving himself to the creature not by producing an effect in it but somewhat as a form gives itself to what it informs.

Karl Rahner, "Some Implications of the Scholastic Concept of Uncreated Grace" (*Zeitschrift für katholische Theologie* 63, 1939; paraphrased). His foothold is Aquinas on the vision, where the divine essence is united to the intellect "as the object actually understood" (ST I q.12 a.2 ad 3), almost as a form, not as an efficient cause. Grace is glory begun; so God's self-gift comes first, created grace disposes for it, and the graced are related to each Person as himself, though the three stay one efficient cause. "Quasi" marks the analogy. On Rahner's line, MC §78's "insofar as" leaves room for a non-efficient relation proper to each Person ([4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md) Ex 2).

**Level:** **rung 5**, a permitted opinion inside MC §78's limits; Rahner as author, **no magisterial level**.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md)

### Infused virtues

Virtues given by God with grace and ordered to the supernatural end, as distinct from virtues acquired by practice.

ST I-II q.110 a.3: grace is to the infused virtues as the natural light of reason is to the acquired; the virtues are "derived from and are ordained to this light"; a.4 seats grace in the soul's essence and the virtues in its powers. A virtue presupposes a nature, so virtues ordered to God as he is need a nature that high, and grace supplies one by participation. Trent VI ch. 7: faith, hope and charity "infused at once"; can. 11: charity poured forth and inherent. Charity is merit's chief principle, and the acts of other virtues merit as commanded by it (q.114 a.4; [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md)). Infused virtue can sit beside old difficulty, so a flat morning after baptism is no evidence against it ([4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md) Ex 1). Owned by [`moral-theology` 3.1](../moral-theology/lessons/03-01-virtue-acquired-and-infused.md)-[3.3](../moral-theology/lessons/03-03-the-cardinal-virtues-and-the-gifts.md).

**Level:** charity inherent, **rung 1** (can. 11); the three theological virtues "infused at once", **at least rung 3** (ch. 7); the moral virtues infused, **rung 4**; the gifts as permanent dispositions, **rung 3**; grace really distinct from charity (Thomists, Suárez) or one with it (Scotus, Bellarmine), **rung 5**; grace seated in the soul's essence, **rung 5**.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md)

### Formal cause

That by which a thing is what it is from within, as whiteness makes a wall white; in justification, the justice by which the sinner is made just.

**The background** ([4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md)): grace acts on the soul "after the manner of a formal cause" (ST I-II q.110 a.2 ad 1), not as an efficient cause.

**The sentence** (Trent VI ch. 7, Waterworth): "the alone formal cause is the justice of God, not that whereby He Himself is just, but that whereby He maketh us just"; and "we are not only reputed, but are truly called, and are, just". [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md) reads each phrase against a rival: "alone" (*unica*) against [double justice](#double-justice); "not that whereby He Himself is just", Augustine's phrase ([`new-testament` 5.2](../new-testament/lessons/05-02-romans-1-4-the-righteousness-of-god.md)), against God's own essential justice as our form, [Osiander](#osiander)'s thesis; "not only reputed" against a justice of [imputation](#imputation) only. Can. 11 condemns justification by "the sole imputation of the justice of Christ", or by remission alone, excluding inherent grace and charity, and calling justifying grace "only the favour of God"; can. 10 condemns saying we are just without Christ's justice, or "formally just" by that justice itself, which reaches any account, Lutheran or Catholic, that makes Christ's justice, imputed, our form. The other four causes: [causes of justification](#causes-of-justification).

**Malloy's critique** ([5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)): Trent's single formal cause is a justice inherent in the soul; the Joint Declaration's Catholic paragraphs never name it.

**Level:** the substance of the formal-cause sentence, **rung 1** (cans. 10-11); the word "alone", **at least rung 3** (it stands only in the chapter); grace as a quality acting formally, **rung 4**.

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)

### Gratuity of the supernatural

The vision of God, and the grace ordered to it, are owed to no created nature, fallen or unfallen.

**Two layers** ([4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md)). *Unowed to merit*, as a pardon is unowed to a debtor who forfeited his claim: [Orange II](#orange-ii) can. 18: "a reward is owed to good works, if they are done", but grace, "which is not owed (*quae non debetur*), goes before, that they may be done" (lesson's translation); Trent VI ch. 8. *Unowed to nature as such*, as a king's friendship is unowed to the most loyal subject, even sinless Adam: Pius V's *Ex omnibus afflictionibus* (1 October 1567) condemned, among seventy-nine propositions from [Baius](#baius), that the raising of human nature into fellowship with the divine "was owed to the integrity of its first condition" (prop. 21), that the first man's happiness "would be a wage, and not grace" (prop. 3), and that God could not have created man as he is now born (prop. 55). [*Humani Generis*](#humani-generis) §26 (12 August 1950): some "destroy the gratuity of the supernatural order" by holding that God cannot create intellectual beings without ordering and calling them to the vision: a thesis about God's freedom, a *cannot*. [*Dei Filius*](#dei-filius) (1870) ch. 2: God "of his infinite goodness" has ordained man to a supernatural end; can. II.3: man can be raised above nature and does not reach it of himself. *Gaudium et Spes* 22: the ultimate vocation is "in fact one, and divine".

**Open (rung 5):** what kind of [natural desire](#natural-desire-to-see-god) nature has ([innate and elicited desire](#innate-and-elicited-desire)); whether [pure nature](#pure-nature) was possible.

**Level:** man can be raised above nature, not of himself, **rung 1** (*Dei Filius* can. II.3); unowed to merit, **rung 1** (Orange II can. 18; Trent VI cans. 1-3, restated in ch. 8); unowed even to unfallen nature, **at least rung 3** (1567, notes applied *respective*); God could create intellectual beings uncalled, **rung 3** (HG 26, a deliberate judgment its own §20 says closes the matter); the human vocation in fact divine, **at least rung 3** (GS 22; *Dei Filius* ch. 2).

**Keep apart:** "sinners don't deserve heaven" is only the first layer, and Baius held exactly it. "Beyond nature's powers" is not "not owed to nature": a wage paid by another exceeds your powers and is still owed (Baius prop. 11 made eternal life owed to obedience by natural law). A disputed desire does not make a disputed gift. "Of his infinite goodness" names the motive of a free act, not a necessity.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md)

### Natural desire to see God

Aquinas's claim that a mind which knows God's effects naturally desires to know what God is, a desire only the vision satisfies.

ST I q.12 a.1, respondeo: "there resides in every man a natural desire to know the cause of any effect which he sees"; if the intellect could not reach the first cause, "the natural desire would remain void". The article's target is an **impossibility** thesis, that no created intellect *can* see God's essence; it answers from faith and from reason. I-II q.3 a.8 runs the same way (wonder arising from effects seen), SCG III.50-57 at length. Both sides accept these texts and dispute what "natural" and "void" mean. The commentators lean on I-II q.5 a.5 ad 1: nature gave man not the wherewithal to attain happiness, but free will to turn to God, "that He may make him happy". CCC 27 (the desire for God written in the heart) locates a teaching and fixes no level.

**The readings:** [innate and elicited desire](#innate-and-elicited-desire); [obediential potency](#obediential-potency); [supernatural existential](#supernatural-existential). The apologetic argument from desire is [`apologetics-foundations` 8.3](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/08-03-apologetics-in-a-secular-age.md)'s.

**Level:** Aquinas as author, **no magisterial level**; the desire's kind, **rung 5**. The vision's being unowed is fixed (rungs 1 and 3; [gratuity of the supernatural](#gratuity-of-the-supernatural)).

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md)

### Innate and elicited desire

An innate desire is a tendency built into a nature before any knowledge; an elicited desire is an act of will woken by prior knowledge.

**The commentators**, from Cajetan (1469-1534) through Suárez to Réginald Garrigou-Lagrange (1877-1964): a nature is specified by an end proportioned to its powers, and "man's happiness is twofold" (I-II q.62 a.1). So the desire for the vision is elicited, woken by knowledge: of supernatural effects, said Cajetan; of God's existence, said Báñez, for whom it is also **conditional**, to see God if he grants it. Lawrence Feingold, *The Natural Desire to See God* (2001; 2nd ed. 2010): q.12 a.1 proves the vision possible, not due; q.3 a.8's desire is wonder, elicited by God's effects; only knowledge makes a desire conditional, so an innate one would be an absolute demand; and a conditional desire is not "void" when unmet. **Henri de Lubac**: innate and absolute; one nature, one end, the gratuity creation's ([pure nature](#pure-nature)). **Rahner**: an unconditional ordering, but not from nature ([supernatural existential](#supernatural-existential)).

Both vocabularies are in use: "innate or elicited" ([`moral-theology` 1.2](../moral-theology/lessons/01-02-beatitude-the-last-end.md)), and "natural and absolute or conditional and elicited".

**Level:** **rung 5** in every form; the authors, **no magisterial level**.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md)

### Obediential potency

A creature's capacity to receive from God what its nature can neither produce nor demand.

For the commentators and Feingold it is what answers the vision in human nature: not a desire that exacts fulfilment but a capacity to be raised ([4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md)). On that reading the desire for the vision is elicited and conditional, and [pure nature](#pure-nature) is possible.

**Level:** school vocabulary, **no magisterial level**; the reading it serves, **rung 5**.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md)

### Pure nature

The commentators' thesis that God could have created humanity with only a natural final end, proportioned to its powers, and no call to the vision.

Cajetan, Suárez, Garrigou-Lagrange; today Steven A. Long, *Natura Pura* (2010), defending nature's own end and law. The picture is the *duplex ordo*: a proportioned natural end, with a supernatural end beyond it. Henri de Lubac (*Surnaturel*, 1946) called pure nature a sixteenth-century device read back into Aquinas; he granted (1949; *The Mystery of the Supernatural*, 1965) that God could make a spirit uncalled, but it would be another being than I. John Milbank (*The Suspended Middle*, 2005) radicalized him; Nicholas Healy (*Communio*, 2008) defended him. In June 1950 his Jesuit superiors removed him from teaching and withdrew *Surnaturel*; *Humani Generis* followed two months later, naming no one ([`thomistic-synthesis` 6.4](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/06-04-transcendental-thomism-and-the-ressourcement-quarrel.md)). He was made a cardinal in 1983.

**What the acts say.** HG §26 protects a possibility, that God could create intellectual beings uncalled (**rung 3**); it never says "pure nature", leaves the desire's kind alone, and speaks of "intellectual beings", not this race. Baius prop. 55, that God could not have created man as he is now born, is condemned. Aquinas's I-II q.62 a.1 asserts no state of pure nature: its proportioned happiness points back to the "imperfect happiness that can be had in this life" of q.5 a.5 ([5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md) Flashback).

**Bearing on limbo.** 4.4: a limbo of natural happiness presupposes that a natural end is possible. 7.4 qualifies: the ITC's 2007 study distinguishes Aquinas's limbo from the later idea of pure nature (§23), so limbo is not pure nature ([limbo](#limbo)).

**Level:** **rung 5**, a school thesis no act proposes; the 1950 measures, discipline, **no rung**.

**Does not assert:** that *Humani Generis* defined pure nature or condemned de Lubac (overstated).

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md), [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

### Supernatural existential

Rahner's name for God's offer of himself, given to every human being with creation and shaping existence before any response, owed to no one and no part of nature.

Karl Rahner's 1950 reply in *Orientierung* (English in *Theological Investigations* I) granted de Lubac that every human being is unconditionally ordered to the vision, and denied that the ordering is nature: a part of nature that God's goodness could not frustrate would make grace owed. So it is an existential, a feature of concrete existence, and supernatural, given to everyone and owed to no one. Nature is a remainder concept, never met alone; and, against de Lubac, the same "I" could have existed uncalled. It shares de Lubac's fact (the unconditional ordering), not its source.

**Level:** **rung 5**; Rahner as author, **no magisterial level**.

**Keep apart:** it is supernatural, not "natural openness to God"; calling it that inverts it.

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md)

### Light of glory

The created light God gives the intellect so that it can see his essence.

The term is conciliar: Vienne's [*Ad nostrum*](#ad-nostrum) (1311-12) condemned among the Beghards' errors (prop. 5) that the soul needs no light of glory raising it (*lumine gloriae, ipsam elevante*) to see God (lesson's translation). Aquinas: the light strengthens the intellect "not as a medium in which God is seen, but as one by which He is seen" (ST I q.12 a.5 ad 2; [`thomistic-synthesis` 4.2](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/04-02-knowing-god-from-creatures-the-three-ways.md)), so it squares with [*Benedictus Deus*](#benedictus-deus)'s exclusion of a creature mediating only as the thing seen. **Owned by** [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md); [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md) points forward to it.

**Level:** that a light of glory is needed, **at least rung 3** (the censure falls on the sect's errors together, no note on this one: default down); its core, that no creature reaches the vision by its own power, **rung 1** (*Dei Filius* can. II.3); what the light is, a created habit (I q.12 a.5), **rung 4**.

**Does not assert:** that Vienne defined the light of glory, as the 1913 *Catholic Encyclopedia* says (overstated); nor is it "Thomist jargon" (understated: the term is the council's).

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md)

### Limbo

The hypothesis that infants who die with original sin only lack the vision of God but suffer no pain of sense; on Aquinas's version, with a natural happiness and no grief.

**History** ([7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md), which owns it). Augustine: such infants "will be involved in the mildest condemnation of all" (*On the Merits and Remission of Sins* I.21). The medieval schools cut the penalty to the loss of the [vision](#beatific-vision); Aquinas added natural happiness without grief (early *Sentences* commentary, the Supplement's Appendix 1 q.1: "no wise man grieves for being unable to fly like a bird"; *De malo* q.5 a.3: they do not know what they lack). Named around 1200 (ITC 2007, §24) from *limbus*, the border of the underworld: *limbus puerorum*. Suárez: Christ's healing grace makes that happiness possible (ITC §90). Not the [limbo of the fathers](#limbo-of-the-fathers), which is taught; this was always a hypothesis.

**The acts.** The [*solo originali* clause](#solo-originali-clause) (Lyons II, 1274; Florence, 1439) sends those who die "with original sin only" to hell, to unlike punishments, naming no infant and no limbo. [*Auctorem fidei*](#auctorem-fidei) (28 August 1794), prop. 26, censured as "false, rash, injurious to the Catholic schools" the Jansenist Synod of Pistoia's charge that limbo is a Pelagian fable.
