Moves to define never reached a vote; the 1970 Missal gave a funeral Mass for children whose parents meant to baptise them; the CDF's *Pastoralis actio* (1980) §13: the Church can only entrust them to God's mercy; CCC 1261: we may hope. The [ITC's 2007 study](#itc-2007) concludes to grounds for hope, not knowledge; limbo remains possible, yet "problematic".

**Level:** limbo **never entered the dogmatic definitions**, though the Magisterium at times mentioned it in ordinary teaching until Vatican II: say "never defined", not "never taught". Its permissibility, **at least rung 3** (*Auctorem fidei*, a censure below heresy); that unbaptised infants lack the vision, **rung 4** (common teaching) until the mid-twentieth century, now **rung 5**; that any infant dies with original sin only, **rung 5**; the ITC, **no magisterial level**.

**Keep apart:** 4.4's point that a limbo of natural happiness presupposes a possible natural end; and 7.4's that Aquinas's limbo is not the later [pure nature](#pure-nature) (ITC §23).

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md), [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

**Justification, merit, and predestination (Module 5)**

### Justification

The change by which a sinner, an enemy of God, becomes just and his friend.

Trent VI ch. 4: a translation from the state of the first Adam into "the state of grace, and of the adoption of the sons of God"; ch. 7: "not remission of sins merely, but also the sanctification and renewal of the inward man". **Court and hospital** ([5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md)): for the Lutheran confessions justification is above all a verdict, God counting the believer righteous for Christ's sake, the renewal that follows real but not what makes him acceptable; for Trent it is verdict and cure in one act, sins forgiven *because* a justice is given that becomes truly the person's own while remaining wholly God's gift. Faith is, for the Reformers, the hand that takes the verdict; for Trent, "the beginning of human salvation, the foundation, and the root of all Justification" (ch. 8), of a justice that also needs hope and charity, without which faith "neither unites man perfectly with Christ" (ch. 7).

**The decree's path** ([Trent Session VI](#trent-session-vi), 13 January 1547). Preparation under [prevenient grace](#prevenient-grace) (ch. 5-6; cans. 4-9: the moved will can refuse; not all works before justification are sins, can. 7; the fear of hell that drives a sinner to mercy is no sin, can. 8). What justification is, and its [causes](#causes-of-justification) (ch. 4, 7; cans. 10-11; [formal cause](#formal-cause)). Faith, and "freely", since nothing that precedes, "whether faith or works", merits it (ch. 8). [Certainty](#certainty-of-grace) (ch. 9; cans. 13-14). Increase by works in grace (ch. 10; can. 24; [`new-testament` 6.3](../new-testament/lessons/06-03-james-faith-and-works.md)); loss by any mortal sin, even where faith remains (ch. 15; [`moral-theology` 3.4](../moral-theology/lessons/03-04-sin-mortal-and-venial.md)); recovery through penance (ch. 14; [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 4.1](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/04-01-penance-the-sacrament-and-its-history.md)). Predestination and perseverance (ch. 12-13; [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md)); [merit](#merit) (ch. 16). Two fronts: against Pelagius, cans. 1-3; against the Reformers, inherence (can. 11) and real consent (can. 4).

**Faith alone.** Trent says we are "justified by faith" (ch. 8). Can. 9 condemns "faith alone" in a stated sense (nothing else co-operates, no preparation of the will); can. 12, justifying faith as mere confidence in mercy. Whether a given Lutheran text falls under a canon is a further question ([5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md); [application of a condemnation](#application-of-a-condemnation)).

**Level:** the canons, **rung 1**; a chapter claim no canon repeats, **at least rung 3** ([chapters and canons](#chapters-and-canons)), so faith as beginning, foundation and root (ch. 8) is **at least rung 3**; ch. 4's "or the desire thereof", presupposed, **not rung 1** ([`sacraments-and-liturgy` 2.2](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/02-02-necessity-infants-and-baptism-of-desire-and-blood.md)); the confessions, Luther and Melanchthon, **no magisterial level**.

**Does not assert:** that faith does not justify; nor does co-operation make Trent semi-Pelagian, since the assent of ch. 5 is to grace that comes first and can. 3 makes the whole preparation grace-borne.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md)

### Causes of justification

Trent VI ch. 7 names five causes of justification: final, efficient, meritorious, instrumental, and one formal cause.

As [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md) names them: final; efficient; meritorious, Christ's passion, by which he merited justification and made satisfaction (read in [`christology` 5.3](../christology/lessons/05-03-satisfaction-anselm-and-aquinas.md)); instrumental, baptism, "the sacrament of faith"; and the single [formal cause](#formal-cause), the justice of God by which he makes us just. The words that identify each of the others stand in ch. 7 itself; read them there.

**Level:** ch. 7 is chapter teaching, **at least rung 3**; the substance of its formal-cause sentence, **rung 1** through cans. 10-11.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md)

### Imputation

Righteousness credited to a person's account rather than given to him as his own.

The Augsburg Confession (1530), art. IV (Schaff, *Creeds* vol. 3): men are justified freely for Christ's sake through faith, and "this faith doth God impute for righteousness before him". Melanchthon's *Apology* (1531), IV.71-72: "by faith itself, we are for Christ's sake accounted righteous". Trent VI can. 11 condemns justification "by the sole imputation of the justice of Christ", or by remission alone, to the exclusion of inherent grace and charity; ch. 7's "not only reputed, but are truly called, and are, just" excludes a justice of imputation only ([formal cause](#formal-cause)). In 1999 the test of a Lutheran explication is whether it keeps what the canon's exclusive words ("sole", "to the exclusion of", "only") deny ([differentiated consensus](#differentiated-consensus)).

**Level:** can. 11, **rung 1**; the confessions, **no magisterial level**.

**Keep apart:** Trent condemns *sole* imputation, not every use of the word.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md)

### Simul iustus et peccator

Luther's "at the same time righteous and sinner": wholly just by God's reckoning in Christ, and still truly a sinner in oneself.

Luther, *Commentary on Galatians* (1535), on 3:6 (English translation of 1575, Philadelphia reprint 1891, p. 227): God "counteth my sin for no sin, which notwithstanding is sin indeed", so that "a Christian man is both righteous and a sinner, holy and profane". The counter-text is Trent V can. 5 (1546): baptism takes away the whole of what has the true and proper nature of sin, not "only rased, or not imputed", and the [concupiscence](#concupiscence) left in the reborn is not truly and properly sin ([Trent Session V](#trent-session-v); [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md)). The crux: whether what remains in the justified is truly sin ([5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md)).

**In 1998-99** ([5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)): the [1998 Response](#catholic-response-1998) called the *simul* as JD §29 explains it "not acceptable", its first and weightiest point, since baptism takes away all that is truly sin. The [Annex](#official-common-statement-and-annex) gives a limited shared *simul*: the justified are truly renewed and "do not remain sinners in this sense", yet none is without sin (1 John 1:8).

**Level:** V can. 5, **rung 1**; the Annex's limited shared sense and the Lutheran concept of sin as whatever the law judges, **no magisterial level**; whether the Annex's *simul* escapes V can. 5 is part of whether JD §41 is right, **rung 5**.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)

### Double justice

The proposal that the justified have a real inherent justice but, since it is imperfect, must also rely at the judgment on Christ's justice imputed to them.

*Duplex iustitia*. A twofold justice stood in article 5 at Regensburg, agreed on 2 May 1541 by Catholic theologians under the legate Gasparo Contarini, Johann Gropper among them, with Melanchthon and Bucer; Rome rejected the article that June. At Trent in autumn 1546 Girolamo Seripando, prior general of the Augustinians, pressed a version of it; the Jesuit Diego Laínez answered it on 26 October 1546, and the council chose "alone": "the alone formal cause" (*unica formalis causa*, VI ch. 7). Seripando was never censured: Pius IV made him a cardinal and a legate of the same council, where he died in 1563.

**Level:** a Catholic opinion before 1547, excluded since: by ch. 7's "alone" (**at least rung 3**), with can. 10 (**rung 1**) reaching any account, Lutheran or Catholic, that makes Christ's justice, imputed, our formal justice. Contarini, Gropper, Seripando, **no magisterial level**.

**Keep apart:** "the alone formal cause" was written against Catholics, not only against Protestants.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md)

### Osiander

Andreas Osiander held that we are just by God's own essential righteousness: Christ's divine righteousness dwelling in us.

Nuremberg, 1525: "the one and only righteousness availing before God is God Himself" (Bente's translation, *Concordia Triglotta*, 1921, Historical Introductions §176). His view became a public storm at Königsberg from 1549, after Trent's decree (13 January 1547). Trent VI ch. 7's "not that whereby He Himself is just", a phrase of Augustine's, excludes his thesis logically: God's own essential justice is not our form ([formal cause](#formal-cause)). [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md) does not claim the drafters aimed at him.

**Level:** Osiander, **no magisterial level**; the clause that excludes him belongs to the formal-cause sentence, whose substance is **rung 1** through cans. 10-11.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md)

### Joint Declaration

The joint text of the Lutheran World Federation and the Catholic Church, finished in 1997 and confirmed at Augsburg on 31 October 1999, which claims a consensus in basic truths of justification and that the sixteenth-century condemnations do not apply to each other's teaching as presented in it.

**What it claims** ([5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)). A consensus in basic truths, not agreement on all (§5). The core, §15, confessed together: God accepts us by grace alone, through faith in what Christ has done and not for any merit of ours, and gives the Spirit, who renews the heart for good works. §18 calls justification "an indispensable criterion". §§19-39 treat seven disputed topics, each confessed together, then explicated by each side. §40: the remaining differences "of language, theological elaboration, and emphasis" are acceptable. §41: the condemnations "appear in a new light"; the Lutheran teaching presented here does not fall under Trent's, nor the Catholic under the Lutheran Confessions'.

**What it leaves standing.** §1 calls the condemnations still valid and church-dividing (its starting point); §7 neither takes them lightly nor disavows the past; §42 takes nothing from their seriousness: some were "not simply pointless", and they remain "salutary warnings"; §43 leaves open the word of God and church doctrine, ecclesiology, authority, church unity, ministry, the sacraments and social ethics.

**The method:** [differentiated consensus](#differentiated-consensus); a canon condemns a proposition in its stated sense, so withdrawing a warrant and checking whether someone fits its description are two acts ([application of a condemnation](#application-of-a-condemnation)). **The exchange:** [Catholic Response (1998)](#catholic-response-1998), [Official Common Statement and Annex](#official-common-statement-and-annex). **The critics:** German Protestant theology teachers (a Tübingen statement of 6 January 1998, about 140 signatures, and a faculty opinion of 17 February 1998 that the differences were only covered over by wording); Eberhard Jüngel (*Um Gottes willen — Klarheit!*, 1997: an "indispensable criterion" beside others gives away the one criterion); Christopher Malloy (*Engrafted into Christ*, 2005: the inherent [formal cause](#formal-cause) is missing, so the condemnations still apply). **Later associations:** World Methodist Council (Seoul, 23 July 2006); Anglican Consultative Council (April 2016, presented by Justin Welby at Westminster Abbey on 31 October 2017); World Communion of Reformed Churches (Wittenberg, 5 July 2017): each affirmed the consensus, none passed a verdict on Trent's canons. Its place among the Church's ecumenical acts is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 5.3](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/05-03-ecumenism-as-doctrine.md)'s.

**Level:** **no rung of its own**: a bilateral agreement, on no list of teaching genres, confirmed by an official act of the Catholic Church as a party; it defines and revokes nothing (can. 749 §3). Its Catholic paragraphs keep the weight of what they restate; Trent's canons stay **rung 1**, unrevoked. §41 is officially the Catholic Church's judgment as a party, so "Rome never said that" is false; but no act proposes it to be believed, held or adhered to, so whether it is correct is **freely disputed, rung 5**. Critics and associations, **no magisterial level**.

**Does not assert:** that 1999 revoked Trent's anathemas; that the signing made §41 doctrine. Nor is it "a mere theologians' paper".

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)

### Differentiated consensus

The method of stating a core together, then explaining each side's idiom so that its remaining differences are judged compatible with the core and not church-dividing.

Two steps ([5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)). The sides state a core together. Then each explains its own idiom: "When Lutherans say X, they do not thereby deny Y." That sentence form is aimed at canons that condemn "X alone, to the exclusion of Y", since an explanation that keeps Y does not assert the condemned proposition. What remains is judged a difference of language, elaboration and emphasis (§40). A worked case: the Annex's "by faith alone", with "we can and must cooperate", falls outside Trent VI can. 9, which condemns "faith alone" only "in such wise as to mean" (*ita ut intelligat*) no cooperation and no preparation of the will. Its limit: matching words is not matching claims (can. 24's "merely the fruits and signs" beside §39's "fruits and signs"); Trent defines the Catholic proposition, and cannot define what another tradition's word names.

**The name** does not occur in the 1999 texts: it is the ecumenists'; the LWF credits the Lutheran ecumenist Harding Meyer with developing the method; the Reformed association of 2017 welcomed the model of differentiating consensus. Whether the method is false irenicism is asked in [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 5.3](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/05-03-ecumenism-as-doctrine.md).

**Level:** a method and its name, **no magisterial level**.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)

### Official Common Statement and Annex

The short statement, with an annex of elucidations, by whose signing at Augsburg on 31 October 1999 the Catholic Church and the LWF confirmed the Joint Declaration "in its entirety".

**Signatories:** for the Catholic Church, Cardinal Edward Cassidy and Bishop Walter Kasper, president and secretary of the Pontifical Council for Promoting Christian Unity (PCPCU); for the LWF, Bishop Christian Krause and Ishmael Noko, president and general secretary, with its vice-presidents. The LWF Council had adopted a resolution on the text on 16 June 1998. John Paul II hailed the signing that day as a milestone.

**The Annex** takes up all of the [1998 Response](#catholic-response-1998)'s points but penance: a limited shared [*simul*](#simul-iustus-et-peccator) (the justified are truly renewed and "do not remain sinners in this sense", yet none is without sin, 1 John 1:8); [concupiscence](#concupiscence) as a word used in different senses; grace alone and "by faith alone", with cooperation by the Spirit's power (quoting the Formula of Concord, "we can and must cooperate"); judgment on works and a reward of grace; the criterion set within the Trinitarian confession; each partner respecting the other's ordered process of doctrinal decision. It narrowed the reservations rather than abolishing them.

**Level:** an official act of the Catholic Church as a party to an agreement, **no rung**: it makes §41 officially the Church's judgment without proposing it as doctrine, and whether §41 holds is **rung 5**. The pope's endorsement defines nothing.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)

### Catholic Response (1998)

The Catholic Church's official note of 25 June 1998, prepared by the Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith with the PCPCU and signed by Cardinal Cassidy, granting a consensus in basic truths but not one removing every difference.

Its points, weightiest first ([5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)): (1) the [*simul iustus et peccator*](#simul-iustus-et-peccator) as JD §29 explains it is not acceptable, since baptism takes away all that is truly sin, and §22 leaves interior renewal unclear; (2) justification is a criterion within the rule of faith (§18); (3) consent is real *cooperatio*, not "mere passive" (§21), and eternal life is grace and reward together; (4) penance needs treating; (5) some differences concern substance, so §§40-41 cannot be affirmed without distinction; (6) what authority has the LWF's synodal consensus? The [Annex](#official-common-statement-and-annex) took up all but (4).

**Level:** its doctrine keeps the weight of what it restates (Trent V can. 5 and VI can. 4, **rung 1**); its assessments of the Declaration's wording are official judgments about an ecumenical text, **no rung of their own**.

**Does not assert:** a veto. A year later the Church signed, Annex attached, confirming the Declaration in its entirety; and the signature does not make every Lutheran paragraph Catholic teaching.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)

### Condign and congruous merit

Condign merit is merit in the strict sense, the reward matched to the act; congruous merit is fittingness without a claim in justice, as when God grants what a friend asks.

Aquinas (ST I-II q.114 a.3): an act as it proceeds from free will has only congruity; as it proceeds from "the grace of the Holy Ghost" it merits eternal life condignly, its worth depending "on the dignity of grace", the grace that makes us adopted heirs ([adoptive sonship](#adoptive-sonship)). Another's first grace only Christ merits condignly; the just merit it congruously (a.6), which leaves room for "an impediment on the part of him whose salvation the just man desires", so years of prayer and work for an estranged brother gave no claim, only hope, while each work merited the one who did it an increase of grace (a.8; [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md) Ex 2). Trent says "truly merit" (can. 32) and uses neither word. Melanchthon's *Apology* rejects *meritum condigni* as robbing Christ of his perpetual mediation ([merit](#merit)).

**Level:** the words, **no magisterial level**: no act uses them; that can. 32's "truly" means condign merit, the schools' reading, **rung 4**; congruous merit for another, **rung 4** (CCC 2010 locates merit for others without raising it).

**Keep apart:** "condign" does not mean an act worth heaven by itself, since from free will alone there is no condignity; and condign *merit* is not Christ's condign *satisfaction*, equal to the offence ([`christology` 1.3](../christology/lessons/01-03-why-the-incarnation.md)).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md)

### Divine ordination

The ground of merit before God: merit exists only because God has ordained acts to ends, so that God is debtor to his own ordering, not to us.

ST I-II q.114 a.1: between God and man there is no equality, so man's merit with God "only exists on the presupposition of the Divine ordination", and God is debtor only to himself (ad 3). The picture ([5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md)): a violin teacher takes on a child who cannot play, gives her instrument, lessons and a promise of a seat in his orchestra when she can play the concerto; when she plays it the seat is hers by right, yet instrument, training and promise were all his, and nothing before he took her on earned the lessons. "What hast thou that thou hast not received?" (1 Cor 4:7, DR): any owing starts on God's side. Trent's counterpart is eternal life rendered "according to the promise of God Himself" (VI ch. 16).

**Level:** Aquinas's explanation, **rung 4** (common teaching); ch. 16's promise, **at least rung 3**; that the justified truly merit, **rung 1** (can. 32). "Reward, not wage" and can. 32's level are graded in [`theology-of-debt` 2.4](../theology-of-debt/lessons/02-04-the-bond-and-the-ransom.md) and [3.2](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-02-the-fathers-alms-satisfaction-and-ransom.md).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md)

### Revival of merits

Good works done in charity and deadened by a later mortal sin recover their power toward eternal life when penance restores grace.

ST III q.89 aa.4-5 ([5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md)): works "deadened" by mortal sin revive with penance.

**Level:** the schools' teaching, **rung 4**; no act defines it.

**Keep apart:** restoration after a fall is itself never merited (ST I-II q.114 a.7); what revives is the merit of the earlier works, once penance has restored grace.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md)

### Guilt and punishment

Forgiveness lifts the guilt of sin and its eternal debt, while a debt of temporal punishment can remain, to be paid in this life or after death.

Trent VI can. 30 (1547): a debt of temporal punishment can remain after guilt is forgiven, "to be discharged either in this world, or in the next in Purgatory". Dying in charity before making satisfaction presupposes it ([6.1](lessons/06-01-death-and-the-particular-judgment.md)); [purgatory](#purgatory) purges what grace has forgiven but not finished ([6.3](lessons/06-03-purgatory.md)). The logic of the debt is [`theology-of-debt` 3.4](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-04-aquinas-satisfaction-and-the-debt-of-punishment.md)'s; what one member's works do for another's temporal punishment (satisfaction borne for another, the treasury, indulgences) is [`theology-of-debt` 3.4-3.6](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-04-aquinas-satisfaction-and-the-debt-of-punishment.md)'s, not merit's ([5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md)). Help for the dead: [suffrages](#suffrages).

**Level:** that a debt of temporal punishment can outlast forgiven guilt, **rung 1** (VI can. 30).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-death-and-the-particular-judgment.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-purgatory.md)

### Suffrages

Prayers, Masses, alms and other works offered by the living for the dead.

Owned by [6.3](lessons/06-03-purgatory.md). Florence's [*Laetentur caeli*](#laetentur-caeli) (6 July 1439), under its *diffinimus*: the souls purged after death are helped by the suffrages of the living. Trent's Session XXV decree (December 1563) reports that the Church has taught that the souls in purgatory "are helped by the suffrages of the faithful, but principally by the acceptable sacrifice of the altar". The Mass may be offered for the dead not yet fully purified (XXII ch. 2, can. 3, 1562; graded in [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 3.4](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/03-04-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md)). The living can help because the Church is one body across death ([communion of saints](#communion-of-saints)); merit, by contrast, belongs to this life (Augustine, *Enchiridion* 110; [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md)). Indulgences as a practice are [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 4.2](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/04-02-indulgences-as-a-practice.md)'s.

**Level:** that suffrages help, the Mass among them, **rung 1** (Florence; XXII can. 3); "principally" the Mass, **at least rung 3** (XXV); how a suffrage reaches a soul, **no act**; whether the souls pray for us (Aquinas: they are not in a condition to, II-II q.83 a.11 ad 3), a school's opinion.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-purgatory.md)

### Lex orandi

The law of prayer: the Church's worship as a witness to her faith.

The axiom is the [*Indiculus*](#indiculus)'s ch. 8, "that the law of praying establish the law of believing" (lesson's translation, [3.2](lessons/03-02-pelagius-augustine-and-the-councils.md)); the tag is [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 6.1](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md)'s. Its uses here:

- **Merit** ([5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md)). The first Preface of the Saints praises God who, "in crowning their merits", crowns "your own gifts" (*eorum coronando merita tua dona coronas*), the Catechism's epigraph before CCC 2006. The Roman Canon's *Nobis quoque peccatoribus* asks admission among the saints "not as an assessor of merit but as a lavish giver of pardon" (*non aestimator meriti, sed veniae ... largitor*; lesson's translations). Praise of merits as gifts and a sinner's plea for pardon, not a denial of merit.
- **Purgatory** ([6.3](lessons/06-03-purgatory.md)). The Church prayed for her dead long before she explained why: Judas's sacrifice "for the sins of the dead" (2 Macc 12:43, DR); Tertullian, "As often as the anniversary comes round, we make offerings for the dead" (*De corona* 3, c. 211); Jerusalem's Eucharist for all "who in past years have fallen asleep among us" (*Mystagogical Catecheses* 5.9, c. 350); Monica's "remember me at the Lord's altar" (*Confessions* IX.11.27).
- **Limbo** ([7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)). The 1970 Missal's funeral Mass for children whose parents meant to baptise them; the ITC counts the funeral prayer as evidence for hope (§100). The rite shows hope, not knowledge.

**Level:** prayers witness and define nothing, **no rung of their own**; a funeral rite, **law, no rung**; the *Indiculus* weighs by reception.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-purgatory.md), [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

### Predestination

God's eternal plan, part of his providence, to bring a rational creature to eternal life, with every means on the way, its free acts included.

"For whom he foreknew, he also predestinated to be made conformable to the image of his Son" (Rom 8:29, DR); he "predestinated us unto the adoption of children" (Eph 1:5). Aquinas: the plan directing a rational creature to eternal life, an end that "exceeds all proportion and faculty of created nature" (ST I q.23 a.1); what it puts in us is its carrying-out, calling and glory (Rom 8:30). Trent presupposes "the secret mystery of divine predestination" (VI ch. 12) without defining it. Not fate: the part of providence ([`thomistic-synthesis` 5.2](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/05-02-providence-and-secondary-causes.md)) concerned with the one end no creature reaches by its own power.

**The asymmetry** ([5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md)). If anyone is saved, the whole credit is God's; if anyone is lost, the whole blame is his own; and no one reads his own name in the plan. Quiercy (853): that some perish "is the desert of those who perish" (lesson's translation). Make it symmetrical and either God authors both outcomes or man does, which is Pelagius. *How* the first two fit, the Church has never said.

**The fence:** no predestination to sin ([reprobation](#reprobation)); the [universal salvific will](#universal-salvific-will); no certainty of one's own election ([certainty of election](#certainty-of-election)); inside it, open, [before or after foreseen merits](#before-or-after-foreseen-merits), negative reprobation, the number of the elect, and how grace wins consent ([de auxiliis](#de-auxiliis)). The plan runs through its means: prayer and works fall under it as the way it is carried out, so "the lazy argument" is the first error (I q.23 a.8; 2 Pet 1:10). Outside the fence, on 5.4's map: Calvin's "some are preordained to eternal life, others to eternal damnation" (*Institutes* III.21.5, tr. Beveridge), and *Cum occasione*'s fifth proposition. The doctrine that frightened the young Francis de Sales in Paris in the 1580s the Church teaches; the despair he drew from it she forbids.

**Level:** that God predestines, **rung 1** by the ordinary universal magisterium (Scripture says it in so many words and the Church has always proposed it as revealed; no canon defines it); that grace infallibly brings consent where God wills, held by both schools as following from predestination, **rung 4**; how many are elect, and Aquinas's "those who are saved are in the minority" (q.23 a.7 ad 3), **no level**.

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)

### Reprobation

God's eternal will, regarding those who are lost, to permit their fall into sin and to punish them for it; never a causing of the sin.

Aquinas (ST I q.23 a.3): "reprobation includes the will to permit a person to fall into sin, and to impose the punishment of damnation on account of that sin"; it causes abandonment and punishment, never the sin: "guilt proceeds from the free-will of the person who is reprobated" (ad 2). A will, not mere foreknowledge, of punishment. [Orange II](#orange-ii)'s conclusion (lesson's translation): that some "have been predestined to evil by divine power (*divina potestate*), we not only do not believe", and it condemns "with all detestation" any who would. Trent VI can. 17 reuses Orange's words: it condemns saying that justifying grace reaches only those "predestined unto life", the others being called but receiving no grace, "as being, by the divine power, predestined unto evil". Can. 6 condemns saying God works evil "not permissively only, but properly, and of Himself". CCC 1037: "God predestines no one to go to hell"; what damns is a wilful turning from God, persisted in to the end.

**Level:** no predestination to sin, God working evil only permissively, grace not given to the predestined alone, **rung 1** (Orange as confirmed and received; Trent VI cans. 6, 17); no one predestined to hell, lost only by his own fault, **at least rung 3** (CCC 1037); whether non-election ("negative reprobation") precedes foreseen sin, as Thomists read a.3, **rung 5**.

**Keep apart:** "double predestination" as words is not the condemned thing: Valence (855) confessed "a predestination of the impious to death" ([Gottschalk and the ninth-century synods](#gottschalk-and-the-ninth-century-synods)). What Orange and can. 17 exclude is predestination *to sin*, and to no grace.

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md)

### Antecedent and consequent will

Aquinas's distinction: God wills all to be saved antecedently, considering each simply, and wills just punishment consequently, with every circumstance, sin included, considered.

ST I q.19 a.6 ad 1: a just judge antecedently wills every man to live, and consequently wills the murderer hanged. It is how Aquinas squares the [universal salvific will](#universal-salvific-will) with the fact that not all are saved ([5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md)). The same reply also lists Augustine's narrow reading of 1 Tim 2:4, "every sort of men", among its readings.

**Level:** a school's account, **no magisterial level**; what it explains, God's will to save each person, **at least rung 3**.

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md)

### Universal salvific will

God wills the salvation of every human being, none excepted.

"Who will have all men to be saved" (1 Tim 2:4, DR), Paul's reason for praying "for all men" (2:1; [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md)). God does not want "any to perish" (CCC 1037, 1058); Christ died for all without exception (CCC 605, quoting Quiercy, 853; Trent VI ch. 3, "He died for all"). That he died for the predestined only is condemned in [*Cum occasione*](#five-propositions) (1653), prop. 5, in that stated sense ([5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)). Aquinas squares it with the lost by [antecedent and consequent will](#antecedent-and-consequent-will). It is the third premise of the limbo problem, beside the necessity of baptism and original sin as each one's own ([7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)), and the ground of the prayer and hope for all ([7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md)).

**Hard fact.** Augustine read "all" as "every sort of men" (*Enchiridion* 103, tr. J. F. Shaw), and Aquinas still lists that reading (I q.19 a.6 ad 1). No act condemned it, but it is not the Church's reading.

**Level:** God's will to save each person, and Christ's death for every human being without exception, **at least rung 3** (CCC 605, 1037, 1058; Trent VI ch. 3; one act behind them is a local synod, and can. 749 §3 defaults down); that Christ did not die for the predestined only, **rung 1** (*Cum occasione* prop. 5, stated sense); rejecting prop. 5 as worded, **at least rung 3**.

**Does not assert:** that CCC 605's quotation makes Quiercy's sentence a definition (the Catechism locates; Quiercy is a local synod); that all are saved (universalism asserted collides with hell's eternity, [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md)). Calling the universal will an opinion because Augustine read it narrowly understates.

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md), [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md), [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

### Certainty of election

No one in this life can know with certainty, without special revelation, that he is among the predestined.

Trent VI ch. 12 forbids presuming to determine for certain that one is among the predestinate, "for except by special revelation, it cannot be known whom God hath chosen unto Himself" (Waterworth); can. 15 condemns saying the justified is bound by faith to believe that he is assuredly among them. For [final perseverance](#final-perseverance), ch. 13 asks instead for "a most firm hope in God's help"; can. 16, on certainty of perseverance, is graded in [`moral-theology` 3.2](../moral-theology/lessons/03-02-faith-hope-and-charity.md). The secret is itself pastoral: revealed to all, it would make some despair and others careless (ST I q.23 a.1 ad 4). Calvin by contrast makes knowledge of one's election the ground of humility and security (*Institutes* III.21.1). [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md) applies Trent's reason to damnation: without special revelation, no one knows who is lost.

**Level:** can. 15, **rung 1**, but narrower than the chapter: it condemns only making certainty of election an obligation of faith; ch. 12's wider rule, fenced chapter teaching, **at least rung 3**, its core reaching **rung 1** through can. 16, since predestination includes final perseverance.

**Keep apart:** [certainty of grace](#certainty-of-grace) (ch. 9), one's present state, against election, one's final outcome.

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md)

### Before or after foreseen merits

Whether God's predestination of someone to glory is decreed before (*ante praevisa merita*) or in view of (*post praevisa merita*) the merits he foresees that person will have by grace.

Aquinas (ST I q.23 a.5): merit cannot be the reason for predestination's whole effect, since "whatsoever is in man disposing him towards salvation, is all included under the effect of predestination"; why God chooses some "has no reason, except the divine will" (ad 3). **Before:** late Augustine ([`patristics` 4.5](../patristics/lessons/04-05-augustine-against-the-pelagians.md)), Aquinas, the Thomists. **After:** other Catholic theologians, the merits themselves being gifts. No act has decided. The linked dispute over efficacious grace, closed undecided in 1607, is a different axis ([de auxiliis](#de-auxiliis)): Bellarmine and Suárez held middle knowledge and also predestination before foreseen merits ([5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)).

**Level:** **rung 5**: permitted, untaught, uncondemned.

**Does not assert:** that "before" is Catholic doctrine because Aquinas held it, or Calvinism because Calvin did.

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md), [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)

### Gottschalk and the ninth-century synods

The first test of Orange's boundary: a monk condemned for his teaching on predestination, then two Frankish synods that put predestination differently and both denied predestination to sin.

In 848 a council at Mainz condemned the monk Gottschalk's teaching on predestination; at Quiercy in 849 he was flogged, made to burn his writings and confined for life, some twenty years. Then the bishops split. Quiercy (853) taught one predestination, to grace or to just punishment; its line that some perish by "the desert of those who perish" frames the asymmetry, and its chapter on Christ's death for all is quoted in CCC 605. Valence (855) confessed two, among them "a predestination of the impious to death", and repeated Orange's condemnation of predestination to evil, naming the council.

**Level:** both local synods, **no rung** by themselves; the boundary both keep, no predestination to sin, is **rung 1** through Orange II and Trent VI can. 17.

**Keep apart:** Valence's "predestination of the impious to death" is not the condemned "predestination to evil" ([reprobation](#reprobation)).

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md)

### Five propositions

The five theses on grace that Innocent X's *Cum occasione* (31 May 1653) censured as taken from Jansen's *Augustinus*.

In outline (lesson's translation): (1) some commandments are impossible to just men who will and strive, by their present powers, and the grace to make them possible is lacking; (2) in the state of fallen nature interior grace is never resisted; (3) for merit and demerit in fallen nature freedom from coercion suffices, freedom from necessity not being required; (4) the semi-Pelagians admitted prevenient grace even for the beginning of faith, and were heretics in wanting it resistible; (5) it is semi-Pelagian to say Christ died or shed his blood for all men without exception (*pro omnibus omnino hominibus*). Every one concerns the fallen state. Prop. 2 is the hinge: if grace is never resisted, a just man who sins lacked it (1), his unavoidable sin needs only freedom from coercion to be imputed (3), resistible grace becomes heresy (4), and Christ's death for all becomes semi-Pelagian (5). Props. 4 and 5 make opponents, the Molinists above all, heirs of the Massilians.

**The censures** ([5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)), each proposition with its own note, not a global censure: (1) rash, impious, blasphemous, condemned by anathema, heretical; (2) and (3) heretical; (4) false and heretical; (5) false, rash, scandalous, and, understood to mean that Christ died for the salvation of the predestined only (*pro salute dumtaxat praedestinatorum*), impious, blasphemous, contumelious, derogating from God's goodness, heretical. The bull approves no other opinion in the book.

**Trent had covered the ground.** Against (1): VI ch. 11, "God commands not impossibilities", calling the contrary a "rash saying", and can. 18. Against (2): can. 4. Against (5): ch. 3, "though He died for all". (3) revives the thirty-ninth of [Baius](#baius)'s propositions (1567): what is done voluntarily is free even if necessary. (4) is false as history, since the Massilians denied that the beginning of faith needs grace, and heretical as doctrine. What the freedom from necessity required for merit is, metaphysically, is [`metaphysics` 6.2](../metaphysics/lessons/06-02-compatibilism.md)-6.4's.

**Level:** the contradictories of (1)-(3), the denial of (4)'s charge that resistible grace is heresy, and the denial that Christ died for the predestined only, **rung 1** (a papal constitution calling itself a definition, largely on ground Trent's canons had defined); rejecting (5) as worded, censured below heresy, **at least rung 3** (whether "false" there is definitive is not manifest: default down, can. 749 §3); that the five are in the *Augustinus* in Jansen's sense, **rung 2** ([droit and fait](#droit-and-fait)).

**Does not assert:** that all five are heretical as worded; that Rome condemned efficacious grace, Augustine or the Thomists with them (Benedict XIV, [*Dum praeterito*](#dum-praeterito-1748)).

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md), [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)

### Composed and divided sense

The will moved to consent cannot at once consent and dissent (the composed sense), yet keeps the power to dissent (the divided sense), as a man sitting can stand, though not while sitting.

ST I-II q.10 a.4 ad 3; the Thomist reading of Trent VI can. 4, which condemns saying the moved will "cannot refuse its consent, if it would" (*neque posse dissentire, si velit*). With [physical premotion](#physical-premotion) it lets the Dominicans say the will both is infallibly moved and could have refused. The Molinist objection ([5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md) Ex 2): in the composed sense the will without the premotion cannot consent, so the withholding of premotion looks like the cause of refusal. The Molinists read can. 4 instead as a power to refuse under that very grace.

**Level:** a school's reading of a **rung 1** canon, itself **rung 5**: the canon defines that the moved will can refuse, not this analysis.

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)

### Simultaneous concurrence

Molina's account of God's causal share in a free act: God concurs in the act itself as co-cause, not by a prior determination of the will.

Against the Thomist [physical premotion](#physical-premotion), a motion prior in the order of causes. On Molina's account God gives the prevenient grace, which is gratuitous and comes first, and co-causes the act; [middle knowledge](#middle-knowledge) is how he knows the outcome, not a withdrawal of causality, so the picture of a God who "only watches" is wrong. On God and evil the schools part again: for the Thomists God causes the act's being, not its defect; for the Molinists he concurs with what the will determines ([5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)).

**Level:** **rung 5**; that God works evil only permissively, which both accounts must keep, **rung 1** (Trent VI can. 6).

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)

### Congruism

The Jesuit refinement of Molinism: an efficacious grace is a congruous grace, fitted to the person and chosen by God for that reason, so that it is a special gift before any consent.

The form Bellarmine and Suárez gave the system of [middle knowledge](#middle-knowledge); the Jesuit general Claudio Aquaviva pressed it on Jesuit teachers in 1613 (how far is disputed). It answers the Thomist's "who distinguisheth thee?" (1 Cor 4:7, DR): God's choice of a congruous grace for one who will accept it is itself the gift, so the difference between two hearers is not simply their own ([5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md) Ex 2).

**Level:** **rung 5**.

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)

### Dum praeterito (1748)

Benedict XIV's letter of 31 July 1748 to the Grand Inquisitor of Spain on the freedom of the Catholic schools of grace.

In the lesson's translation ([5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md) P3): the Thomists, decried as followers "not only of Jansen but even of Calvin", hold their opinion "with impunity", since it "has never been reprobated by the Apostolic See"; of the followers of Molina and Suárez, proscribed "as if they were Semipelagians", the popes "have not yet passed judgment on this Molinist system", and they "may continue" its defence. Bishops and inquisitors are to look not at the notes that quarrelling doctors set against one another but at whether the opinions so marked have been reprobated by the Apostolic See. Benedict adds that "as private doctors" popes may favour one opinion, but as pontiffs he reprobates neither. [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md) uses it to show that Thomist efficacious grace was never condemned with Jansen.

**Level:** discipline, **no rung**; it reports the state of the question and reprobates nothing. The schools it protects stay **rung 5**.

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md), [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)

### Droit and fait

The distinction between the question of right (are these propositions heresy?) and the question of fact (are they in this book, in its author's sense?).

Jansen's defenders condemned the five and denied the *Augustinus* contained them: Antoine Arnauld wrote in 1655 that he had read the book without finding them and condemned them wherever they were (Pascal, *Provincial Letters* I, 23 January 1656). Pascal: the quarrel concerns a fact, where councils, he quoted Bellarmine, "may err in questions of fact" (Letter XVII, 23 January 1657); and his dilemma: if Jansen taught only efficacious grace, he has no errors; if something else, no defenders. The Church claimed to judge both. Alexander VII, *Ad sanctam beati Petri sedem* (16 October 1656): "declaramus et definimus" that the five were taken from the *Augustinus* and condemned "in the sense intended by" Jansen. The reason ([5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)): a teaching office that can condemn an error but never say *where* it is cannot protect anyone from a book.

**Level:** the right, **rung 1** ([five propositions](#five-propositions)); the fact, **rung 2**, the textbook [dogmatic fact](#dogmatic-fact): defined in 1656, owed interior assent by 1705 ([respectful silence](#respectful-silence)), not revealed, so denying it is not heresy (can. 751) but sets one against definitive teaching (can. 750 §2). Arnauld and Pascal, **no magisterial level**.

**Keep apart:** the judgment is on a public text's sense, not on a dead man's mind. 1656 speaks of "the sense intended by" Jansen, 1705 of the sense "their words plainly bear"; Jansen died before the condemnation, his will submitting the book to the Holy See (its authenticity later questioned), and the doctrine says nothing of his soul. The fact is neither "mere history at no rung" (Pascal) nor dogma.

*Lessons:* [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)

### Respectful silence

The Jansenist position that on the question of fact a Catholic owes outward silence, not inward assent.

Arnauld's party concluded it from the fact's not being of faith ([droit and fait](#droit-and-fait)). The Peace of Clement IX (1669) accepted four bishops' signatures of the [formulary](#formulary) as unreserved, though they had told their synods that silence on the fact sufficed. Clement XI's *Vineam Domini Sabaoth* (16 July 1705) ruled that obedience is not satisfied "by that respectful silence" (*obsequioso illo silentio*): the condemned sense, which "their words plainly bear", must be rejected "not with the mouth only but with the heart" (lesson's translations).

**Level:** the fact it concerns, **rung 2** ([dogmatic fact](#dogmatic-fact)); on such a fact, silence is not obedience.

*Lessons:* [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)

### Formulary

The oath condemning the five propositions in Jansen's sense, first required by the French bishops and then made papal.

Alexander VII, *Regiminis apostolici* (15 February 1665): the signer swears that he rejects the five "with a sincere mind" (*sincero animo*), "in the sense intended by the same author" (lesson's translation). The Peace of Clement IX (1669) accepted reserved signatures as unreserved; *Vineam Domini* (1705) closed that gap ([respectful silence](#respectful-silence)).

**Level:** the oath, **law, no rung**; what it swears to, that the five are in the *Augustinus* in Jansen's sense, **rung 2** ([dogmatic fact](#dogmatic-fact)); their condemnation, **rung 1** ([five propositions](#five-propositions)).

*Lessons:* [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)

### Rigorism

The Jansenist-era pastoral severity that kept communion from all but the proven and put satisfaction before absolution.

Arnauld's *On Frequent Communion* (1643). A Holy Office decree of 7 December 1690, under Alexander VIII (a decree, not a bull; Jansenist in origin, though it names no school), condemned thirty-one propositions "respectively", a [global censure](#in-globo-censure): among them, that satisfaction must precede absolution by Christ's law (prop. 16); that those without the purest love of God, free of all admixture, are to be kept from communion (prop. 23); that an unbeliever must sin in every work (prop. 8; [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md)); and holding a doctrine clearly grounded in Augustine "without regard to any papal bull" (prop. 30). **Undone as discipline:** *Sacra Tridentina Synodus* (Congregation of the Council, 1905) opened daily communion to anyone in grace with a right intention, communion being no "reward or prize" for virtue; it says that as the Jansenist "plague" spread, few were judged worthy of daily communion, merchants and the married were excluded as classes, and even some reputable theologians allowed it only rarely. *Quam singulari* (8 August 1910) set first communion at about seven ([`sacraments-and-liturgy` 3.5](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md)). The sacraments are remedies, not trophies ([5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)).

**Level:** the 1690 propositions, excluded, **at least rung 3**, with no precise note on any one; the decrees of 1905 and 1910, disciplinary decrees of Roman congregations under Pius X, **law, no rung**, not doctrinal definitions.

*Lessons:* [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)

**The last things (Module 6)**

### Particular judgment

Each person is judged by God at the moment of death, and his eternal destiny begins at once.

What is defined is the immediate recompense. Florence's [*Laetentur caeli*](#laetentur-caeli) (6 July 1439), under its *diffinimus*: the souls with nothing to purge, and the purified, "are received at once (*mox*) into heaven"; those who die in actual mortal sin "go down at once (*mox*) to hell" (lesson's translation). The CDF's *Doctrinal Commentary* on the concluding formula of the *Professio fidei* (29 June 1998), §11, lists "the immediate recompense after death" among truths to be believed as revealed. The **name** is the schools': the Roman Catechism (1566), Part I, Art. VII, "Two Judgments": each one at death "is instantly placed before the judgment-seat of God ... This is called the particular judgment" (tr. McHugh and Callan, 1923). CCC 1022 puts each person's eternal retribution at the moment of death; CCC 1021 grants that the New Testament speaks of judgment chiefly as the last one. The texts: Lazarus carried to "Abraham's bosom" while the rich man's brothers still live (Luke 16:22, 28); "This day thou shalt be with me in paradise" (23:43); "to be dissolved and to be with Christ" (Phil 1:23); "once to die, and after this the judgment" (Heb 9:27, DR). What is judged is a surviving conscious self, the [separated soul](#separated-soul) (CDF 1979 letter, point 3). Aquinas: "after death man enters into an unchangeable state as to all that concerns the soul" (ST III q.59 a.5 ad 1).

**Level:** that each person is judged immediately after death, **rung 1** (Florence; the 1998 commentary), whatever is said of [*Benedictus Deus*](#benedictus-deus)'s *ex cathedra* standing ([`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-04-infallibility-in-exercise-and-the-ordinary-universal-magisterium.md)). Calling the recompense a "judgment" adds nothing the definitions lack. The judgment-seat and the scrutiny of every thought: imagery, **no level** (the CDF's 1979 letter warns against "arbitrary imaginative representations"). Why the will is fixed at death (III q.59 a.5): a school's explanation.

**Keep apart:** the rule from any verdict. A canonized saint is in glory, **rung 2**; that a named person is damned is **taught by no act** (CCC 1861: we entrust "judgment of persons" to God). The last judgment never reopens this one: *nihilominus* ([last judgment](#last-judgment)).

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-death-and-the-particular-judgment.md), [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-parousia-the-last-judgment-and-the-new-creation.md)

### Last judgment

The public, universal judgment at Christ's return, when all appear in their bodies and the verdict on each whole life is completed and made known.

Christ "shall come again, with glory, to judge both the quick and the dead" (Creed of 381, Schaff). [*Firmiter*](#firmiter) (Lateran IV, 1215): he comes "at the end of the age" to render to each according to his works, "to the reprobate as to the elect" (*tam reprobis quam electis*; lesson's translation). Michael VIII's [profession at Lyons II](#lyons-ii-profession) (1274) and [*Benedictus Deus*](#benedictus-deus) (1336), after the immediate destinies, add *nihilominus*, "none the less": on the day of judgment all will appear with their bodies before Christ's tribunal to give account of their deeds (2 Cor 5:10). What the last day adds: the **body** ([resurrection of the body](#resurrection-of-the-body)); **publicity**, "we must all be manifested before the judgment seat of Christ" (2 Cor 5:10, DR; LG 48), God's justice shown before all (CCC 1039-1040); and, for Aquinas, the **whole life**, which goes on after death in memory and reputation, in children, in the results of one's acts (Arius's error still spreading), in the body and in the things one loved, so that "a perfect and public Judgment" must wait (ST III q.59 a.5). God judges twice "according to different respects" (ad 2). Gregory the Great: the just already in heaven gain at the judgment "that their bodies also shall be partakers of eternal bliss" (*Dialogues* IV.25, tr. "P.W.", ed. Gardner). The criterion, Matt 25:31-46: "as long as you did it to one of these my least brethren, you did it to me" (25:40, DR).

**Level:** Christ's return to judge all by their works, **rung 1** (the creeds; *Firmiter*); the bodily appearing and the standing particular verdict, **rung 1**, held together by *nihilominus*; publicity, **at least rung 3** (LG 48; CCC 1039-1040); Matt 25 read as doctrine, judgment by works (**rung 1**) and Christ identified with those served, so that how we treat a neighbour shows grace accepted or refused (CCC 678; GS 27 applies 25:40 to every neighbour), **at least rung 3**; who "the least" are in Matthew's own sense (all the needy, or Christ's disciples), **rung 5** as exegesis; Aquinas's reasons for a second judgment, **no magisterial level**.

**Keep apart:** it is not a retrial; the soul's verdict is never reopened. Not the [parousia](#parousia) itself, which it follows, nor the [new creation](#new-creation), which follows it.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-death-and-the-particular-judgment.md), [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-parousia-the-last-judgment-and-the-new-creation.md)

### Reincarnation

The belief that a person lives successive earthly lives in different bodies; excluded, because each has one earthly life, closed by death and judgment.

"It is appointed unto men once to die, and after this the judgment" (Heb 9:27, DR); its *hapax*, "once", also marks Christ's one offering (9:28; [`new-testament` 6.2](../new-testament/lessons/06-02-hebrews.md)). *Lumen Gentium* 48 speaks of the *unico* course of our earthly life, citing the verse: the Vatican's English drops "single", and CCC 1013 keeps it and concludes that there is no reincarnation after death. The same exclusion covers a **second chance**: death ends the time open to accepting or refusing grace (CCC 1021), so [purgatory](#purgatory) is not one.

**Level:** no reincarnation and no second chance, **at least rung 3** (LG 48; CCC 1013, 1021). Never an open question, since their contraries collide with rung-1 truths: all rise "with their own bodies which they now bear" (*Firmiter*) and each destiny takes effect at death (Florence). Not "defined" either: no act condemns reincarnation by name. Both errors are graded strictly.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-death-and-the-particular-judgment.md)

### Intermediate state

The condition of the dead between death and the resurrection: already judged and already in their outcome, but without their bodies.

The CDF's *Letter on Certain Questions Concerning Eschatology* (17 May 1979), paraphrased: a spiritual element endowed with consciousness and will survives and subsists after death, so that the human self subsists, and the Church keeps the word "soul" (point 3); Christ's glorious manifestation is "distinct and deferred" with respect to the situation of people just after death (point 5). That excludes the modern proposal of a resurrection at the moment of death. The older picture: Irenaeus kept the souls of the faithful in "the invisible place allotted to them by God" until the resurrection (*Against Heresies* V.31.2, tr. Rambaut); Tertullian kept all souls in Hades until the end, already in "punishments and consolations", the martyrs alone in Paradise (*On the Soul* 55, 58); Gregory put the perfect in heaven "straight after death" (*Dialogues* IV.25). The definitions side with Gregory: Florence's *mox*, *Benedictus Deus*'s "even before the resumption of their bodies".

**Level:** that a conscious self survives, **rung 3** (CDF 1979; [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md)); that the resurrection waits for Christ's coming and is not each person's at death, **at least rung 3** (1979, points 5-6), rung 1 arguable from *Firmiter*'s "he will come ... all of whom will rise" and *Benedictus Deus*'s *ante resumptionem suorum corporum* ([7.1](lessons/07-01-the-resurrection-of-the-body.md)); the immediate outcomes within it, **rung 1** ([particular judgment](#particular-judgment)).

**Keep apart:** no one waits in neutral: each soul is already in heaven, in purification, or in hell. What the separated soul is, metaphysically, is [separated soul](#separated-soul)'s. Handed over by [`christology` 6.1](../christology/lessons/06-01-he-descended-into-hell.md).

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-death-and-the-particular-judgment.md)

### Lyons II profession

The profession of faith of the emperor Michael VIII Palaeologus, read at the Second Council of Lyons; it witnesses Roman teaching on the last things and defines nothing of itself.

Clement IV proposed the formula to Michael in 1267; Michael's letter offered it to Gregory X at the council's fourth session, 6 July 1274. Its clauses on the dead give the shape Florence later defines: the truly penitent who die in charity before making satisfaction are purged after death *poenis purgatoriis seu catharteriis*, "by purgatorial or cleansing pains", and the suffrages of the living help them; the stainless and the purged are "received at once into heaven" (*mox in caelum recipi*), with **no vision named**; those who die *in mortali peccato vel cum solo originali* go down at once into hell, *poenis tamen disparibus puniendas*; and *nihilominus*, on the day of judgment all will appear with their bodies before Christ's tribunal to render an account of their deeds.

**Level:** a profession, **defines nothing of itself**; the level of its content is fixed by later acts, chiefly Florence (**rung 1**).

**Keep apart:** its wording differs from Florence's: *mortali peccato* without *actuali*, and *cum solo originali* (see [solo originali clause](#solo-originali-clause)). Because it names no vision, John XXII's [deferred vision](#deferred-vision) could claim it. "Lyons II defined the immediacy", "defined purgatory" or "defined the vision" names the wrong act, and the last overstates twice.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-death-and-the-particular-judgment.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-purgatory.md), [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

### Benedictus Deus

Benedict XII's constitution of 29 January 1336, which settled against John XXII's opinion that the purified see God's essence at once after death, and stated that those dying in actual mortal sin go down at once to hell.

Its central sentence (lesson's translation, abridged): "By this constitution, valid for ever, we define by apostolic authority (*diffinimus*)" that the souls of the saints with nothing to purge, or once purged, "at once (*mox*) after their death and that purging, even before the resumption of their bodies and the general judgment" (*etiam ante resumptionem suorum corporum et iudicium generale*), are in heaven and "see the divine essence with an intuitive and even face-to-face vision" (*visione intuitiva et etiam faciali*), "no creature mediating as the object seen" (*nulla mediante creatura in ratione obiecti visi se habente*), the essence showing itself "immediately, plainly, clearly and openly". The vision ends their acts of faith and hope, and "has continued and will continue" (*continuata exstitit et continuabitur*) for ever. A second *diffinimus*: those who die "in actual mortal sin" go down to hell "immediately after their death" (*mox ... ad inferna descendunt*), "where they are tormented with infernal punishments" (*cruciantur*). Then *nihilominus*: all will appear with their bodies before Christ's tribunal (2 Cor 5:10). The text spells the verb *diffinimus*. It presupposes purgation without defining it (*cum post mortem suam fuerint purgatae*).

**Level:** its *ex cathedra* standing is **open** ([`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-04-infallibility-in-exercise-and-the-ordinary-universal-magisterium.md)), and the content on immediacy is **rung 1** regardless, by Florence and the 1998 commentary. Its own precisions (intuitive; no creature as the object seen; faith and hope ended): **rung 1** if *ex cathedra*, **at least rung 3** regardless, with Florence's "clearly ... as he is" carrying their substance at rung 1.

**Keep apart:** "no creature mediating" excludes a creature *as the thing seen*, not the [light of glory](#light-of-glory) *by which* one sees (ST I q.12 a.5 ad 2). The hell clause has *mox* and *cruciantur*, no *perpetuam*: cite [*Firmiter*](#firmiter) and the [Athanasian Creed](#athanasian-creed) for eternity, *Benedictus Deus* only for the immediacy. That clause is conditional in form, naming no one ([7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md)). It dropped [*Ne super his*](#ne-super-his)'s hedge without denying an increase of happiness with the body. Not Pius IV's bull of the same incipit confirming Trent (1564; [`church-history` 6.4](../church-history/lessons/06-04-trent-and-catholic-reform.md)).

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-death-and-the-particular-judgment.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-purgatory.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md), [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-parousia-the-last-judgment-and-the-new-creation.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md)

### Laetentur caeli

The Council of Florence's decree of union with the Greeks (6 July 1439), whose clauses on the dead are the act that defines the immediate outcomes, purgatory, degrees of glory and the "original sin only" clause.

The clauses on the dead run under the decree's *diffinimus* (*Diffinimus insuper ... Item ... Item*). **Purgatory:** if "the truly penitent die in the love of God (*in Dei caritate*)" before making satisfaction by worthy fruits of penance, "their souls are purged after death by purgatorial pains (*poenis purgatoriis*)", and the suffrages of the living avail them. **Heaven:** the stainless and the purged are received at once (*in caelum mox recipi*) and "clearly behold God himself, three and one, as he is, yet, according to the diversity of merits, one more perfectly than another" (*pro meritorum tamen diversitate alium alio perfectius*). **Hell:** those who die "in actual mortal sin, or with original sin only, go down at once into hell", "to be punished, however, with unlike punishments" (*poenis tamen disparibus*). All lesson's translation. *Lumen Gentium* 49 quotes its "clearly ... as he is"; LG 51 re-proposes the decree.

**Level:** **rung 1**, a conciliar definition. It is the act behind the immediate judgment (6.1), the vision at once (6.2), purgatory and the suffrages (6.3), the descent and the unlike punishments (6.4), and the clause on original sin only, as worded (7.4).

**Keep apart:** Florence lacks *Benedictus Deus*'s "intuitive" and "no creature mediating", and says "at once" where that act adds "even before the resumption of their bodies": the precisions rest on *Benedictus Deus*, while Florence's *mox* and "clearly ... as he is" carry their substance at rung 1. Against Lyons II it adds *Dei* to *in caritate* and *actuali* to *mortali peccato*, drops *seu catharteriis* and *cum*, and names no fire (nor did Lyons), after Mark of Ephesus attacked the Latin chapters on purgatorial fire (Ferrara, 1438). Its hell clause says nothing of eternity (that is *Firmiter*'s) and names no infant and no limbo; the ITC (2007, §37) reads its thrust as the immediacy of judgment. 6.4 renders *disparibus* "unequal", 7.4 "unlike" (see [solo originali clause](#solo-originali-clause)).

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-death-and-the-particular-judgment.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-purgatory.md), [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md), [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

### Purgatory

The final purification after death of those who die in God's grace and love but not yet wholly purified; every one of them reaches heaven.

**Defined** at Florence (1439): the truly penitent who die "in the love of God" before making satisfaction are purged after death "by purgatorial pains", the suffrages of the living avail them, and the purged are received "at once" into heaven. **Professed** at Lyons II (1274), defining nothing of itself. **Related claims defined** at Trent: a debt of temporal punishment can remain after forgiven guilt, "to be discharged either in this world, or in the next in Purgatory" (VI can. 30, 1547; [guilt and punishment](#guilt-and-punishment)); the Mass is rightly offered for those "departed in Christ, and who are not as yet fully purified" (XXII ch. 2, can. 3, 1562, Schaff). **Restated** without a canon in [Session XXV](#trent-session-xxv) (3 December 1563). CCC 1030-1032: the souls are assured of their salvation; it is "entirely different from the punishment of the damned" (1031). What is purged is what grace has forgiven but not finished: unrepented small sins, habits, what remains of a debt.

The evidence 6.3 gives, in its order: the **practice** came first, the [law of prayer](#lex-orandi): Judas's "sacrifice to be offered for the sins of the dead" (2 Macc 12:43, DR); Tertullian, "As often as the anniversary comes round, we make offerings for the dead" (*De corona* 3, c. 211); the Jerusalem Eucharist's prayer for "all who in past years have fallen asleep among us" (*Mystagogical Catecheses* 5.9, c. 350); Monica's request (*Confessions* IX.11.27). The **texts** the tradition reads: Matt 12:32 (Gregory, *Dialogues* IV.39), Matt 5:26 (Cyprian, Ep. 51 §20), 1 Cor 3:11-15: no verse carries the doctrine alone. And the **distinction** of guilt from the debt of punishment. The argument from prayer for the dead, as a skeleton, is in the Arguments section.

**Level:** purgatory, the help of [suffrages](#suffrages) and the purged reaching heaven, **rung 1** (Florence); VI can. 30 and XXII can. 3, **rung 1**; the souls' own assurance of salvation, **at least rung 3** ([*Exsurge Domine*](#exsurge-domine)); a real fire, a place, a duration, a mechanism, **no act, rung 5**, though fire as Scripture's image stands; *Spe Salvi* §47's fire-as-Christ, **rung 5**; the Supplement's vision delayed plus "corporeal fire" (Appendix 1 q.2 a.1) and its place near hell, only "probable" (Appendix 2 a.2), **rung 5**.

**Does not license:** a second chance (only those who die "in the love of God"; CCC 1021; "No one, then, need hope that after he is dead he shall obtain merit with God which he has neglected to secure here", Augustine, *Enchiridion* 110); a milder hell, or "hell with an exit" (CCC 1031; CDF 1979, point 7); preaching a furnace as dogma, or a painless purification (the 1979 letter cuts both ways). The Orthodox *Confession* of Peter Mogila (1643) prays for the dead and refuses a temporary purifying punishment (no magisterial level). Indulgences for the dead are [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 4.2](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/04-02-indulgences-as-a-practice.md)'s.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-death-and-the-particular-judgment.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-purgatory.md)

### Deferred vision

John XXII's opinion, preached from All Saints' Day 1331, that the souls of the just see Christ's humanity, not the divine essence, until the resurrection and the judgment.

At its strongest (6.2): its texts tie the reward to the judgment ("Come, ye blessed ... possess you the kingdom", Matt 25:34; "when he shall appear", 1 John 3:2); the martyrs "under the altar" (Apoc 6:9-11), read as sheltered by Christ's humanity now and seeing his divinity after the judgment; its anthropology, "the soul, without the body, is not man" (ST I-II q.4 a.5 obj. 3); its Father, Augustine, for whom the separated soul's desire to rule its body holds it back (*Literal Meaning of Genesis* XII.35, in obj. 4). Aquinas had already named and rejected the view in I-II q.4 a.5. The history: the Dominican Thomas Waleys preached against it at Avignon in 1333 and was imprisoned; Ockham attacked it; at the turn of 1334 the Paris theologians affirmed the immediate vision and called the pope's view a personal opinion; in consistory John said he had not meant to decide; on 3 December 1334, the day before he died, he retracted ([*Ne super his*](#ne-super-his)); his successor decided against him ([*Benedictus Deus*](#benedictus-deus), 1336).

**Level:** the sermons were **no magisterial act**. Calling the view heresy in 1331 overstates: no act yet excluded it, and Lyons II's "received at once into heaven" named no vision. Since 1336 and 1439 it is excluded (the vision at once, **rung 1**, Florence).

**Keep apart:** it does not refute infallibility. A preacher's opinion on an undecided question, never defined and submitted to the Church, fails [`fundamental-theology` 4.2](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-02-infallibility-and-its-conditions.md)'s conditions: the second "pope outside the conditions" case after Honorius (E&M 4.4). What the body adds to the blessed: Aquinas's "not in intensity, but in extent" (I-II q.4 a.5 ad 5), **rung 4**.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md)

### Ne super his

John XXII's deathbed declaration of 3 December 1334, retracting his opinion on the vision and submitting all he had said to the Church.

The purified separated souls are in heaven and see the divine essence face to face, clearly, "as far as the state and condition of the separated soul allows" (*in quantum status et condicio compatitur animae separatae*; lesson's translation). Whatever he had said otherwise, he submitted all his words and writings to the determination of the Church and of his successors (*submittimus determinationi Ecclesiae ac successorum Nostrorum*). He died the next day, 4 December 1334.

**Level:** a retraction and submission; the lessons give it **no rung** as teaching.

**Keep apart:** its hedge ("as far as ... allows") is not in *Benedictus Deus*, which dropped it without denying that the body adds something (6.2, Ex 2).

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md)

### Ad nostrum

The Council of Vienne's decree condemning errors of the Beghards and Beguines; its fifth proposition denied that the soul needs a light of glory to see God.

Vienne (1311-12) condemned among the Beghards' errors (proposition 5) that the soul needs no light of glory raising it (*lumine gloriae, ipsam elevante*) to see God (lesson's translation). The censure is **one** condemnation of the sect "with" its errors, with no note on any proposition.

**Level:** its contradictory, that a light of glory is needed, **at least rung 3** (one global censure, so default down, can. 749 §3); its core, that no creature reaches the vision of itself, **rung 1** (*Dei Filius* can. II.3; [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md)); what the light *is*, a created habit (ST I q.12 a.5), **rung 4**.

**Keep apart:** the 1913 *Catholic Encyclopedia* ("Heaven") says Vienne "defined" the light of glory: overstated. "Thomist jargon" understates: the term is the council's. Not Vienne's decree on the soul as form of the body ([Council of Vienne](#council-of-vienne)). The light is the [light of glory](#light-of-glory).

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md)

### Incomprehensibility

No created mind ever takes the full measure of God, not even the blessed who see him.

[*Firmiter*](#firmiter) (Lateran IV, 1215) and [*Dei Filius*](#dei-filius) ch. 1 (1870) confess God *incomprehensibilis* (the attribute is [`trinity-and-god` 1.2](../trinity-and-god/lessons/01-02-the-one-true-god-and-what-reason-can-reach.md)'s). Applied to the vision: the blessed see God's whole, simple essence, but not as perfectly as he is knowable, since a finite light cannot know the infinite infinitely (ST I q.12 a.7). So seeing the essence is not seeing in it all that God does or can do (I q.12 a.8); the blessed see in God what belongs to them (Suppl. q.72 a.1).

**Level:** God's incomprehensibility, **rung 1** (*Firmiter*; *Dei Filius*); its application to the blessed, stated by no act, **rung 4**.

**Keep apart:** "seeing God" from "comprehending him" ("we will know everything" overstates). The Greek form of "never comprehended" is Gregory of Nyssa's endless ascent ([`patristics` 3.3](../patristics/lessons/03-03-the-two-gregories.md)).

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md)

### Degrees of glory

All the blessed see the same God, one more perfectly than another according to merit, and none envies another.

Florence: "yet, according to the diversity of merits, one more perfectly than another" (*pro meritorum tamen diversitate alium alio perfectius*; lesson's translation). Trent VI can. 32 (1547) counts "an increase of glory" among what the justified truly merit ([merit](#merit)). Aquinas measures the light of glory by charity: more charity, more desire, more light, a more perfect vision (ST I q.12 a.6); the object is the same for all, the difference lies in the glorified power of seeing (ad 3). Why it is no league table, Augustine (*Tractates on John* 67.2, tr. Innes): "There will not, therefore, be any envying amid this diversity of brightness, since in all of them will be reigning the unity of love." 1 Cor 15:41: "star differeth from star in glory".

**Level:** degrees of glory by merit, **rung 1** (Florence; Trent VI can. 32); charity as their measure, **rung 4**.

**Keep apart:** "heaven is equal for all" understates a rung-1 clause; unequal glory is not rivalry.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md)

### Communion of saints

The one Church of pilgrims on earth, the dead being purified, and the blessed in glory, joined in one charity and helping one another.

The Creed confesses "the communion of saints". *Lumen Gentium* ch. VII (paraphrased): one Church in three states; the blessed, closer to Christ, intercede for us through the one Mediator (§49), and the Church has long implored their help (§50). Trent's Session XXV decree on the invocation of saints (December 1563, Waterworth) teaches that the saints pray for men and that it is "good and useful" to invoke them, and that those who deny it "think impiously"; Session XXII can. 5 (17 September 1562) condemns calling Masses for obtaining their intercession "an imposture". 6.3 adds: the communion is the ground of [suffrages](#suffrages) for the dead; no one is saved alone, so prayer can reach another's purification after death ([*Spe Salvi*](#spe-salvi) §48).

**Level:** the communion of saints, **rung 1** (the Creed, which names the communion, not every mode of it); the saints' intercession and the goodness of invoking them, **at least rung 3** (Trent XXV, a decree without canons; LG 49-50), presupposed by XXII can. 5, **rung 1** as worded; how the saints know our prayers (in the divine essence, Suppl. q.72 a.1, a posthumous compilation), **rung 5**.

**Keep apart:** whether the souls in purgatory pray for us: Aquinas says they are "not in a condition to pray" (II-II q.83 a.11 ad 3), a school opinion. Mary's intercession is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 6.5](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/06-05-mary-in-the-economy-in-the-church-and-in-devotion.md)'s; the argued case with traditions that reject invocation is [`apologetics-catholic-claims`](../apologetics-catholic-claims/syllabus.md)'s.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-purgatory.md)

### Spe Salvi

Benedict XVI's encyclical on Christian hope (30 November 2007), which the course cites on heaven, on purgatory and on hope for others.

Paraphrased (copyrighted): §§10-12, whether anyone really wants eternal life, if it means endless living; eternity is not "an unending succession of days in the calendar" but something like a supreme moment of fulfilment (6.2). §44, grace "does not make wrong into right" ([7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md)). §45, death makes a life's choice definitive, and there can be people who have destroyed all love in themselves (6.3, 7.3). §46, in "the great majority", we may suppose, an openness to God remains (7.3). §47, "some recent theologians" hold that the purifying fire is Christ himself, whose gaze burns, heals and transforms, and its "duration" escapes earthly time (6.3). §48, prayer for the dead is common to East and West; no one is saved alone, so prayer reaches the dead; hope is always hope for others too (6.3, 7.3).

**Level:** an encyclical, authentic ordinary magisterium: **rung 3** for what it teaches. Much of it is reflection, cited for what it says, and it raises no claim's rung (6.2). §47's fire-as-Christ is reported as theologians' opinion and stays **rung 5**; §46's "we may suppose" teaches nothing.

**Keep apart:** "Benedict admitted eternal life sounds boring" inverts §§10-12, which raise the worry to reject its picture.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-purgatory.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md)

### Trent Session XXV

Trent's last session (3-4 December 1563): its decree on purgatory restates the doctrine without a canon and orders bishops to keep the subtle, the uncertain and the profitable out of preaching; its decree on the saints teaches their intercession.

The Decree concerning Purgatory (3 December 1563, Waterworth, 1848): the Church "has ... taught, in sacred councils, and very recently in this oecumenical Synod, that there is a Purgatory", and that the souls there are helped "by the suffrages of the faithful, but principally by the acceptable sacrifice of the altar".
