Bishops are to see that the sound doctrine is "believed, maintained, taught, and every where proclaimed"; "the more difficult and subtle questions" are kept from popular preaching; "such things as are uncertain" are not to be made public; what tends to curiosity or superstition, or "savour of filthy lucre", is prohibited. The one doctrinal verb is reported: the Church *has taught*. No canon, and no fire, place, length of time, or account of how a suffrage reaches a soul. The same session's decree on invocation of saints: see [communion of saints](#communion-of-saints).

**Level:** **no canon**. The doctrine it reports is **rung 1** by the earlier acts (Florence; VI can. 30; XXII can. 3). Its "principally" (the Mass first among suffrages; Florence ranks nothing), **at least rung 3**. The orders to bishops, **discipline, no rung**. The saints' intercession and invocation, **at least rung 3**.

**Keep apart:** "Trent XXV defined purgatory", with or without an anathema, names the wrong act. Trent's own catechism still says "the fire of purgatory" (Roman Catechism I, art. 5): the Latin imagery ran ahead of the definition. Purgatory preaching had become a trade ([`theology-of-debt` 3.6](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-06-luther-trent-and-the-reform-of-indulgences.md)).

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-purgatory.md)

### Exsurge Domine

Leo X's bull of 15 June 1520, condemning forty-one propositions of Luther under one global censure; proposition 38 concerns the souls in purgatory.

Prop. 38: "the souls in purgatory are not sure of their salvation, at least not all" (lesson's translation). The bull condemns the list [*in globo*](#in-globo-censure), "respectively", under notes such as "heretical", "false" and "offensive to pious ears", assigning none to any one proposition. (Its prop. 17, on the treasury, is [`theology-of-debt` 3.6](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-06-luther-trent-and-the-reform-of-indulgences.md)'s.)

**Level:** that the souls in purgatory are themselves sure of their salvation, **at least rung 3** (6.3's decision): the bull binds Catholics to reject prop. 38 but fixes no note, and can. 749 §3 forbids calling it defined; CCC 1030 restates "assured". Split the claim: that the purged **reach** heaven is **rung 1** (Florence).

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-purgatory.md)

### Athanasian Creed

The Latin creed *Quicumque*, long ascribed to Athanasius, who did not write it: received as a creed in the Latin Church, never adopted in the East.

V. 41: "at whose coming all men shall rise again with their bodies; and shall give account for their own works". V. 43: "And they that have done good shall go into life everlasting: and they that have done evil, into everlasting fire" (Latin *qui vero mala, in ignem aeternum*). Both in Schaff, *Creeds* vol. 2.

**Level:** its authority is a creed's, by reception in the Latin Church. With [*Firmiter*](#firmiter), one of the two acts that fix the eternity of hell's punishment at **rung 1** (6.4, 7.3); with *Firmiter*, the act for **rung 1** that all rise with their bodies (7.1).

**Keep apart:** "everlasting fire" is Scripture's word for the destination (Matt 25:41) and defines no physics: a pain beyond the loss is **at least rung 3**, the fire's nature **rung 5** ([pain of loss and pain of sense](#pain-of-loss-and-pain-of-sense)). *Benedictus Deus* is not an act for eternity.

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-resurrection-of-the-body.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md)

### Pain of loss and pain of sense

The schools' division of punishment into the loss of God (*poena damni*) and a positive suffering besides (*poena sensus*), named fire.

The definitions say "punishment" and do not divide it. Aquinas, ST I-II q.87 a.4 (English Dominican Province): as sin turns away from God, its punishment is the pain of loss, "which also is infinite, because it is the loss of the infinite good, i.e. God"; as it turns to a creature, the pain of sense, which is finite (in intensity, though everlasting). Innocent III had split the two by kind of sin: the lack of the vision for original sin, perpetual torment for actual sin ([*Maiores Ecclesiae causas*](#maiores-ecclesiae-causas), 1201). CCC 1035: eternal separation from God is the "chief punishment"; the CDF's 1979 letter (point 7): eternal punishment for a sinner deprived of the sight of God, affecting his whole being. What the fire is: real fire tormenting even spirits (Augustine, *City of God* XXI.10), or "every sinner kindles for himself the flame of his own fire" (Origen, *On First Principles* II.10.4, via Rufinus). **For original sin** (7.4): the schools assign the loss only, since sensible pain would punish "out of proportion to his guilt" (Supplement, Appendix 1 q.1 a.1); [*Auctorem fidei*](#auctorem-fidei) protects teaching a state of the pain of loss "without the pain of fire"; Florence's *disparibus* says "unlike", not whether in kind or degree.

**Level (6.4 decides):** that the lost lack the vision of God for ever, **rung 1** (the definitions give the vision to the blessed alone); that this loss is the chief, essential punishment, **at least rung 3** (CCC 1035; CDF 1979; Innocent III); a punishment besides the loss, named fire, **at least rung 3**; the scholastic pair and its aversion-conversion rationale, **rung 4**; what the fire is, corporeal or conscience, **rung 5**, provided Scripture's image is not emptied (CDF 1979). For original sin: the lack of the vision, **rung 1** ([solo originali clause](#solo-originali-clause)); the loss *only*, without pain of sense, a school opinion (Lombard; Aquinas), taught by no act.

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md), [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

### Self-exclusion

The Catechism's formula for hell: to die unrepentant in mortal sin is to remain separated from God for ever by one's own free choice.

CCC 1033: "definitive self-exclusion" from communion with God and the blessed. CCC 1037: God predestines no one to hell; a wilful turning from God, persisted in to the end, is required ([5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md)). John Paul II, general audience of 28 July 1999: "Rather than a place, hell indicates the state" of those who freely and definitively separate themselves from God, and it is not "a punishment imposed externally by God". Yet CCC 1035 keeps "the punishments of hell" and "chief punishment". The shut door of Matt 25:10-12 holds both descriptions: the foolish virgins shut themselves out, and the bridegroom shut the door.

**Level:** no one is in hell but by his own unrepented mortal sin, and self-exclusion as its description, **at least rung 3** (CCC 1033, 1037); no predestination to sin, **rung 1** ([5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md); 7.3); the 1999 audience, authentic teaching at its lightest grade ([`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md)). Whether the punishment is only the choice's inner consequence or also something God inflicts, and why the will is fixed at death, **rung 5**.

**Keep apart:** whether the 1992 formula is a legitimate development or a softening is **open**: *Mysterium Ecclesiae* (CDF, 1973) lets the Church restate a dogma's meaning in new words ([`fundamental-theology` 5.2](../fundamental-theology/lessons/05-02-newmans-theory-of-development.md)). "The Catechism replaced punishment with self-exclusion" misreads CCC 1035. "A state, not a place" says what hell is, not how long: eternity stays **rung 1** (7.3).

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md)

### Annihilationism

The view that the wicked are finally destroyed and cease to exist rather than punished for ever; in its conditionalist form, that immortality is God's gift to the saved alone.

Defenders: Edward Fudge, *The Fire That Consumes* (1982); tentatively John Stott (1988). Texts: "eternal punishment in destruction, from the face of the Lord" (2 Thess 1:9, DR); "destroy both soul and body in hell" (Matt 10:28, DR). Two doctrines meet it. Make immortality a gift to the saved alone, and Lateran V's *Apostolici regiminis* (1513) is denied ([immortality of the soul](#immortality-of-the-soul)). Grant immortality and let God destroy the wicked anyway, and [*Firmiter*](#firmiter) is denied: "perpetual punishment with the devil" is a punishment undergone, like *Benedictus Deus*'s *cruciantur*.

**Level:** it contradicts **rung 1**: the eternity of the punishment (*Firmiter*), and in its conditionalist form also the soul's natural immortality (Lateran V).

**Keep apart:** answering it with Lateran V alone does not reach the annihilationist who grants natural immortality; the decisive text is *Firmiter*. What may be kept: "destruction" as a true name for a life's ruin without God; and nothing defined says how the pain of sense is felt (6.4, Ex 1).

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md)

**The consummation and the open questions (Module 7)**

### Glorified body

The risen body of the just, wholly at the service of a soul that sees God, traditionally given four gifts: impassibility, brightness, agility, subtlety.

Christ "will reform the body of our lowness, made like to the body of his glory" (Phil 3:21, DR); the four gifts are read off the four contrasts of 1 Cor 15:42-44. The Roman Catechism (Art. XI): these "four are specially mentioned by the Fathers, which they infer from the doctrine of St. Paul, and which are called gifts"; brightness varies by merit, impassibility is equal in all. The posthumous Supplement treats them (qq.82-85). All rise immortal; only the just rise glorified: for Aquinas death, a defect of nature, is undone in all, sin, a defect of the person, only in those joined to Christ (*Summa contra Gentiles* IV.81, paraphrased). Christ's rising is the pattern and cause of ours, its power extending "to the good and wicked alike" (ST III q.56 a.1 ad 3).

**Level:** that the just rise glorified, conformed to Christ's body, **at least rung 3** (Phil 3:21; CCC 999; CDF 1979, point 6); the four gifts as a scheme, **rung 4** (a catechism locates and fixes no level, and this one calls them the Fathers' inference; christology 6.2 grades the same); Christ's rising as cause of all, **rung 4**; the age of the risen (Augustine's "about thirty", *City of God* XXII.15, which he would "not obstinately dispute"), **rung 5**.

**Keep apart:** "the four gifts are dogma because the Roman Catechism lists them" overstates. Mary's bodily glory anticipates the other elect's (1979, point 6; [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 6.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/06-04-the-assumption.md)). What makes the risen body the same is the [sameness objection](#sameness-objection)'s question.

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-resurrection-of-the-body.md)

### Spiritual body

Paul's "spiritual body" (1 Cor 15:44) is a body governed and animated by the Spirit, not a body made of spirit.

"It is sown a natural body: it shall rise a spiritual body" (1 Cor 15:44, DR; *sōma psychikon*, *sōma pneumatikon*). The seed concedes discontinuity, "not the body that shall be" (v.37), and keeps the subject, "to every seed its proper body" (v.38; *idion sōma*, "its own body", the possessive *Firmiter*'s *suis propriis* doubles). Verse 50's "flesh and blood cannot possess the kingdom of God" names a condition, as its twin clause shows: "neither shall corruption possess incorruption". Verse 53's "*this* corruptible" (*touto*) the Roman Catechism reads as "evidently designating by the word this, his own body". Leave v.51 aside: the Douay follows the Vulgate there, not the Greek.

**Level:** that the dead rise in their own bodies, **rung 1** ([resurrection of the body](#resurrection-of-the-body)); the exegesis is [`new-testament` 4.3](../new-testament/lessons/04-03-paul-at-work.md)'s, and v.44's single subject, read for Christ's body, is [`christology` 6.2](../christology/lessons/06-02-resurrection-and-ascension-as-doctrine.md)'s.

**Keep apart:** "spiritual" names what governs the body, not its stuff: changed, not swapped.

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-resurrection-of-the-body.md)

### Sameness objection

If a body's matter is eaten, burned, dispersed or absorbed into other bodies, what makes the risen body the same as the one that died?

The forms. Celsus (in Origen, *Against Celsus* V.14, tr. Crombie): rising in "the self-same flesh" is a hope "which might be cherished by worms"; a wholly corrupted body cannot return, and pleading omnipotence is "a most absurd refuge". The chain of eating (Athenagoras, *On the Resurrection* IV, tr. Pratten): animals eat the unburied dead, men eat those animals, in famine men eat men, so "the same parts cannot rise again with one set of bodies, and with another as well"; Augustine asks the same (*City of God* XXII.20). The modern form: matter exchanged over a life and dispersed after it (the "atoms every seven years" version is graded in [`metaphysics` 6.6](../metaphysics/lessons/06-06-personal-identity-over-time.md)).

The replies 7.1 names, each a school's: **reassembly**: human flesh is not man's natural food, so it never becomes the eater's, and God reunites each part "for the exact construction and formation of the same body" (Athenagoras VIII); eaten flesh is "like a pecuniary loan" that "must be returned to the lender" (Augustine XXII.20). **The form with some of the same matter**: nothing essential to a man passes wholly into nothing, for his soul remains, and matter too; as matter comes and goes in life while he stays one man, he rises with enough of it, God supplying the rest (SCG IV.81 nn.6, 12); the corpse is the same body only equivocally, so sameness runs through the soul ([`thomistic-synthesis` 5.3](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/05-03-the-human-soul-in-the-system.md)). **The soul alone**: Durandus of Saint-Pourçain (d. 1334), the same soul with any matter makes the same body; most manuals required some of the same matter. The Dicastery for the Doctrine of the Faith, replying to Cardinal Zuppi (9 December 2023, approved by Francis in audience): bodily identity is material, though the matter is transfigured, and "does not imply the recuperation of the identical particles of matter"; the resurrection can occur "even if the body has been totally destroyed or dispersed".

**Level:** that all rise "with their own bodies which they now bear", **rung 1** ([*Firmiter*](#firmiter)); that the identical particles are not required, **rung 3** (the 2023 reply); what makes it the same body, by any of the three replies, **rung 5**; reassembly survives as a permitted opinion about what God will do, not a requirement of the dogma.

**Keep apart:** the soul-based replies divide as in [`metaphysics` 6.5](../metaphysics/lessons/06-05-what-a-person-is.md): for a survivalist the person persists in the [separated soul](#separated-soul); for a corruptionist he ceases at death, and the reply needs one more premise, that a substance can exist again after a gap if one of its principles persists through it. "Soul alone suffices" is Durandus's, not Aquinas's. The argument in premises is in the Arguments section.

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-resurrection-of-the-body.md)

### Cremation and burial

The Church prefers burial and permits cremation unless it is chosen for reasons contrary to the faith; the norms are law, not doctrine.

Until 1963 those who chose cremation were refused funeral rites. The Holy Office's *Piam et constantem* (5 July 1963) judged cremation not in itself opposed to the faith and ended the refusal, save where it is chosen in denial of the faith (as *Ad resurgendum cum Christo* §1 reports). Canon 1176 §3 "earnestly recommends" burial and forbids cremation only when chosen "for reasons contrary to Christian doctrine"; can. 1184 §1 2° withholds a Church funeral in that case; CCC 2301. The CDF's *Ad resurgendum cum Christo* (15 August 2016): burial is preferred, the body being part of the person's identity (§3); ashes go to a sacred place, not kept at home, not divided, not scattered on air, land or sea, nor set in jewellery (§§5-7). The 2023 reply to Cardinal Zuppi allows commingled ashes in a defined sacred place, with names recorded, and lets the authority allow "a minimal part" kept in a place that mattered to the dead, ruling out pantheist, naturalist or nihilist readings.

**Level:** the canons, *Ad resurgendum*'s norms and the 2023 permissions, **law, no rung**; so "cremation is contrary to the faith" is false as a general claim. Under the law: the resurrection itself (**rung 1**) and the 2023 reply's gloss on the identical particles (**rung 3**).

**Keep apart:** God needs no ashes; the rite guards what it confesses, that the body is the person's and awaits resurrection, and keeps prayer and memory for the dead.

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-resurrection-of-the-body.md)

### Parousia

Christ's return in glory at the end of history, visibly, to judge the living and the dead.

Greek *parousia*, "arrival". "He shall come again, with glory, to judge both the quick and the dead; whose kingdom shall have no end" (Creed of 381, Schaff). [*Firmiter*](#firmiter): he comes *in fine saeculi*, at the world's end, to render to each, reprobate and elect, according to his works. CCC 673: imminent since the Ascension, and yet "delayed". The date: "Of that day and hour no one knoweth" (Matt 24:36, DR); Acts 1:7; LG 48 ("we know not the day nor the hour"); GS 39; CCC 673, 1040; Augustine calls computing the years left "in vain" (*City of God* XVIII.53). The New Testament's expectation and delay are [`new-testament` 4.3](../new-testament/lessons/04-03-paul-at-work.md) and [6.1](../new-testament/lessons/06-01-the-disputed-pauline-letters-and-the-pastorals.md)'s: Paul's "we who are alive" (1 Thess 4:15 [DR 4:14]); 2 Peter's scoffers, "Where is his promise?" (3:4, DR).

**Level:** the return, to judge all by their works, **rung 1** (the creeds; *Firmiter*); the date unknowable, **at least rung 3** (LG 48; GS 39; CCC 673), a rung-1 reading through the ordinary universal magisterium being arguable but not manifest, so default down (can. 749 §3); preachers may not fix it, **law, no rung** ([*Supernae maiestatis praesidio*](#supernae-maiestatis-praesidio)); reckonings of the end from Daniel's or Revelation's numbers, no standing.

**Keep apart:** "no one knows the day" is taught, not a pious caution. Mark 13:32's "nor the Son" is a question about Christ's human knowledge ([`christology` 4.2](../christology/lessons/04-02-christs-human-knowledge-the-classical-account.md)). What precedes it: the final trial ([Antichrist](#antichrist)); what follows: the [last judgment](#last-judgment) and the [new creation](#new-creation).

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-parousia-the-last-judgment-and-the-new-creation.md)

### Supernae maiestatis praesidio

Lateran V's decree on preaching (19 December 1516), which forbade preachers to fix the time of future evils, the coming of Antichrist, or the day of judgment.

Lateran V, Session XI, Leo X with the council's approval (Mansi vol. 32, col. 946; lesson's translation): preachers are not to "presume to preach or assert a fixed time of future evils, or the coming of Antichrist, or a certain day of judgment" (*tempus praefixum*, *certum diem iudicii*), "for the Truth says that it is not ours to know the times or moments which the Father has put in his own power"; those who have so far dared it "have lied" (*mentitos*), to the harm of those who preach rightly. The reason is Acts 1:7 with one word changed: the Vulgate's *vestrum*, "yours", becomes *nostrum*, "ours", so the council turns the warning on itself. Prophecy is not banned; claimed revelations of future events go to the Apostolic See for examination first. The decree was backed by excommunication.

**Level:** the prohibition is **law, no rung**; it presupposes the teaching that the date is unknowable, **at least rung 3** ([parousia](#parousia)).

**Keep apart:** the ban from the doctrine under it. The same shape, a discipline resting on a doctrine, as the 1944 decree on [mitigated millenarianism](#mitigated-millenarianism) ([`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md) teaches the separation).

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-parousia-the-last-judgment-and-the-new-creation.md)

### Pilgrim Church

The Church on earth as the kingdom begun but not finished: holy but imperfect, perfected only in glory.

*Lumen Gentium* ch. VII, §48 (paraphrased): the Church attains her full perfection only in the glory of heaven; the restoration has begun in Christ, and the world's renewal is "irrevocably decreed" and anticipated in her real but imperfect holiness; until the new heavens and earth she bears the form of this passing world. The final sorting waits for the harvest, which "is the end of the world" (Matt 13:39, DR; CCC 681), and belongs to Christ, so the parable is no policy for now and does not abolish her discipline ([`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 5.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/05-04-a-church-of-sinners.md)). She enters glory by following her Lord through death, not by "a historic triumph" (CCC 677).

**Level:** **at least rung 3** (LG 48; *Lumen Gentium* defined nothing new).

**Keep apart:** LG 49-50, on the communion with the Church in heaven, are [communion of saints](#communion-of-saints)'s (6.2). Handed over by [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 1.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/01-04-people-of-god-communion-sacrament.md) and 5.4.

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-parousia-the-last-judgment-and-the-new-creation.md)

### Antichrist

The supreme religious deception, a pseudo-messianism in which man glorifies himself in God's place, at the height of the Church's final trial before Christ returns.

CCC 675-677, paraphrased: before Christ's return the Church must pass through a trial that will shake the faith of many (Luke 18:8); its height is the Antichrist's deception, man glorifying himself in place of God (2 Thess 2:3-4; DR "the man of sin ... the son of perdition"); she enters glory by following her Lord through a final Passover, not by "a historic triumph". The deception is already here: 1 John names present "Antichrists" while presupposing the one expected, "as you have heard that Antichrist cometh" (2:18, DR); CCC 676 says it takes shape whenever the messianic hope is claimed within history ([secular messianism](#secular-messianism)).

**Level:** a final trial and the Antichrist's deception before the return, **at least rung 3** (CCC 675-677, summing up a constant teaching no act defines); that the Antichrist is one individual man, head of the wicked (Aquinas, ST III q.8 a.8), **rung 4**; identifying him with any person or power, **no level**; preaching the time of his coming, forbidden by **law** (Lateran V).

**Keep apart:** [`new-testament` 6.4](../new-testament/lessons/06-04-peter-jude-and-the-letters-of-john.md)'s reading of 1 John (deniers already present) is reconciled, not contradicted: the present Antichrists presuppose the expected one. The Church expects her own Passion before her glory, and names neither date nor actors.

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-parousia-the-last-judgment-and-the-new-creation.md)

### Millenarianism

The expectation of a reign of Christ on earth, with the risen just, before the end of history; CCC 676 widens the word to any claim to realize the kingdom inside history.

Patristic chiliasm: Papias, Justin and Irenaeus had the risen just reign on a renewed earth before the judgment ([`patristics` 2.2](../patristics/lessons/02-02-irenaeus-recapitulation.md)); Justin granted that many "true Christians, think otherwise" (*Dialogue with Trypho* 80). Augustine, who once held a spiritual form of it, read Revelation 20's thousand years as the Church's age (*City of God* XX.7; [`new-testament` 6.5](../new-testament/lessons/06-05-revelation.md)). In 1944 the Holy Office judged that the system "cannot safely be taught" ([mitigated millenarianism](#mitigated-millenarianism)). CCC 676 extends the name to every claim to realize within history the messianic hope that only the final judgment fulfils, above all a [secular messianism](#secular-messianism). CCC 1047 still quotes Irenaeus's millenarian chapter (*Against Heresies* V.32.1) for creation restored, minus the reign.

**Level:** the Fathers as authors, **no magisterial level**, and no Father is censured by name; Augustine's thousand years, **rung 5** as exegesis; the system of Christ's visible reign on earth before the final judgment, cannot safely be taught, **rung 3**.

**Keep apart:** "millenarianism is heresy" overstates; "an open option because Irenaeus held it" understates.

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-parousia-the-last-judgment-and-the-new-creation.md)

### Mitigated millenarianism

The view that Christ will come visibly to reign on this earth before the final judgment, with or without a prior resurrection of many just.

Holy Office decree of 19 July 1944, approved by Pius XII. Asked what to think of the system of mitigated millenarianism, which teaches that Christ the Lord will come visibly to this earth to reign before the final judgment, whether or not a resurrection of many just comes first, it replied: *Systema Millenarismi mitigati tuto doceri non posse*, "the system of mitigated millenarianism cannot safely be taught". CCC 676 cites it. Irenaeus's scheme fits the description, though no Father is named.

**Level:** **rung 3**, a dicastery's doctrinal decree with papal approval. Its note is a **safety** note: not "heretical", not "false"; holding the view is not heresy, teaching it is unsafe. (New-testament 6.5's "at least rung 3" agrees, sharpened here to the decree's own note.)

**Keep apart:** "heresy" overstates; "open, because a Father held it" understates. The decree is a doctrinal judgment, unlike Lateran V's ban on dates, which is law.

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-parousia-the-last-judgment-and-the-new-creation.md)

### Secular messianism

The claim to realize the kingdom of God inside history by human or political means.

CCC 676, paraphrased: the Antichrist's deception takes shape whenever the claim is made to realize within history the messianic hope that only the eschatological judgment fulfils; the Church has rejected even modified forms of this falsification of the kingdom under the name of millenarianism, especially the "intrinsically perverse" political form of a secular messianism. CCC 677: not "a historic triumph of the Church through a progressive ascendancy". *Gaudium et Spes* 39, paraphrased: the hope of a new earth must quicken our care for this one; earthly progress must be "carefully distinguished" from the growth of Christ's kingdom, yet matters vitally to it insofar as it betters society; the goods of dignity, brotherhood and freedom nurtured here we will find again, "transfigured". GS 39 steers between two errors: progress mistaken for the kingdom, and the earth treated as disposable. *Laudato Si'* §244 (2015): all the good here will be taken up into the heavenly feast.

**Level:** CCC 676's rejection, **at least rung 3**; GS 39's distinction, **at least rung 3** (a pastoral constitution's doctrinal principle, [`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md)); *Laudato Si'* §§243-244, **rung 3**.

**Keep apart:** rejecting secular messianism is not contempt for this world: work for it is not wasted. The practice is [`catholic-social-teaching`](../catholic-social-teaching/syllabus.md)'s.

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-parousia-the-last-judgment-and-the-new-creation.md)

### Hope for all

Balthasar's position: Christians may, and even must, hope and pray that all human beings will be saved, while asserting nothing about the outcome and holding damnation a real possibility for each, oneself first.

Hans Urs von Balthasar, *Was dürfen wir hoffen?* (1986) and *Kleiner Diskurs über die Hölle* (1987); in English *Dare We Hope "That All Men Be Saved"? With a Short Discourse on Hell* (Ignatius, 1988). Paraphrased: the New Testament gives two series of texts, two outcomes (judgment and the fire, Matt 25) and God willing and able to save all (1 Tim 2:4; John 12:32; Rom 11:32), and neither may be dissolved into the other. So no one knows the outcome, and the Christian may and must hope for all, since grace may move even the worst sinner to repent before death (as Dulles reports); one chapter is "The obligation to hope for all". He rejects a hell emptied at the end. The four data any Catholic position must hold (7.3): God wills all saved; hell is eternal; none is lost but by his own fault; the warnings are real. The three positions: many are lost, and we know it (long dominant: Augustine's "many more", *City of God* XXI.12; Aquinas's "minority", ST I q.23 a.7 ad 3); hope for all; [universalism](#universalism) asserted. The critics: Ralph Martin, *Will Many Be Saved?* (Eerdmans, 2012), on *Lumen Gentium* 16's *at saepius*, "but often", and a hope heard as near-certainty draining evangelization; Germain Grisez and Peter F. Ryan (*New Blackfriars* 95, 2014), warnings that must be "truthful information"; Avery Dulles ("The Population of Hell", *First Things*, May 2003), "orthodox" but "at least adventurous". The defence: a warning is true if the danger is real; the universal texts are as plain as the threats; a hope that asserts nothing cannot honestly be preached as a forecast.

**Level:** **rung 5**: no act condemns it and none teaches it. What is taught around it: in hope the Church prays for all to be saved (CCC 1821; 1058), **at least rung 3**; damnation a real possibility for every living person (general audience, 28 July 1999; CCC 1036), **at least rung 3**; no one knows without special revelation whom God has chosen ([Trent VI](#trent-session-vi) ch. 12), **at least rung 3**; the devil's perpetual punishment, **rung 1**, so the hope concerns human beings. "Many are lost, and we know it": an opinion, **rung 5**, which no act ever taught. Francis's interview of 14 January 2024 ("I like to think of hell as empty", lesson's translation, prefaced "not a dogma of faith") and John Paul II's book *Crossing the Threshold of Hope* (1994, "unequivocal", as Dulles quotes it): **no magisterial level**.

**Keep apart:** Balthasar did not teach that hell is empty. The Church teaches that she prays in hope for all, not the hope that all together are saved ([hope for each and for all](#hope-for-each-and-for-all)). Balthasar's Holy Saturday is a separate dispute ([`christology` 6.1](../christology/lessons/06-01-he-descended-into-hell.md)).

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md)

### Hope for each and for all

Hoping of every person, one at a time, that he will be saved, as against hoping that all together will be saved.

The lesson's analogy: you can hope of every lottery ticket that it wins, knowing they will not all win. Augustine, *City of God* XXI.24 (c. 426, tr. Dods): the Church prays for all her enemies who yet live, "since she has this certainty regarding no man"; "and yet she is not heard in behalf of all". The ground of the prayer is ignorance; the denial of the hope for all comes from Matt 25:41, 46 read as a forecast (it is "excessively presumptuous" to deny the eternal punishment of those God said shall go away). Balthasar keeps the prayer and declines the "and yet". CCC 1821 ("In hope, the Church prays for 'all men to be saved'") and 1058 (that no one be lost) teach the prayer without settling which hope it carries.

**Level:** hope for each is common ground (Augustine, Grisez and Ryan, Balthasar), resting on teaching at **at least rung 3** (the prayer for all; the [universal salvific will](#universal-salvific-will)); whether the prayer licenses hope for all together, or only for each, **rung 5**.

**Keep apart:** to hope a murderer is saved is to hope he repents, not that the murder stops mattering: grace "does not make wrong into right" (*Spe Salvi* §44; 7.3, Ex 1).

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md)

### Threat and prediction

Whether Christ's warnings of damnation are true as warnings of a real danger (a threat, "minatory") or as reports of an outcome (a prediction).

The lesson's sign: "Dangerous current. Swimmers drown here." It is honest if the current is real, needs no body count, and is no lie because the lifeguard hopes no one drowns; written by someone who sees the future, the same words could be a report. Dulles (2003): Balthasar's position can be reconciled with Scripture if Jesus's statements on hell are "minatory rather than predictive". Grisez and Ryan (2014): Christ would have been dishonest to move hearers with warnings that were not "truthful information"; the warnings convey that more than a few will be lost. The texts: Matt 7:13-14; Luke 13:23-24, where, asked whether few are saved, Jesus commands and forecasts: "Strive to enter by the narrow gate: for many, I say to you, shall seek to enter and shall not be able" (DR); Matt 25:46's "these shall go", a plain future (*apeleusontai*). CCC 1036 reads such texts as "an urgent call to conversion".

**Level:** any reading of Matt 25:46 as forecast or as warning, **rung 5**: no act fixes its sense. The verse's inerrancy is **rung 1**; grading a reading at rung 1 because Scripture cannot err confuses the two.

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md)

### Universalism

The claim that all will in fact be saved, held as known rather than hoped.

Two forms. **Restorationist**: hell is temporary or purgative, as in Origen's [*apokatastasis*](#apokatastasis) (6.4) and David Bentley Hart, an Eastern Orthodox theologian (*That All Shall Be Saved*, Yale, 2019). Hart, paraphrased: a God who creates freely out of nothing answers for every final end he permits; a rational will is moved by its nature toward the Good, so a creature freed of all ignorance and compulsion cannot finally refuse God; so all will be saved, as a conclusion and not a hope; *aiōnios* means "of the age". The philosopher Marilyn McCord Adams argued the same from God's defeat of horrendous evils ([`apologetics-foundations` 2.3](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/02-03-evil-and-hiddenness-the-replies-that-cost-something.md); [`philosophy-of-religion` 4.3](../philosophy-of-religion/lessons/04-03-theodicies.md)). **Empty hell, known**: an eternal hell certainly empty of human beings.

**Level:** excluded, at two levels. A hell that ends contradicts **rung 1**: *Firmiter*'s *poenam perpetuam* and the [Athanasian Creed](#athanasian-creed) (not *Benedictus Deus*, which fixes only the immediate descent). An eternal hell known to be empty contradicts **at least rung 3**: Trent VI ch. 12, "except by special revelation, it cannot be known whom God hath chosen unto Himself" (Waterworth), and the 1999 audience's "real possibility"; it is not heresy, since no definition says that any human being is damned. Hart and Adams as authors: **no magisterial level**.

**Keep apart:** to hope is permitted (**rung 5**, [hope for all](#hope-for-all)); to know is not. In 7.3's map, Balthasar holds the four data by refusing to know; Hart fails at the second, hell's eternity.

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md)

### Limbo of the fathers

The resting place of the just who died before Christ, "Abraham's bosom", whom Christ freed when he descended to the dead; not the limbo of children.

CCC 633: the just who died before Christ, "while they await the Redeemer", in "Abraham's bosom" (Luke 16:22). Aquinas calls it the "hell of the Fathers" (ST III q.52 a.2); "limbo" for it is the Supplement's word, and Aquinas's own at II-II q.2 a.7 ad 2. The difference from the children's limbo (Suppl. q.69 a.6): the fathers had faith, grace and hope, and their stay was temporary; the children, on the theory, had none of these. Christ's descent did not deliver children who died in original sin (ST III q.52 a.7): deliverance came by union with his Passion, and the three channels to it (the child's own faith, the parents', a sacrament) were closed; and the vision needs grace. The Greek Catholic funeral for any infant, baptised or not, asks that the child be received into "the bosom of Abraham" (ITC 2007, §100).

**Level:** **rung 3** (CCC 633, as christology 6.1 grades it). The limbo of children was always a hypothesis, **rung 5** ([limbo](#limbo)).

**Keep apart:** "Christ emptied limbo on Holy Saturday": he freed the fathers (CCC 633), not the children (III q.52 a.7). Owned by [`christology` 6.1](../christology/lessons/06-01-he-descended-into-hell.md).

*Lessons:* [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

### Maiores Ecclesiae causas

Innocent III's letter of late 1201 to Humbert (Ymbertus), archbishop of Arles, distinguishing the penalty of original sin from that of actual sin.

"The punishment of original sin is the lack of the vision of God (*carentia visionis Dei*); the punishment of actual sin is the torment of everlasting hell" (lesson's translation; *gehennae perpetuae cruciatus*). The letter insists that God gave the infants who die daily "some remedy" (*aliquod remedium*) for salvation: baptism. 6.4 cites the same sentence for the pain of loss as chief punishment.

**Level:** a papal letter, **at least rung 3**; its core coincides with the rung-1 content of Florence's clause, that those dying with original sin only lack the vision ([solo originali clause](#solo-originali-clause)).

**Keep apart:** it splits the penalties by kind of sin, the root of the schools' loss-only limbo, but names no limbo and decides no infant's case ([pain of loss and pain of sense](#pain-of-loss-and-pain-of-sense)).

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md), [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

### Solo originali clause

The conciliar clause that the souls of those who die in actual mortal sin, or with original sin only, go down at once into hell, to be punished with unlike punishments.

Florence, *Laetentur caeli* (6 July 1439): *Illorum autem animas, qui in actuali mortali peccato vel solo originali decedunt, mox in infernum descendere, poenis tamen disparibus puniendas*: "But the souls of those who die in actual mortal sin, or with original sin only, go down at once into hell", "to be punished, however, with unlike punishments" (lesson's translation). Michael VIII's profession at Lyons II (6 July 1274) reads *in mortali peccato vel cum solo originali*, without *actuali*. Three words carry it. *Infernum* is the whole lower world, the children's "hell" included (ST III q.52 a.2; *Auctorem fidei*'s "that place of the lower regions"). *Disparibus* says unlike, not whether in kind (loss without sense, the schools) or in degree (Augustine's mildest condemnation). *Mox*, at once, is the clause's thrust (ITC 2007, §37; [particular judgment](#particular-judgment)). It names no infant and no limbo.

**Level:** **rung 1 as worded**, through Florence's *diffinimus*; at Lyons II, a profession that defines nothing of itself. Hence "those who die with original sin only lack the vision of God", **rung 1**. Whether any infant dies so, **rung 5**: no act says, and the ITC grants the clause and asks only whether these infants must die in that state (§§37, 83). So the shorthand "the fate of unbaptized infants, rung 5" ([`fundamental-theology` 4.4](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-04-the-ladder-of-doctrinal-authority.md)) splits into the conditional (rung 1) and its application (rung 5).

**Keep apart:** "Florence defined limbo", or the punishment of infants, overstates; "merely medieval, not binding" understates. The empty class: for Aquinas no one past the age of reason dies with original sin only (I-II q.89 a.6), so a hope for every unbaptised infant would leave the clause describing nobody; a conditional can be true and grave with no member known, as the Church defines the fate of those dying in mortal sin and names none ([7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md)). On translation: 7.4 renders *disparibus* "unlike", since "unequal" (6.4's word) quietly decides for degree.

*Lessons:* [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

### Auctorem fidei

Pius VI's constitution of 28 August 1794 condemning propositions of the Jansenist Synod of Pistoia (1786); its proposition 26 rejects the charge that limbo is a Pelagian fable.

Prop. 26 censures the doctrine that rejects as a Pelagian fable "that place of the lower regions" (which the faithful commonly call the limbo of children), where souls departing with original guilt alone are punished with the pain of loss without the pain of fire, as if that brought in the Pelagians' middle state "free of guilt and of punishment" (lesson's translation of fragments). The proposition's own note: "false, rash, injurious to the Catholic schools" (*falsa, temeraria, in scholas catholicas iniuriosa*), not "heretical". Each proposition of the bull carries its own censure: it is not an [*in globo*](#in-globo-censure) list. Popes from the sixteenth to the eighteenth century had protected the right to teach Augustine's sterner view in the same way, fire included (ITC 2007, §26).

**Level:** **at least rung 3** (censures below heresy; default down). What it protects is a **permission**, that limbo is not Pelagianism and may be taught, not limbo's truth (ITC §38).

**Keep apart:** "*Auctorem fidei* made limbo Church teaching" overstates. The limbo proposition is prop. 26, not prop. 29 (transubstantiation); and the bull's censures are not collective ([`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md)'s description of a collective list does not fit it). The Synod's charge belongs with the [Jansenists](#jansenism) and the anti-Pelagian canons ([Pelagianism](#pelagianism)).

*Lessons:* [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

### ITC 2007

The International Theological Commission's study *The Hope of Salvation for Infants Who Die Without Being Baptised* (2007), which finds grounds for hope, not sure knowledge, that such infants are saved.

Approved *in forma specifica* by the Commission's members; Cardinal Levada, with Benedict XVI's approval given in audience on 19 January 2007, approved it for publication; published April 2007. Its route, as 7.4 gives it in prose (outline only; the premises are the Arguments section's): argue from the better known, since these infants' fate is unrevealed (§§41, 79); God wills all saved and did not bind his power to the sacraments (§82, citing ST III q.64 a.7), so the sacrament's necessity is not absolute while Christ's is; it grants that those who die in original sin lack the vision, and asks whether these infants must die in it (§§37, 83, taking up Innocent III's "some remedy"); possible remedies: conformity to Christ in their death, the Holy Innocents' pattern, a direct gift like Mary's preservation (§§85-87); Christ's solidarity with all outranks Adam's (§§88-91; Rom 5:15-20; *Gaudium et Spes* 22); the Church that supplies faith at the font might supply a desire beyond it (§98); her funeral prayer is evidence (§100; [lex orandi](#lex-orandi)); conclusion, grounds for hope, not knowledge (§102); the duty to baptize stands (§103). On limbo: it "never entered into the dogmatic definitions", though the Magisterium at times mentioned it in ordinary teaching until Vatican II (opening summary); it remains possible, "a possible theological hypothesis" (opening summary), "a possible theological opinion" (§41), yet "problematic" (§§90, 95); Aquinas's natural-happiness limbo must not be confused with the later [pure nature](#pure-nature) (§23).

**Level:** **no magisterial level** (an advisory study; [`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md)). The hope it argues for is the one the Church permits: she can only entrust these children to God's mercy, as in the funeral rite (CDF, *Pastoralis actio* §13, 1980), and CCC 1261 lets us hope, **rung 3**; the funeral rite itself, **law, no rung**.

**Keep apart:** "limbo was never taught": say never **defined**; it was common teaching until the mid-twentieth century (ITC §26). The study's genre and its §102 are [`sacraments-and-liturgy` 2.2](../sacraments-and-liturgy/lessons/02-02-necessity-infants-and-baptism-of-desire-and-blood.md)'s.

*Lessons:* [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

## Arguments

The canonical arguments as numbered-premise skeletons, in the form the lessons give them, with each premise's source in brackets. An argument carries no more weight than its weakest premise, so each skeleton ends by marking what is defined and what is a school's. **Six are outline only, and a seventh in part**, because a Boss part asks you to build them (4(a), 5(a), 5(b), 6(b), 7(b), 7(d)): there the card names the steps the lesson teaches and stops.

**Creation (Module 1)**

### Argument: the beginning held by faith

Why the world's beginning needs a warrant of its own, and where it lies.

1. All that is not God has its whole being from him, out of nothing. [*Firmiter*; *Dei Filius* can. 5: **rung 1**]
2. "Out of nothing" is not "after nothing": an eternalist could hold the world made "from nothing, not ... after nothing". [ST I q.46 a.2 ad 2]
3. So dependence does not entail a beginning. [1, 2]
4. The beginning has its own act: *Firmiter*'s *ab initio temporis*, which *Dei Filius* ch. 1 repeats and no canon of Vatican I contains. [**rung 1** through *Firmiter*]
5. Reason's part is disputed: "By faith alone do we hold, and by no demonstration can it be proved, that the world did not always exist" (Aquinas, q.46 a.2); Bonaventure held it demonstrable (*Sentences* II). [**rung 5**]

∴ The world began, and we know it by faith (**rung 1**); whether reason can prove it is open (**rung 5**); no cosmology settles it ([`apologetics-foundations` 4.3](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/04-03-cosmology-what-a-beginning-would-and-would-not-show.md)). See [beginning in time](#beginning-in-time), [creation out of nothing](#creation-out-of-nothing).

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-creation-out-of-nothing.md)

### Argument: one principle, no nature of evil

Augustine against the Manichees (*Concerning the Nature of Good* 1, 4), and the case he answers.

**Faustus's case** (*Reply to Faustus* XXI.1, tr. Stothert): (a) there are two principles, God and Hyle, "or ... the devil", and two principles are not two gods; (b) all the power of evil belongs to Hyle, all the power of good to God; (c) so no evil comes from the good God, and no theodicy is needed. The ethic follows: "I think all flesh unclean" (VI.1).

**Augustine's reply:**

1. God is the highest good, and unchangeable.
2. All other goods are "from Him, not of Him": made, not portions of God.
3. Made out of nothing, they are changeable.
4. "Every nature, so far as it is nature, is good."
5. Ask what evil is before asking whence it comes: it is corruption of the measure, form or order that belong to a nature.
6. So a corrupted nature is evil so far as it is corrupted and good so far as it is nature. [5]

∴ Evil is parasitic on a good nature and needs no principle of its own: one source of all, nothing evil in what it is. Levels: one principle and no nature of evil, **rung 1** ([*Firmiter*](#firmiter); [Decree for the Jacobites](#decree-for-the-jacobites) defines the negative half); the privation analysis of 5-6, common teaching, **rung 4** ([evil as privation](#evil-as-privation)). Why such [dualism](#dualism) was condemned rather than tolerated: it denies the Creed's first article.

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-and-god-saw-that-it-was-good.md)

**The human person (Module 2)**

### Argument: the soul created, not generated

1. The intellective soul's understanding is no bodily process, so the soul subsists in its own right. [[`thomistic-synthesis` 5.3](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/05-03-the-human-soul-in-the-system.md); [2.2](lessons/02-02-body-and-soul-one-substance.md)]
2. A spiritual substance "cannot be caused through generation, but only through creation by God". [ST I q.118 a.2]
3. So the soul is neither a subtle body sown with the seed (Tertullian's [traducianism](#traducianism)) nor produced by the parents' souls ([generationism](#generationism)). [1, 2]
4. Parents still beget a human being, not a body awaiting a tenant: "man begets his like" by disposing matter for its form. [q.118 a.2 ad 4]

∴ God creates each soul with no created cause between, which is all "immediately" means: it names no moment and no gap in embryology. Levels: the conclusion, **at least rung 3** ([immediate creation](#immediate-creation-of-the-soul); HG §36); 2, **rung 4**; the timing, **rung 5** ([time of ensoulment](#time-of-ensoulment)).

**The pressure the other way.** Augustine to Jerome (Letter 166 §10, 415): if souls are made new at each birth, "where, in the case of infant children, is sin committed by these souls"? Traducianism explains original sin at a stroke; the creationist owes another route, and Trent names none ([transmission of original sin](#transmission-of-original-sin)).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-trent-session-v-the-definition.md)

### Argument: the soul immortal by nature

1. The human soul is a subsistent form. [[2.2](lessons/02-02-body-and-soul-one-substance.md); [`thomistic-synthesis` 5.3](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/05-03-the-human-soul-in-the-system.md)]
2. "It is impossible for a subsistent form to cease to exist." [ST I q.75 a.6]
3. Reduction to nothing would imply "not in the creature a potentiality to non-existence, but in the Creator the power of ceasing to sustain existence". [ad 2]

∴ The soul is immortal by nature and held in being by God: a gift only as all existence is, not a [preternatural gift](#preternatural-gifts) like Adam's [bodily immortality](#immunity-from-death). Levels: immortal and one for each body, **rung 1** ([*Apostolici regiminis*](#apostolici-regiminis); CDF 1998, §11); whether reason proves it, **rung 5** (Aquinas yes; Scotus found no argument compelling; Cajetan doubted that Aristotle proves it). Lateran V condemns asserting mortality true "at least according to philosophy", not doubting a proof.

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md)

### Argument: the weight of immediate creation

Why 2.3 grades it **at least rung 3, rung 1 when so argued**.

*For rung 1, by the ordinary universal magisterium:*

1. *Humani Generis* §36 ascribes the obligation to "the Catholic faith" (*catholica fides nos retinere iubet*).
2. *Retinere* means keeping what is already held.
3. The teaching is constant since Anastasius II (498).
4. It follows from defined truths.

*Against:*

5. No act proposes it as revealed.
6. *Humani Generis* says it in a parenthesis.
7. The CDF's 1998 commentary omits it from its examples.
8. Can. 749 §3 defaults down.

∴ **At least rung 3** is the floor, and anything below it is wrong; rung 1 is defensible only when argued from 1-4. Paul VI's *Credo* (1968) confesses God creator of each soul without the word "immediately".

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-the-soul-immediately-created-and-immortal.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-creation-and-evolution-in-church-teaching.md)

**Original sin (Module 3)**

### Argument: the first man created in grace

Aquinas runs backward from the loss (ST I q.95 a.1).

1. The first state's rectitude: reason subject to God, the lower powers to reason, the body to the soul; and the first subjection caused the other two.
2. The second and third were not from nature, "otherwise [they] would have remained after sin".
3. What sin destroyed cannot have been nature, which survives sin even in the demons; and an effect cannot outrun its cause.

∴ The first subjection was "a supernatural endowment of grace", and integrity stood or fell with it, which is why the gifts fell together. Levels: Aquinas hedges ("seems to require ... as others say"), so this is the better opinion, not faith; Trent wrote *constitutus*, not *creatus*, so as not to decide: **rung 5** ([constituted, not created](#constitutus-not-creatus)). Lombard, Bonaventure and the Scotists held that grace came later.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-original-justice-and-the-fall.md)

### Argument: Rahner on monogenism, 1954 and 1967

**1954** ("Theologisches zum Monogenismus"):

1. Original sin is a situation of damnation that precedes every personal choice yet arises in history.
2. Such a situation is inconceivable unless it began in one real individual's act.

∴ Monogenism, never infallibly taught, "must be affirmed with inner (but not in itself irreformable) assent" (as quoted by McMahon, 2002).

**1967** ("Evolution and Original Sin"):

1. Biology finds genetic unity in a population, not an individual.
2. God meant the first humans to hand grace on to one another.
3. A first sin in the first community, by one or several, tore that web for all.

∴ He saw "no reason" for the Magisterium to intervene against polygenism; *Foundations* (1976) makes original sin the [situation of guilt](#situation-of-guilt) that every free act is co-determined by, reaching back to the first sin.

Levels: Rahner as author, **no magisterial level**; *Humani Generis* §37 still stands at **rung 3**, its reason epistemic ([monogenism and polygenism](#monogenism-and-polygenism)). Any proposal is tested clause by clause against Trent ([clause-by-clause test](#clause-by-clause-test)).

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md)

### Argument: what the genetics excludes

1. Human genetic variation implies a long-term effective population of about ten thousand (Takahata, 1993).
2. So no single pair could be our sole ancestors within roughly the last 500,000 years, on stated assumptions.
3. Genealogy is not genetics: a recent pair can be a genealogical ancestor of everyone without being the sole source of our genes (Rohde, Olson and Chang, 2004; Swamidass, 2019).

∴ The data exclude only a sole-ancestor pair inside the window: not an older pair, nor a pair among many. "Genetics has disproved Adam" overstates; Hössjer and Gauger (2019), a minority, argue that a single recent couple fits. No step has a rung; the doctrinal weight is *Humani Generis* §37's (**rung 3**). See [population genetics and the first pair](#population-genetics-and-the-first-pair), [genealogical and genetic ancestry](#genealogical-and-genetic-ancestry), and Kemp's [biological and theological species](#biological-and-theological-species).

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-monogenism-polygenism-and-the-modern-reinterpretations.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md)

**Grace (Module 4)**

### Argument: grace a good caused in the soul

Aquinas, ST I-II q.110 a.1.

1. "Grace" is said in three senses: favour, a gift freely given, and thanks. [[three senses of grace](#three-senses-of-grace)]
2. Man's love presupposes the good it loves; God's love causes it, since "the creature's good springs from the Divine will".
3. So "every love of God is followed at some time by a good caused in the creature, but not co-eternal with the eternal love".

∴ God's grace never names a favour that leaves us as we were. Levels: inherence, against grace as "only the favour of God", **rung 1** (Trent VI can. 11); grace as a quality or habit, the schools' account, **rung 4** ([sanctifying grace](#sanctifying-grace)). The argument against "favour only" is a.1's; q.110 a.2 is the one on quality and motion.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-what-grace-is.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md)

### Argument: the necessity of grace

**Outline only: Boss 4(a) asks you to reconstruct q.109 a.2.** The steps the lesson teaches, in the article's order:

- the starting point: what human nature can do in its two states, integrity and corruption;
- fallen nature still works "some particular good" ("build dwellings, plant vineyards") yet "cannot do all the good natural to it";
- the count: "for one reason ... but for two reasons", then "Beyond this";
- a principle the article relies on without stating it here, that acts are proportioned to their end (a.5; drilled in [`moral-theology` 3.3](../moral-theology/lessons/03-03-the-cardinal-virtues-and-the-gifts.md));
- the helps that result are stated at [the three helps](#the-three-helps); the [*de auxiliis*](#de-auxiliis) quarrel concerns the first, God's motion of the will (strictly, that motion as efficacious grace: [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)).

The doctrine the article explains: grace is needed for every [salutary act](#salutary-act), **rung 1** (Orange II can. 7; Trent VI cann. 1-3), and the line runs between natural and salutary acts, not between good acts and sins ([necessity of grace](#necessity-of-grace)).

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md)

### Argument: grace the root of the virtues

ST I-II q.110 aa.3-4.

1. A virtue presupposes a nature it perfects.
2. The infused virtues dispose man toward a higher end, God as he is, and so "in relation to some higher nature".
3. Grace supplies that nature, as "a participation of the Divine Nature" (2 Pet 1:4): one gift, also named being "born again sons of God" and "the light of grace".
4. As the natural light of reason stands to the acquired virtues, so the light of grace to the [infused virtues](#infused-virtues).

∴ Grace is seated in the soul's essence, the virtues in its powers (a.4). Levels: inherence, **rung 1** (VI can. 11); this account of it, **rung 4**; whether grace and charity are really distinct, **rung 5** (Thomists and Suárez distinguish them; Scotus and Bellarmine identify them).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md)

### Argument: Rahner on uncreated grace

His 1939 essay, paraphrased.

1. Scripture and the Fathers make created grace the consequence of God's self-gift; Scholasticism made it the basis.
2. In the vision the divine essence is united to the intellect "as the object actually understood" (ST I q.12 a.2 ad 3): almost as a form, not as an efficient cause.
3. Grace is glory begun.

∴ God's self-gift comes first, by [quasi-formal causality](#quasi-formal-causality); created grace disposes for it; and the graced are related to each Person as himself, the three remaining one efficient cause. Levels: **rung 5**, against the Thomist order at **rung 4**; *Mystici Corporis* §78 declines to censure either. What both keep: the Giver given and the gift inhering (**rung 1**) ([uncreated and created grace](#uncreated-and-created-grace)).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-sanctifying-grace-the-indwelling-and-divinization.md)

### Argument: from the natural desire to see God

ST I q.12 a.1, the text every side reads.

1. "There resides in every man a natural desire to know the cause of any effect which he sees; and thence arises wonder."
2. If the created intellect "could not reach so far as to the first cause of things, the natural desire would remain void".
3. [Left implicit: a natural desire is not void.]

∴ Against those who held that no created intellect *can* see God's essence: it can. The dispute is over what "natural" and "void" mean ([natural desire to see God](#natural-desire-to-see-god); [innate and elicited desire](#innate-and-elicited-desire)):

- **The commentators** (Cajetan through Suárez to Garrigou-Lagrange): the desire is elicited, woken by knowledge (of supernatural effects, said Cajetan; of God's existence, said Báñez, for whom it is also conditional); nature's correlate is an [obediential potency](#obediential-potency); so [pure nature](#pure-nature) is possible. **Feingold** (2001; 2nd ed. 2010) restated it: q.12 a.1 proves the vision possible, not due; I-II q.3 a.8's desire is wonder, elicited by God's effects; only knowledge makes a desire conditional, so an innate one would be an absolute demand; and a conditional desire is not "void" when unmet.
- **De Lubac** (*Surnaturel*, 1946; 1965): a spirit is the creature whose desire ends only in God; the desire innate and absolute, the end one, the gratuity creation's; a spirit God made uncalled would be "another being than I".
- **Rahner** (1950): the unconditional ordering is real, but a part of nature God's goodness could not frustrate would make grace owed; so it is a [supernatural existential](#supernatural-existential), unowed.

Levels: which reading is right, **rung 5**; that the vision is unowed, **rung 1** and **at least rung 3** ([gratuity of the supernatural](#gratuity-of-the-supernatural)). The distinction the debate needs: "beyond nature's powers" is not "not owed to nature" ([Baius](#baius), prop. 11).

*Lessons:* [4.4](lessons/04-04-nature-and-grace-the-gratuity-of-the-supernatural.md)

**Justification, merit, and predestination (Module 5)**

### Argument: Trent's chain of causes

**Outline only: Boss 5(a) asks you to tabulate the causes and read the formal-cause sentence.** Chapter 7 answers five questions about one change, in this order:

- for what end (the final cause);
- by whom (the efficient cause);
- through whose merit (the meritorious cause, Christ's passion, as [`christology` 5.3](../christology/lessons/05-03-satisfaction-anselm-and-aquinas.md) reads it);
- by what instrument (the instrumental cause, baptism);
- by what form a person is made just (the one [formal cause](#formal-cause)).

Around it, the decree's path: preparation under prevenient grace (ch. 5-6), justification (ch. 7-8), increase (ch. 10), loss by mortal sin (ch. 15), recovery through penance (ch. 14). The positions the formal-cause sentence closes off are at [double justice](#double-justice), [Osiander](#osiander) and [imputation](#imputation); its canons and level at [causes of justification](#causes-of-justification).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-trent-session-vi-justification.md)

### Argument: merit by divine ordination

**Outline only: Boss 5(b) asks you to reconstruct q.114 a.1 and to use a.3.** The steps the lesson teaches:

- merit is a reward owed for what is freely done, and between God and man there is no equality;
- so merit "only exists on the presupposition of the Divine ordination", and God is a debtor only to himself (a.1, ad 3);
- a.3 splits one act by its two principles, free will and "the grace of the Holy Ghost", and gives congruity to the one and condignity to the other;
- the "reward, not wage" point and can. 32's level are [`theology-of-debt` 3.2](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-02-the-fathers-alms-satisfaction-and-ransom.md)'s; the first grace is [Orange II](#orange-ii) can. 18's.

Levels: that the justified truly merit, **rung 1** (VI can. 32); *condign* and *congruous*, words no act uses, **rung 4** at most. See [merit](#merit), [divine ordination](#divine-ordination), [condign and congruous merit](#condign-and-congruous-merit).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-merit.md)

### Argument: the asymmetry of predestination

1. If anyone is saved, the whole credit is God's: grace went first, and even the yes was a gift. [Orange II; Trent VI cann. 1-3: **rung 1**; [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md)]
2. If anyone is lost, the whole blame is his own: God planned no one's sin, willed him saved, and gave him real grace. [no predestination to sin: Orange II's conclusion, Trent VI can. 17, **rung 1**; 1 Tim 2:4, **at least rung 3**; grace refusable, can. 4]
3. Make the two symmetrical, and either God authors both outcomes or man does, which is Pelagius. [the lesson's reduction]
4. No one reads his own name in the plan: "except by special revelation, it cannot be known whom God hath chosen". [Trent VI ch. 12, **at least rung 3**, its core **rung 1** through can. 16]

∴ [Predestination](#predestination) is God's plan to bring someone to glory, every means included, and its carrying-out is calling and glory; *how* 1 and 2 fit, why the gift wins one consent and is refused by another, the Church has never said: [before or after foreseen merits](#before-or-after-foreseen-merits), **rung 5**; the mode of efficacy, [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md). Quiercy (853), a local synod: that some perish "is the desert of those who perish" (lesson's translation).

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md)

### Argument: Calvin from free grace to a double decree

Stated in its own terms (*Institutes* III.21.5, tr. Beveridge).

1. Grace is wholly free, so salvation cannot hang on anything God foresees in us; to make predestination depend on foreknowledge is "absurd".
2. Scripture says *predestinated*, and not all are saved.

∴ The difference lies in God's decision: "some are preordained to eternal life, others to eternal damnation". Knowing one's election grounds humility and security (III.21.1); the decree concerning the fall is "dreadful" (III.23.7).

Where the Catholic texts part: predestination *to sin*, and to no grace, is excluded (Orange II; Trent VI can. 17, **rung 1**), while Valence (855) confessed a predestination of the impious *to death*, so "double predestination" is not as such the condemned formula; certainty of one's own election is excluded (ch. 12; [certainty of election](#certainty-of-election)); predestination before foreseen merits, which Aquinas also held, is **rung 5** ([reprobation](#reprobation)).

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-predestination.md)

### Argument: Molina from freedom to middle knowledge

Molina's *Concordia* (Lisbon, 1588), as 5.5 states it.

1. A free agent is one that, with everything required for acting in place, can act or not act.
2. God's providence is certain: he knows future free acts. [*Dei Filius* ch. 1, **rung 1**]
3. So that certainty cannot come from a prior determination of the will; God concurs in the act itself by [simultaneous concurrence](#simultaneous-concurrence). [1, 2]
4. Between God's natural knowledge (all that could be) and his free knowledge (what he decrees) lies [middle knowledge](#middle-knowledge): comprehending each created will, he knows what it *would* freely do in any order of circumstances, though it could do otherwise. [*Concordia* Part IV]
5. Grace is gratuitous and comes first. [Orange II; Trent VI can. 3, **rung 1**]
6. Knowing who would consent to what, God chooses which graces to give. [4, 5]

∴ The plan is infallible and the act free: a grace is efficacious by the consent God foresees (extrinsic efficacy). [Congruism](#congruism) (Bellarmine, Suárez; pressed on Jesuit teachers by the general Aquaviva in 1613, how far is disputed) adds that the efficacious grace is *congruous*, chosen because fitted to the person, a special gift before any consent. Levels: **rung 5**, uncondemned (1607); the charge against it, semi-Pelagianism, was forbidden. Molina's God does not merely watch: he gives the grace and concurs; middle knowledge is how he knows.

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)

### Argument: Banez on physical premotion

Báñez and the Dominicans (*Apologetica*, 1595), as 5.5 states them.

1. Whatever passes from not acting to acting is moved by the first cause, and the will is no exception: God applies it to its act. [the First Mover of [4.2](lessons/04-02-the-necessity-of-grace.md); [`thomistic-synthesis` 5.2](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/05-02-providence-and-secondary-causes.md)]
2. The help that brings consent is a real, efficient motion, prior in the order of causes, not of time: [physical premotion](#physical-premotion) ("physical" means real, not merely moral).
3. So efficacious grace is efficacious of itself: the will consents because grace moves it, not the reverse.
4. God moves each thing according to its nature: his will "extends not only to the doing of something by the thing which He moves, but also to its being done in a way which is fitting to the nature of that thing". [ST I-II q.10 a.4 ad 1]
5. Moved to consent, the will cannot consent and dissent at once (the composed sense), yet keeps the power to dissent (the divided sense), as a man sitting can stand, though not while sitting. [ad 3; [composed and divided sense](#composed-and-divided-sense)]

∴ Sufficient grace gives a real power, efficacious grace gives the act, and freedom is untouched ([sufficient and efficacious grace](#sufficient-and-efficacious-grace)). Levels: **rung 5**, uncondemned (1607; *Dum praeterito*, 1748); the charge against it, Calvinism, was forbidden. Each side presses the other with a rung-1 limit (5.5 Ex 2): the Molinist with God working no evil (can. 6), the Thomist with grace coming first ("who distinguisheth thee?", 1 Cor 4:7).

*Lessons:* [5.5](lessons/05-05-the-de-auxiliis-controversy.md)

### Argument: differentiated consensus and section 41

The Joint Declaration's method (1999), paraphrased.

1. A canon of Trent condemns a proposition taken in a stated sense ("If any one saith ..."), not a church or a word.
2. So two questions come apart: is the canon true (for a Catholic, settled at **rung 1**), and does this teaching fall under it? [[application of a condemnation](#application-of-a-condemnation)]
3. The sides first state a core together (§15).
4. Each then explains its own idiom: "When Lutherans say X, they do not thereby deny Y."
5. A canon condemning "X alone, to the exclusion of Y" is not met by an explanation that keeps Y.

∴ The condemnations do not apply to the teaching as this text presents it (§41), and remain "salutary warnings" (§42). Objections: a consensus only in wording (the Tübingen statement, 1998); justification demoted from *the* criterion to one (Jüngel, 1997); Trent's single inherent formal cause never named, hence the *simul*, no increase, no true merit (Malloy, 2005). Levels: §41 is no teaching act, yet the Church signed as a party; whether it is correct, **rung 5**. See [differentiated consensus](#differentiated-consensus), [joint declaration](#joint-declaration).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-1999-joint-declaration.md)

### Argument: the five propositions turn on the second

How *Cum occasione* (31 May 1653) reads as one system.

1. In the fallen state interior grace is never resisted. [prop. 2]
2. So a just man who sins lacked it, and the commandment was impossible to him. [prop. 1]
3. His unavoidable sin is still imputed, so freedom from coercion must be enough for merit and demerit. [prop. 3]
4. A resistible grace becomes the heresy, so the semi-Pelagians erred in wanting one. [prop. 4]
5. Christ's death for all, bringing grace to all, becomes semi-Pelagian. [prop. 5]

Levels: each carries its own note: 1-4 heretical (1 and 4 with further notes); 5 "false, rash, scandalous" as worded, and heretical in the sense that Christ died for the predestined only. The contradictories of 1-3, the denial of 4's charge that resistible grace is heresy, and the denial that Christ died for the predestined only, **rung 1**; rejecting 5 as worded, **at least rung 3**; that the five are in the *Augustinus* in Jansen's sense, **rung 2** ([five propositions](#five-propositions); [dogmatic fact](#dogmatic-fact)).

*Lessons:* [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)

### Argument: Pascal on the fact

The defenders' case (*Provincial Letters*, 1656-1657), and the Church's answer.

1. Either Jansen taught only efficacious grace, which the Thomists also teach, or he taught something else.
2. If only that, "he has no errors"; if something else, "he has no defenders".
3. Whether the five are in the book is a question of fact, and councils "may err in questions of fact" (Pascal quoting Bellarmine).

∴ On the fact a Catholic owes [respectful silence](#respectful-silence), not inward assent (Arnauld's party). **The answer:** a teaching office that can condemn an error but never say *where* it is cannot protect anyone from a book; Alexander VII *declaramus et definimus* the fact (1656); *Vineam Domini* (1705): respectful silence does not satisfy. Levels: the fact, **rung 2**, so denying it sets one against definitive teaching without being heresy ([droit and fait](#droit-and-fait)).

*Lessons:* [5.6](lessons/05-06-jansenism.md)

**The last things (Module 6)**

### Argument: the deferred vision and its answer

John XXII's case at its strongest (sermons from All Saints' Day 1331), as 6.2 states it:

1. Scripture ties the reward to the judgment: "Come, ye blessed ... possess you the kingdom" is said when the king judges (Matt 25:34); we shall see him "when he shall appear" (1 John 3:2).
2. The martyrs' souls wait "under the altar" (Apoc 6:9-11), read as Christ's humanity, which shelters them until they see his divinity.
3. Beatitude perfects a human being, and "the soul, without the body, is not man" (ST I-II q.4 a.5 obj. 3).
4. Augustine: the separated soul's desire to rule its body holds it back from the vision (*Literal Meaning of Genesis* XII.35).

∴ Until the judgment the just see Christ's humanity, not the divine essence: the [deferred vision](#deferred-vision).

**The answer:**

5. *Benedictus Deus* (1336) defines that the purified see the divine essence at once after death and purging, before the resumption of their bodies and the general judgment. [content **rung 1** through Florence and the CDF's 1998 commentary; its *ex cathedra* status open: [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-04-infallibility-in-exercise-and-the-ordinary-universal-magisterium.md)]
6. The soul has what suffices for happiness and wishes its body to share it, so that with the body happiness "increases not in intensity, but in extent". [I-II q.4 a.5 ad 4-5, **rung 4**]
7. The general judgment still comes, in the body: *nihilominus*. [[last judgment](#last-judgment)]

Why infallibility is untouched: a preacher's opinion on an undecided question, never defined, and submitted to the Church in [*Ne super his*](#ne-super-his) (3 December 1334); calling it heresy in 1331 overstates, since no act yet excluded it.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-heaven-and-the-beatific-vision.md)

### Argument: why a second judgment

Aquinas, ST III q.59 a.5.

1. A deed cannot be fully judged until its results are in.
2. A life goes on after death: in memory and reputation, in children, in the good or harm one's work still does, in the body, in the things one loved.
3. Yet the person's own verdict is settled at death and never reopened. [[particular judgment](#particular-judgment), **rung 1**]

∴ "A perfect and public Judgment" waits for the end of history, and God judges twice "according to different respects" (ad 2): not a retrial. Levels: both judgments, **rung 1** (*nihilominus*); Aquinas's reasons, **no magisterial level**.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-death-and-the-particular-judgment.md), [7.2](lessons/07-02-the-parousia-the-last-judgment-and-the-new-creation.md)

### Argument: from prayer for the dead

**Outline only: Boss 6(b) asks you to reconstruct it and mark each premise's source.** The materials the lesson lays out, in its order:

- the practice came first: Judas's sacrifice for the dead (2 Macc 12:43, read in [`old-testament` 6.2](../old-testament/lessons/06-02-tobit-judith-baruch-and-the-maccabees.md)), Tertullian's anniversary offerings (*De corona* 3), Jerusalem's Eucharist for those fallen asleep (*Mystagogical Catecheses* 5.9): the [law of prayer](#lex-orandi);
- what can remain after forgiveness: guilt and the eternal debt lifted, a debt of temporal punishment left (Trent VI can. 30, as [`theology-of-debt` 3.6](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-06-luther-trent-and-the-reform-of-indulgences.md) reads it; [guilt and punishment](#guilt-and-punishment));
- the dead merit nothing more (Augustine, *Enchiridion* 110), so what reaches them is the Church's help ([suffrages](#suffrages));
- the texts the tradition reads, none carrying the doctrine alone: Matt 12:32 (Gregory, *Dialogues* IV.39), Matt 5:26 (Cyprian), 1 Cor 3:11-15.

The same practice read without a purifying punishment: Peter Mogila's *Orthodox Confession* (1643) prays for the dead's pardon and denies any middle state. Levels: [purgatory](#purgatory) and the help of suffrages, **rung 1** (Florence; graded in the sibling courses); fire, place, duration, **nothing defined**.

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-purgatory.md)

### Argument: eternity from Matthew 25

Augustine, *City of God* XXI.23, as the lessons use it.

1. Matthew 25:46 says "everlasting punishment" and "life everlasting" in one sentence, one Greek adjective for both (*kolasin aiōnion*, *zōēn aiōnion*).
2. The life is endless.

∴ So is the punishment; to deny it is "excessively presumptuous". He reads the warning as a forecast ([threat and prediction](#threat-and-prediction)). Levels: the eternity is **rung 1** by *Firmiter*'s *poenam perpetuam* and the [Athanasian Creed](#athanasian-creed), not by this exegesis; a reading of Matt 25:46 is **rung 5** ([hell](#hell)).

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-hope-for-all.md)

### Argument: why the loss is eternal

ST I-II q.87 aa.3-4.

1. Mortal sin is a turning away from God and a turning toward a creature.
2. The turning away is irreparable in itself, so its punishment is eternal (a.3).
3. To the turning away answers the pain of loss, "which also is infinite, because it is the loss of the infinite good, i.e. God"; to the turning toward, the finite pain of sense (a.4).

∴ The lost lack God for ever, which is the worst of it, and suffer besides. Levels: eternity and the loss of God, **rung 1**; that the loss is the chief punishment, and a pain besides it, **at least rung 3** (CCC 1035; CDF 1979); this mapping of loss and sense, **rung 4**; what the fire is, **rung 5** ([pain of loss and pain of sense](#pain-of-loss-and-pain-of-sense)).

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md), [7.4](lessons/07-04-limbo.md)

### Argument: against annihilationism

1. [Annihilationism](#annihilationism): the wicked are finally destroyed, argued from "eternal punishment in destruction" (2 Thess 1:9, DR) (Fudge, 1982; tentatively Stott, 1988).
2. Make immortality a gift to the saved alone, and Lateran V is denied (the soul immortal, **rung 1**).
3. Let God destroy the wicked anyway, and *Firmiter* is denied: "perpetual punishment with the devil" is undergone, like *Benedictus Deus*'s *cruciantur*.

∴ Excluded on either route. *Firmiter* is the decisive text, since an annihilationist who grants natural immortality escapes Lateran V.

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md)

### Argument: Gregory of Nyssa on restoration

*On the Soul and the Resurrection* (380); *Great Catechism* 26 (385).

1. Evil is a turning from the good, with no being of its own to last.
2. God's fire is a refiner's: what he inflicts is not punishment chiefly, but the separation of good from evil.
3. God will be "all in all" (1 Cor 15:28).

∴ Evil will be annihilated "in the long revolutions of the ages", nothing left outside the world of goodness, and Christ heals "even the introducer of evil himself" ([*apokatastasis*](#apokatastasis)). Levels: the conclusion is excluded by *Firmiter*'s perpetual punishment (**rung 1**). Gregory was never named or condemned: the fifteen anathemas tied to 553 strike a restoration bound to pre-existence, which he rejected; Justinian's ninth canon of 543 fits his conclusion and has no conciliar rung ([Origenist anathemas](#origenist-anathemas)).

*Lessons:* [6.4](lessons/06-04-hell.md)

**The consummation and the open questions (Module 7)**

### Argument: the sameness objection and the replies

**Outline only: Boss 7(b) asks you to set out the eaten-body objection and give the reply through the soul.** What the lesson supplies:

- the objection's sharpest form, Athenagoras, *On the Resurrection* IV, with Augustine, *City of God* XXII.20; today's form, matter exchanged over a life ([sameness objection](#sameness-objection));
- the claim to be defended: *Firmiter*'s "with their own bodies which they now bear" (lesson's translation), **rung 1**; the identical particles not required, **rung 3** (DDF, 2023);
